Furthermore, we introduced the graphical Feynman diagram notation for the weight, where the spectral parameter written next to the propagator line specifies the weight’s exponent.

In the following subsections, we first introduce the chain relations and then map the scalar, generalized propagator weights to a lattice made of spectral lines, which satisfy a YBE and show the integrability of generalized Feynman graphs.

3.1.1
Scalar chain relation

The chain relations are beneficial auxiliary relations which, in terms of momentum-space Feynman diagrams, correspond to bubble integrals with arbitrary exponents of the propagators.
The chain relations ensure that the convolution of two weights of the form (
3.7
) with two arbitrary spectral parameters
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
yield a single weight with spectral parameter
u_{1}+u_{2}-\frac{D}{2}
times a factor depending on
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
.
It reads

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{1}{\left[x_{10}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{20}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\penalty\ =\penalty\

\displaystyle r_{0}(D-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\cdot\frac{1}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}+u_{2}-\frac{D}{2}}}\penalty\ ,

(3.8a)

\displaystyle\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ =\penalty\

\displaystyle r_{0}(D-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.8b)

In section
3.1.2
below, we derive the chain relation as a limiting case of the star-triangle relation, which in turn is proved in section
A.1
.
The constant
r_{0}
is the
\ell=0
case of

r_{\ell}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\penalty\ :=\penalty\ \pi^{\frac{D}{2}}\cdot a_{0}(u_{1})\,a_{\ell}(u_{2})\,a_{\ell}(u_{3})\penalty\ ,

(3.9)

where the
a_{\ell}(v)
were defined in (
3.5
).
This notation will be used frequently in the following.

3.1.2
Bosonic star-triangle relation

The relation that allows the identification of generalized scalar propagators (
3.7
) as solutions to a star-triangle relation is the CFT uniqueness relation.
It describes the equality
[
32
]

\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\frac{1}{\left[x_{01}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{02}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{03}^{2}\right]^{u_{3}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3}=D}}{{=}}r_{0}\left(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\right)\frac{1}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{3}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{13}^{2}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{23}^{2}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{1}}}\penalty\ ,

(3.10)

which holds if the exponents of the l. h. s. add up to the dimension of spacetime,
u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3}=D
.
We provide a detailed proof of the uniqueness relation (
3.10
) in appendix
A.1
.
In terms of Feynman diagrams, (
3.10
) is represented as

\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3}=D}}{{=}}r_{0}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\cdot\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.11)

It is a special case of a star-triangle relation (STR) and, hence, a sufficient condition for the ability to construct R-matrices satisfying a YBE.
Let us explain the conventions for Feynman diagrams, using (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) as examples.
Internal spacetime points, which are integrated over, are denoted by a filled circle, while external spacetime points are indicated by an unfilled circle.
Furthermore, the exponents of the r. h. s. of (
3.11
) add up to
\frac{D}{2}
to ensure conformality.

There is an important special case of (
3.10
), namely the limit when one of the star’s exponents approaches
\frac{D}{2}
, i. e.
u_{1}=\frac{D}{2}-\varepsilon
in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
Then, the vertical side of the triangle on the r. h. s. of (
3.11
) effectively disappears and on the l. h. s. of the remaining equation (
3.11
) the weight
W_{u_{1}}(x_{10})
divided by
r_{0}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
acts like annihilating the integral in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
Accordingly, one interprets this combination as a representation of the
D
-dimensional delta function

\delta^{(D)}\left(x_{10}\right)\penalty\ =\penalty\ \lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\penalty\ \pi^{-\frac{D}{2}}a_{0}(\varepsilon)\cdot W_{\frac{D}{2}-\varepsilon}(x_{10})\penalty\ =\penalty\ \scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},

(3.12)

which is denoted by a dotted line in a Feynman graph.

Furthermore, observe that setting
u_{1}=u
and
u_{2}=D-u-\varepsilon
in (
3.8
) and taking the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
gives the prescription of the delta-distribution (
3.12
) on the r. h. s. .
We may then write

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ \penalty\ W_{u}(x_{10})W_{D-u-\varepsilon}(x_{20})\penalty\ =\penalty\ \pi^{D}a_{0}(u)a_{0}(D-u)\cdot\delta^{(D)}(x_{12})\penalty\ ,

(3.13)

which could also be obtained from the uniqueness relation (
3.10
) by setting one of the spectral parameters to
\varepsilon
and then taking the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.

We still did not prove the chain relation (
3.8
).
As mentioned above, we can obtain it from the uniqueness relation (
3.10
) by sending one external point to infinity,
x_{i}\rightarrow\infty
.
Collecting the divergent factors of (
3.10
) yields
\left[x_{i}^{2}\right]^{-u_{i}}
on each side and canceling them leaves us with the chain relation (
3.8
).

3.1.3
Bosonic x-unity relation

The x-unity relation is the central tool in calculating the critical coupling of the integrable QFT under investigation.
Here we derive the bosonic version in detail, based on the special case of the chain relation (
3.13
) and the STR (
3.10
).
Since the chain relation is a consequence of a star-triangle relation, the x-unity relation holds whenever the weights satisfy a STR.
The converse may not be true, as we will see later.

Let us make two observations before deriving the x-unity relation.

•

Consider a three-spiked star integral, where the weights add up to
D
, similar to the l. h. s. of (
3.11
), and where we take one propagator exponent to be
\varepsilon
and then take the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
The propagator then disappears in the limit, and we obtain the integral relation (
3.13
).
This yields the relation

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\pi^{D}a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(D-u)\penalty\ \cdot\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.14)

•

We obtain another helpful relation when integrating one external point of a three-spiked star integral, in the case where the propagator weights add up to
D
.
We can then evaluate the central star integral by the STR (
3.10
) and afterwards the integral over the external point can be performed by the chain relation (
3.8
).
The remaining diagram contains two weights,
W_{\frac{D}{2}-u_{1}}(x_{23})W_{u_{1}-\frac{D}{2}}(x_{23})
, which is one.
Thus, we are only left with a factor which can be further simplified by
a_{0}(u)a_{0}(\frac{D}{2}-u)=1
.
We obtain the equality

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\pi^{D}a_{0}(u_{1})\,a_{0}(D-u_{1})\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.15)

We observe that the whole expression reduces to a factor independent of
u_{2}
.

The derivation of the x-unity relation based on (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) is

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Inversion_WWeights}}}{{=}}\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\\
\delta\rightarrow 0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\pi^{D}a_{0}(\varepsilon)a_{0}(D-\varepsilon)}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(3.16a)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:3ptFctnWithOneVanishingParameter_Scalar}}}{{=}}\left[\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{a_{0}(u)a_{0}(D-u)}{a_{0}(\varepsilon)a_{0}(D-\varepsilon)}\right]\cdot\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(3.16b)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:3ptFctnOnePointIntegrated_Scalar}}}{{=}}\left[\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\\
\delta\rightarrow 0\end{subarray}}\pi^{D}a_{0}(u)a_{0}(D-u)\frac{a_{0}(\delta)a_{0}(D-\delta)}{a_{0}(\varepsilon)a_{0}(D-\varepsilon)}\right]\cdot\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(3.16c)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phantom{\eqref{eq:3ptFctnOnePointIntegrated_Scalar}}}}{{=}}\pi^{D}a_{0}(u)a_{0}(D-u)\hskip 14.22636pt\cdot\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.16d)

To summarize, the x-unity relation allows to replace a Feynman integral with four external points and a particular choice of propagator exponents by a single delta function.
It is the unity integration kernel, therefore the name x-unity.
For later reference, the scalar x-unity is finally

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \pi^{D}a_{0}(u)a_{0}(D-u)\hskip 14.22636pt\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(3.17)

and we will also use the version of (
3.17
) where the left and right external points are interchanged.

3.1.4
Integrable scalar Feynman graphs

So far, this section presented integral identities for Feynman graphs with generalized propagators, which are inspired from a non-local deformation of the kinetic term in some action.
Now, we present how they encode YBEs and what R-matrices solve them.
Next, we will explain how these propagators are related to the YBE, as well as to the R-matrices solving the latter.

The medial lattice

Let us get back to the spectral lines introduced in section
2.1
.
The generalized propagator (
3.7
) may be associated to an intersection of two spectral lines in two ways,

W_{u}(x_{12})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\bar{W}_{u}(x_{12})=W_{\frac{D}{2}-u}(x_{12})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.18)

The exponent of the propagator is related to the difference of the spectral parameters, carried by each of the two spectral lines,
u=p-q
.
There are two options on how the propagator is related to the intersecting spectral lines.
If both spectral lines point to the same side of the propagator, we assign the weight
W_{u}(x_{12})
.
If the spectral lines point to different sides of the propagator, this indicates the assignment of the so-called crossed weight
\bar{W}_{u}(x_{12})=W_{\frac{D}{2}-u}(x_{12})
.
The term
crossing
refers to the flipping of one spectral line involved in the intersection and we see that
W_{u}(x_{12})
and
\bar{W}_{u}(x_{12})
differ by such a transformation.
It effectively exchanges the spectral parameter difference
u
by
\frac{D}{2}-u
, which is an example of the general crossing transformation of a spectral parameter
u\rightarrow\eta-u
with the crossing parameter
\eta
.
Thus, we can identify the crossing parameter in integrable, generalized Feynman graphs as

\eta=\frac{D}{2}

(3.19)

We denote the crossed spectral parameter by
\bar{u}=\frac{D}{2}-u
, which implies
\bar{\bar{u}}=u
.

In (
3.18
), the angle to the horizontal line is indicated as
\frac{2\pi}{D}p
and
\frac{2\pi}{D}q
for the spectral line carrying the spectral parameters
p
and
q
, respectively.
The spectral parameter can be thought of as proportional to the slope of the corresponding spectral line.
The proportionality factor is the ratio of the two crossing factors,
\pi
for the geometric angle picture and
\frac{D}{2}
for the generalized Feynman graphs.
Thus, the exponent of the propagator weights (
3.18
) is proportional to the angle under which the two spectral lines intersect.
The geometric interpretation of the spectral parameters as angles is very natural in 2-dimensional integrable scattering, where the spectral lines correspond to trajectories and their intersections to the scattering described by the S-matrix satisfying the YBE
[
144
]
.
However, for illustrative reasons, we will not draw the medial lines with the slope corresponding to their spectral parameter.

Another important graphical feature of (
3.18
) is the shading of those faces of the graph that do not contain an external point the propagator is attached to
[
31
,
30
]
.
It hints towards the general relation between a planar generalized Feynman graph
\mathscr{F}
and the graph spanned by multiple spectral lines
\mathscr{G}
.
The latter is assumed to be a planar graph, which implies that the spectral lines can be imagined to live on a two-dimensional surface.
This is instrumental, since integrability in QFTs appears in the context of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM in the planar limit, where its leading-order Feynman diagrams often have a toroidal topology.
The map (
3.18
) tells us to associate with every edge in
\mathscr{F}
a vertex in
\mathscr{G}
, to every vertex in
\mathscr{F}
a face in
\mathscr{G}
, and with every face in
\mathscr{F}
a face in
\mathscr{G}
, as well.
Thus, some of the faces of
\mathscr{G}
correspond to a vertex of
\mathscr{F}
and others correspond to a face in
\mathscr{F}
.
The latter faces are exactly the ones we will shade, when drawing the graph
\mathscr{G}
corresponding to
\mathscr{F}
.
Note that the two sets of faces in
\mathscr{G}
are alternating, this means that two faces from the same set touch each other at most at one single vertex, while two faces, which are not in the same set, touch each other along an edge in
\mathscr{G}
.
Hence, two shadings are possible for a graph
\mathscr{G}
.

A graph
\mathscr{G}
that is constructed from
\mathscr{F}
in such a way is known as the
medial
graph of
\mathscr{F}
and we denote it
\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{M}(\mathscr{F})
.
In the following, we will also use the term
medial lines
for the spectral lines drawn in blue, c. f. (
3.18
).
The medial map
\mathscr{M}
is two-to-one, since the dual
1
1

1

Remember that the dual graph is obtained by replacing each vertex of the original planar graph by a face and each face by a vertex, while the dual graph’s edges correspond to a pair of faces separated by an edge in the original graph.
graph of
\mathscr{F}
,
\mathscr{F}^{*}
, admits the same medial graph,
\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{M}(\mathscr{F})=\mathscr{M}(\mathscr{F}^{*})
.
If we equip the medial map with a shading, we obtain an one-to-one map.
Our convention is to shade the faces of
\mathscr{G}
, which correspond to the faces in
\mathscr{F}
, so that we can draw external and internal vertices of
\mathscr{F}
into unshaded faces of
\mathscr{G}
.
We denote the shaded medial graph of
\mathscr{F}
as
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}
.
If we invert the shading, we obtain
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}^{*}}
.

In summary, the shaded medial map is a one-to-one relation of a planar Feynman graph
\mathscr{F}
to a shaded medial graph
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}
.
If we take the exponents of the propagators
\left\{u_{i}\right\}
and the spectral parameters of the medial lines
\left\{p_{i}\right\}
into account we have the invertible, one-to-one map
[
31
,
30
]

\mathscr{F}(\left\{u_{i}\right\})\longleftrightarrow\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}(\left\{p_{i}\right\})\penalty\ ,

(3.20)

where the exponents are differences of various spectral parameters,
u_{i}=p_{j}-p_{k}
.
In the context of integrable QFTs,
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}(\left\{p_{i}\right\})
is sometimes called
Baxter lattice
or
loom

[
110
,
145
]
.
A minimal illustration of these concepts is (
3.18
): the equation on the left shows the Feynman graph
\mathscr{F}(u)
in black consisting of a single propagator with two external vertices connected by one edge and exponent
u=p-q
.
The corresponding shaded medial graph is
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}(p,q)
in blue on the r. h. s. of the left equation in (
3.18
).
The equation on the right therein shows the dual Feynman graph in black and the corresponding inversely shaded medial graph in blue.

The power of the map (
3.20
) manifests itself when applying it on the STR (
3.11
) by using (
3.18
),

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1}=\frac{D}{2}-(p_{2}-p_{3})\\
u_{2}=p_{1}-p_{3}\\
u_{3}=\frac{D}{2}-(p_{1}-p_{2})\end{subarray}}}{{=}}r_{0}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(3.21)

from which we gain multiple insights.
First, we observe that the constraint
u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3}=D
is automatically satisfied when we understand the exponents coming from the spectral parameters of the medial lines.
This implies that a generalized Feynman graph, which is constructed from its shaded medial graph, has conformal internal vertex integrations by construction, stemming from a CFT.
Furthermore, (
3.21
) illustrates that closed faces in
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}
correspond to internal, integrated vertices in the generalized Feynman graph.
Additionally, only planar Feynman graphs can be generated from a shaded medial graph.
In addition, inverting the shading just switches the r. h. s. and l. h. s. of the equation.

Secondly, equation (
3.21
) crucially shows that the uniqueness relation (
3.10
) on the Feynman graph
\mathscr{F}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
hides a YBE under the map (
3.20
) on the level of the shaded medial graph
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
!
Therefore, the shaded medial graph uncovers the integrable structure of generalized Feynman graphs.
In contrast to the usual form of the YBE, (
3.21
) involves the factor
r_{0}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
, which should not be forgotten in concrete calculations
2
2

2

In fact, the factor
r_{0}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
is absent if we use redefined weights in (
3.18
).
Therein, the left diagram should correspond to
\Gamma(u)W_{u}(x_{12})
and the diagram on the right to
\Gamma(\frac{D}{2}-u)\bar{W}_{u}(x_{12})
. The
\pi^{\frac{D}{2}}
can be associated to a rescaled integration measure.
.
Yet, (
3.21
) allows us to transfer a plethora of methods and techniques developed for integrable models, using the central equation of integrability, the YBE, to the medial graph.
Thus, by the map (
3.20
), and practically by (
3.18
), we can study the implications of integrability on generalized Feynman graphs.
In a next step, one can fix the spectral parameters to specific values where the propagators (
3.7
) describe physical propagators of the theory under investigation.

As a first application of this strategy, we can ask for the form of unitarity as a generalized Feynman graph.
In short, unitarity refers to the fact that two spectral lines, which intersect each other twice, can be untangled.
After translating the medial picture back to the Feynman graph, depending on the shading, we find two relations,

\displaystyle=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(3.22a)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u=\frac{D}{2}-(p_{1}-p_{2})}}{{=}}\pi^{D}a_{0}(u)a_{0}(D-u)\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.22b)

Regarding (
3.22a
), we observe that unitarity translates to the trivial fact
W_{u}(x_{12})W_{-u}(x_{12})=1
on the generalized Feynman graph, with
u=p_{1}-p_{2}
.
When we invert the shading, we find the Feynman graphs corresponding to (
3.13
) and therefore we have to include the factor
\pi^{D}a_{0}(u)a_{0}(D-u)
in (
3.22b
).
In this case we have
u=\frac{D}{2}-(p_{1}-p_{2})
.

The R-matrices

In (
3.21
) we saw that a YBE appears graphically when we translate the STR (
3.11
) to the shaded medial lattice.
By going a step back and forgetting the shading for the moment, we present the corresponding algebraic YBE and the R-matrix solving it.
However, the shading procedure allows us to study single Feynman graphs, and hence reinstalling it leads us to a fused R-matrix, which satisfies a staggered YBE.
The fusion leads to objects, which depend on staggered medial lines.

Disregarding the shading in
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}
yields the medial graph
\mathscr{G}
.
This has the drawback that one deals with the Feynman graph
\mathscr{F}
and its dual
\mathscr{F}^{*}
at the same time.
Therefore, on the level of the weights (
3.18
), we have to combine the weight and its crossed counterpart into a single object.
This is the R-matrix

R^{x_{2}y_{2}}_{x_{1}y_{1}}(u)=W_{u}(y_{12})\bar{W}_{u}(x_{12})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u=p_{1}-p_{2}}}{{=}}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(3.23)

where the weight
W_{u}(y_{12})
corresponds to the horizontal propagator representing
\mathscr{F}
, and
\bar{W}_{u}(x_{12})
to the vertical one representing
\mathscr{F}^{*}
.
Within the R-matrix
R^{x_{2}y_{2}}_{x_{1}y_{1}}(u)
the two weights do not interfere with each other, giving the factorized form in the spacetime coordinates
x_{i}
,
y_{i}
.
The YBE is obtained when we remove the shading of (
3.21
) and draw the dual graph as well,

\scalebox{1.1}[1.1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \scalebox{1.1}[1.1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}.

(3.24)

On the level of the individual Feynman graphs
\mathscr{F}
(red) and
\mathscr{F}^{*}
(green), the STR (
3.11
) yields (
3.24
).
While the red graph represents (
3.11
) in the displayed order, the green graph represents (
3.11
) with left- and right hand side interchanged.
In contrast to (
3.11
), we notice the absence of the factor
r_{0}
.
It appears in the relations of both graphs
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{*}
and cancels.
Informally, one could write
\mathrm{YBE}=(\mathrm{STR})^{2}
to describe the relationship between YBE and STR.
In terms of the R-matrix (
3.23
), the YBE (
4.1
) reads

\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ R^{x_{2}y_{3}}_{x_{0}y_{1}}(p_{12})R^{y_{1}x_{0}}_{y_{2}x_{3}}(p_{13})R^{x_{0}y_{3}}_{x_{1}y_{2}}(p_{23})=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}y_{0}\penalty\ R^{y_{0}x_{1}}_{y_{2}x_{3}}(p_{12})R^{x_{2}y_{3}}_{x_{1}y_{0}}(p_{13})R^{y_{1}x_{2}}_{y_{0}x_{3}}(p_{23}),

(3.25)

where we write for short
p_{ij}=p_{i}-p_{j}
.
The equation (
3.25
) is known as a facetype-YBE, which can be brought to the usual vertex-type by intertwining vectors
[
146
]
.
Moreover, unitarity of the R-matrix (
3.23
) is a combination of the relation (
3.22a
) and (
3.22b
).

The R-matrix (
3.23
) is less useful for QFT applications due to the construction from the medial graph
\mathscr{G}
, since practically one is interested in a single Feynman graph instead of two,
\mathscr{F}
and its dual
\mathscr{F}^{*}
.
Therefore, the R-matrix should be constructed from a shaded medial graph
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}
.
The way to go is through a procedure called
fusion
.
Graphically, it amounts to considering the intersection of a pair of medial lines with another pair (we may consider such a pair as a kind of bound-state of two elementary, partonic excitations).
We call the fused R-matrix
\mathbb{R}
.
Of course, a two-times-two intersection can be decomposed into four one-times-one intersections of medial lines of type (
3.18
), but the main advantage of
\mathbb{R}
is that it can be composed, while respecting the shading.
Hence, if we consider the shaded intersection of two pairs of medial lines and if we use the weights (
3.18
) for the one-times-one intersections, we obtain

\mathbb{R}^{x_{2}y_{2}}_{x_{1}y_{1}}(p_{1},p_{2}|p_{3},p_{4})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.26)

Note that the R-matrix depends on three free parameters, since the four exponents satisfy the constraint
p_{31}+\bar{p}_{41}+\bar{p}_{32}+p_{42}=D
.
The parameterization in terms of the three free parameters
\Delta_{1}
,
\Delta_{2}
and
u
is taken from the original derivation
[
96
]
and we use the notation
\mathbb{R}^{x_{2}y_{2}}_{x_{1}y_{1}}(u;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})=\mathbb{R}^{x_{2}y_{2}}_{x_{1}y_{1}}(p_{1},p_{2}|p_{3},p_{4})
.
The scaling dimensions
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
are the labels of the two representations of the scalar principal series
3
3

3

The scalar principle series representation of the conformal group in
D
dimensions is acting on the vector space of square-integrable functions

V_{\Delta}=\mathrm{L}^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{D},(1+x^{2})^{2\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)-D}\mathrm{d}^{D}x\right)

and the second argument is the integral measure.
The representation can be generalized to account for spinning fermionic propagators as well.
Accordingly, the representation acts on the tensor product of
V_{\Delta}
with the complex vector space of symmetric traceless tensors of rank
\ell
.
We denote it by
V_{\Delta,\ell,\bar{\ell}}
and the R-matrix (
3.26
) is an element of
\mathrm{End}(V_{\Delta,\ell,\bar{\ell}}\otimes V_{\Delta,\ell,\bar{\ell}})

[
147
,
148
]
.

, which are associated with the pairs of medial lines.
The R-matrix
\mathbb{R}
describes the intersection of these two representations.
For the QFT applications in section
5
, the R-matrix (
3.26
) is of high importance, since tuning the spectral parameter and conformal dimensions to suited values yields parts of physical Feynman diagrams, which build up the entire Feynman graph.
Therefore, these evaluated R-matrices are called graph builders, see e. g.
[
82
]
.

There exists an interesting limit of the R-matrix (
3.26
), namely
u=\varepsilon
and
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}
.
When we multiply the R-matrix with a compensation factor and eventually take the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, we get

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\pi^{-D}a_{0}(\varepsilon)^{2}\cdot\mathbb{R}^{x_{2}y_{2}}_{x_{1}y_{1}}(\varepsilon;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{1})\penalty\ =\penalty\ \scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \mathbb{P}_{xy}\penalty\ .

(3.27)

In this limit, the spectral parameters of the horizontal medial lines are the same as the spectral parameter of the vertical ones.
Hence, we see that for a vanishing spectral parameter, the R-matrix becomes the permutation operator
\mathbb{P}_{xy}
, which interchanges the two vector spaces it is acting on.
Here, they are labeled by spacetime points
x
and
y
.

Naturally, the R-matrix (
3.26
) solves a YBE, which we call
\mathbb{YBE}
to distinguish it from (
4.1
).
The
\mathbb{YBE}
, graphically displayed as the generalized Feynman graph
\mathscr{F}
together with the shaded medial graph
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}
, is (the exponents of the weights are suppressed for clarity)

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.28)

Contrary to the Feynman graph, proving it up to a factor is straightforward on the corresponding graph
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}
: starting from the l. h. s. , the STR (
3.21
) allows to shift the medial lines
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
to the very right side and yields the r. h. s. .
However, it is left to show that all the factors
r_{0}
, (
3.9
), which are produced or annihilated by the STR, cancel.
We demonstrate the complete proof of (
3.28
) at the level of the generalized Feynman diagram
\mathscr{F}
.
However, a priori it is not clear where and in which direction the STR (
3.11
) should be used.
That is why the medial picture and the map (
3.20
) are so helpful.
Having figured out, which medial line gets pushed through which medial vertex in
\mathscr{G}_{\mathscr{F}}
, shows us which star (if exponents add up to
D
) should be turned into a triangle and which triangle (if exponents add up to
\frac{D}{2}
) should be turned into a star within
\mathscr{F}
.
The diagram part of the proof is performed in multiple steps by usage of (
3.11
) in both ways,

\begin{gathered}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 3\times\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 3\times\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\\
\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .\end{gathered}

(3.29)

Along the steps, we pick up multiple
r_{0}
factors, according to (
3.11
).
After the final step, we have to multiply the last diagram in (
3.29
) by

\frac{r_{0}(\bar{p}_{35},\bar{p}_{13},p_{15})\;r_{0}(\bar{p}_{45},\bar{p}_{24},p_{25})\;r_{0}(\bar{p}_{46},\bar{p}_{14},p_{16})\cdot r_{0}(\bar{p}_{36},\bar{p}_{23},p_{26})}{r_{0}(\bar{p}_{45},\bar{p}_{14},p_{15})\cdot r_{0}(\bar{p}_{35},\bar{p}_{23},p_{25})\;r_{0}(\bar{p}_{36},\bar{p}_{13},p_{16})\;r_{0}(\bar{p}_{46},\bar{p}_{24},p_{26})}=1

(3.30)

Remember that the factor
r_{0}
(defined in (
3.9
)) factorizes in
a_{0}
factors (defined in (
3.5
)) of their arguments.
And since the factors in (
3.30
) in the numerator have the same arguments as the ones in the denominator, we obtain one.
This proves the
\mathbb{YBE}
(
3.28
).

Naturally, the R-matrix (
3.26
) satisfies a unitarity relation,

\scalebox{0.75}[0.75]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \pi^{2D}a_{0}(\bar{p}_{13})\,a_{0}(D-\bar{p}_{13})a_{0}(\bar{p}_{24})\,a_{0}(D-\bar{p}_{24})\cdot\scalebox{0.75}[0.75]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(3.31)

which is a consequence of the unitarity of the weights (
3.22
).

3.2
The generalized fermionic propagator as a lattice weight

The propagator of spin-
\frac{1}{2}
Weyl fermions
\psi_{\alpha}
has, analogously to the scalar case in section
3.1
, a generalization based on a non-local deformation of the kinetic term.
Accordingly, its derivation is similar to the scalar case.
In Euclidean space, the deformed kinetic term reads

-\mathrm{i}\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\penalty\ \bar{\psi}\square^{v}\bar{\partial}\psi=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{1}\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{2}\penalty\ \bar{\psi}_{\dot{\alpha}}(x_{1})\left[-\mathrm{i}\delta^{(D)}(x_{12})\,\bar{\sigma}^{\mu,\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\square_{2}^{v}\partial_{2,\mu}\right]\psi_{\alpha}(x_{2})\penalty\ ,

(3.32)

where we use the notations
x_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=x_{\mu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}
and
\bar{x}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}=x_{\mu}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu,\dot{\alpha}\alpha}
.
Here, we use the standard sigma matrices
\sigma^{\mu}
and
\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}
, appropriate for even D-dimensional spacetime.
The odd
D
-dimensional case should be described by using gamma matrices
\gamma^{\mu}
instead.
Importantly, the matrices satisfy a Clifford algebra and their properties for
D=3
and
D=4
are shown in appendix
D
.
In this section, we use the symbol of sigma matrices for a representation of the
D
dimensional Clifford algebra, knowing that in spacetime dimensions other than four, the actual matrix representation has to be adjusted.
However, all our findings carry over to other spacetime dimensions as well; see e. g.
[
149
]
.
The propagator
G_{v,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(x_{12})=\langle\psi_{\alpha}(x_{1})\bar{\psi}_{\dot{\beta}}(x_{2})\rangle
as the inverse of the kinetic operator satisfies

\left[-\mathrm{i}\,\bar{\sigma}^{\mu,\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\square^{v}\partial_{\mu}\right]G_{v,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(x)=\delta^{(D)}(x)\cdot\delta^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\dot{\beta}}\penalty\ .

(3.33)

As in the scalar case, we can solve this equation via Fourier transform and integration by parts, which gives the relation
\bar{\sigma}^{\mu,\dot{\alpha}\alpha}p_{\mu}\tilde{G}_{v,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(p)=-(p^{2})^{-v}\delta^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\dot{\beta}}
, where
\tilde{G}_{v,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(p)
is the Fourier transform of
G_{v,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(x)
.
We can solve the relation for
\tilde{G}_{v,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(p)
by use of the Clifford algebra of the sigma matrices, see .
Then, we find
\tilde{G}_{v,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(p)=-\sigma^{\nu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}\,p_{\nu}(p^{2})^{-v-1}
and we can reverse the Fourier transform.
Eventually, we obtain

G_{v,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(x)=-\mathrm{i}\,\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}\partial_{\mu}G_{v+1}(x)=c_{\ell}(v+\tfrac{1}{2})\cdot\frac{x_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}}{\left[x^{2}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-v}}

(3.34)

where
G_{v+1}(x)
is the scalar generalized propagator from (
3.4
) and
c_{\ell}(v)
is the factor defined in (
3.6
).
Note that we obtain the conventional local propagator in (
3.34
), if we set
v\rightarrow 0
.
Its scaling dimension is then the conventional
1-D
, which is twice the scaling dimension for fermions in
D
dimensions,
\frac{1-D}{2}
.
We define the corresponding fermionic lattice weight by neglecting the factor in
(
3.34
), which is not spacetime dependent,

W_{u,\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(x_{10})=\frac{1}{\left(x_{10}^{2}\right)^{u}}\frac{x_{10,\alpha\dot{\beta}}}{\left|x_{10}\right|}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.35)

In
W_{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}(x_{10})
, we suppress the spinor indices.
Furthermore, the notation stems from the fact that we can combine the scalar weight (
3.7
) and the fermionic one (
3.35
) into a single object

W_{u,\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{\ell}(x_{10})=\frac{1}{\left(x_{10}^{2}\right)^{u}}\left[\frac{x_{10,\alpha\dot{\beta}}}{\left|x_{10}\right|}\right]^{2\ell}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},\penalty\ \ell=0\\
\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},\penalty\ \ell=\frac{1}{2}\end{array}\right..

(3.36)

Note that higher spinning generalized propagators were studied in
[
85
,
86
,
87
]
.
Switching to Lorentz indices, we can represent them as

W_{u}^{\ell,\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2\ell}}(x_{10})=\frac{1}{\left(x_{10}^{2}\right)^{u}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{2\ell}\frac{x_{10}^{\mu_{i}}}{\left|x_{10}\right|}\right]=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}.

(3.37)

Here, we focus on
\ell=0
and
\ell=\frac{1}{2}
.
Furthermore, we introduce a graphical way to represent a conjugated weight, which is contracted with the Pauli matrix
\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}
,

W_{u}^{\ell,\dot{\alpha}\beta}(x_{10})=\frac{1}{\left(x_{10}^{2}\right)^{u}}\left[\frac{\bar{x}_{10}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}}{\left|x_{10}\right|}\right]^{2\ell}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},\penalty\ \ell=0\\
\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},\penalty\ \ell=\frac{1}{2}\end{array}\right..

(3.38)

Note that the conjugated weight is identical to the ordinary one, when we use gamma matrices in odd dimensional spacetime.
Furthermore, it is antisymmetric in
x_{0}\leftrightarrow x_{1}
for
\ell=\frac{1}{2}
and we have to give the graphical representation of the generalized propagator a direction by adding a little arrow at the end of the propagator.

With these new lattice weights, we repeat now the derivation of the STR, unitarity, chain rule, and x-unity and show how to construct a fermionic R-matrix that satisfies a YBE.
The situation is almost identical to the scalar case; however, we have to take into account the spin.
Concerning the map (
3.20
), there is a new feature.
In the fermionic case, the medial lines carry a spin label
\ell
, as well as a spectral parameter
p
.
We collectively denote the parameter of a medial line by
\mathbf{p}=(p,\ell)
.
The map between generalized Feynman graph and shaded medial graph is given by the graphical representation of the weight (
3.35
) and its crossed counterpart,

\begin{array}[]{cc}W_{u,\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{\ell}(x_{12})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},&\bar{W}_{u}^{\ell,\dot{\alpha}\beta}(x_{12})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
W_{u}^{\ell,\dot{\alpha}\beta}(x_{12})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},&\bar{W}_{u,\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{\ell}(x_{12})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},\end{array}

(3.39)

depending if the fermionic line is to the right or the left, respectively.
Again, we denote the crossed weights by
\bar{W}_{u,\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{\ell}(x_{12})=W_{\frac{D}{2}-u,\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{\ell}(x_{12})
and
\bar{W}_{u}^{\ell,\dot{\alpha}\beta}(x_{12})=W_{\frac{D}{2}-u}^{\ell,\dot{\alpha}\beta}(x_{12})
.

In appendix
A.2
, we show that the fermionic weights (
3.39
) satisfy a fermionic version of the STR
[
33
]
,

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ r_{\ell}(u_{3},u_{1},u_{2})\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(3.40)

which has the bosonic STR (
3.21
) as a special case for
\ell=0
.
For
u_{3}\rightarrow\frac{D}{2}
, we obtain the corresponding generalizations of the unitarity relations

\displaystyle=\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{(\ell)}\penalty\ ,

(3.41a)

\displaystyle=\pi^{D}a_{\ell}(u)a_{\ell}(D-u)\cdot\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{(\ell)}\penalty\ .

(3.41b)

Note that we used the property of Clifford algebras, which is here
\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu}+\sigma^{\nu}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}=-2g^{\mu\nu}\mathbb{I}
with
\mathbb{I}
either
\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}
or
\delta_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\dot{\beta}}
.
Hence, the unit matrix
\mathbb{I}^{(\ell)}
is the one appropriate to the spin structure on the right-hand side of the
D
-dimensional Clifford algebra: One has
\mathbb{I}_{2^{D/2-1}}
for even
D
and
\mathbb{I}_{2^{(D-1)/2}}
for odd
D
if
\ell=\frac{1}{2}
.
In the scalar case
\ell=0
, the unit matrix becomes a scalar factor,
\mathbb{I}^{(0)}=1
.
The relation (
3.41a
) is sometimes called
merging relation

[
150
]
, since it allows to merge propagators, which start and end at the same spacetime point.
While the merging leads to an annihilation in (
3.41a
), there is also the constructive case
W_{u_{1}}^{\ell,\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2\ell}}(x_{12})\cdot W_{u_{2}}^{0}(x_{12})=W_{u_{1}+u_{2}}^{\ell,\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2\ell}}(x_{12})
, which follows directly from the definition of the weights (
3.37
).

Furthermore, we can consider the limits
x_{3}\rightarrow\infty
and
x_{2}\rightarrow\infty
(or equivalently
x_{1}\rightarrow\infty
).
This way we find the chain relations

\displaystyle=r_{\ell}(D-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{(\ell)}\penalty\ ,

(3.42a)

\displaystyle=r_{\ell}(u_{1},u_{2},D-u_{1}-u_{2})\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(3.42b)

respectively.
The fermionic chain relation (
3.42b
) also holds for
\ell>\frac{1}{2}
and the higher spin weights (
3.37
).
However, the Lorentz indices have to be contracted with a symmetric traceless tensor
4
4

4

The higher-spin chain relation (
3.42b
) can be shown by using the scalar chain relation (
3.8
) and the useful representation of the higher-spin weight (
3.37
) by derivatives acting on a scalar one (
3.7
),

C_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2\ell}}W_{u}^{\ell,\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2\ell}}(x_{12})=\frac{1}{(-2)^{2\ell}}\frac{\Gamma(u-\ell)}{\Gamma(u+\ell)}C_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2\ell}}\partial_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots\partial_{1}^{\mu_{2\ell}}W_{u-\ell}(x_{12})\penalty\ .

(3.43)

Here,
C_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2\ell}}
has to be a traceless symmetric tensor
[
148
]
.
.

Next, we will generalize the scalar x-unity relation (
3.17
) to include fermionic generalized propagators.
Again, we have two auxiliary relations, which are the generalizations of (
3.14
) and (
3.15
).
They read

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ChainRuleFermionFermion}}}{{=}}\pi^{D}a_{\ell}(u)\,a_{\ell}(D-u)\penalty\ \cdot\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{(\ell)}\penalty\ ,

(3.44a)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:GenFermionicPropagatorWWeight_medial}}}{{=}}\pi^{D}a_{\ell}(u_{1})\,a_{\ell}(D-u_{1})\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{(\ell)}\penalty\ .

(3.44b)

Then, we derive the x-unity relation from (
3.44a
) and (
3.44b
) as

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Inversion_WWeights}}}{{=}}\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\\
\delta\rightarrow 0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\pi^{D}a_{0}(\varepsilon)a_{0}(D-\varepsilon)}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(3.45a)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:3ptFctnWithOneVanishingParameter_Fermion}}}{{=}}\left[\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{a_{\ell}(u)a_{\ell}(D-u)}{a_{0}(\varepsilon)a_{0}(D-\varepsilon)}\right]\cdot\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{(\ell)}

(3.45b)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:3ptFctnOnePointIntegrated_Fermion}}}{{=}}\left[\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\\
\delta\rightarrow 0\end{subarray}}\pi^{D}a_{\ell}(u)a_{\ell}(D-u)\frac{a_{0}(\delta)a_{0}(D-\delta)}{a_{0}(\varepsilon)a_{0}(D-\varepsilon)}\right]\cdot\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{(m)}\mathbb{I}^{(\ell)}

(3.45c)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phantom{\eqref{eq:3ptFctnOnePointIntegrated_Fermion}}}}{{=}}\pi^{D}a_{\ell}(u)a_{\ell}(D-u)\hskip 14.22636pt\cdot\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{(m)}\mathbb{I}^{(\ell)}\penalty\ .

(3.45d)

In summary, the fermionic x-unity relation allows, as in the scalar case, to annihilate a four-point integral into a single delta function.
Compactly, we write

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \pi^{D}a_{\ell}(u)a_{\ell}(D-u)\hskip 14.22636pt\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{(m)}\mathbb{I}^{(\ell)}\penalty\ .

(3.46)

Lastly, we construct the generalization of the fused R-matrix (
3.26
).
Therefore, we consider the intersection of a bosonic pair of medial lines with another pair subject to the fermionic generalization.
By (
3.39
), we find

\mathbb{R}^{x_{2}y_{2}}_{x_{1}y_{1}}(p_{1},p_{2}|\mathbf{p}_{3},\mathbf{p}_{4})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.47)

The fermionic R-matrix was derived in
[
96
]
, we can adapt their parametrization of the exponents by writing
\mathbb{R}^{x_{2}y_{2}}_{x_{1}y_{1}\,m\ell}(u;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})=\mathbb{R}^{x_{2}y_{2}}_{x_{1}y_{1}}(p_{1},p_{2}|\mathbf{p}_{3},\mathbf{p}_{4})
, and it first used in the QFT context of the dynamical fishnet theory in
[
76
]
.
Note that in the case
\ell=m=0
, we obtain the all-scalar R-matrix (
3.26
).
The generalized fermionic R-matrix solves a YBE, similar to (
3.28
),

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.48)

Its proof works analogously to the scalar case (
3.29
), instead of the scalar STR we use the generalized fermionic one (
3.40
).
Also, the composite factor made up from the usage of all the individual STRs cancels to one.
It is the same as (
3.30
) after replacing
r_{0}
by
r_{\ell}
or
r_{m}
.
Finally, as in the scalar case (
3.31
), the R-matrix satisfies unitarity relations, where the generalized propagators are either fermionic or scalar.

3.3
The generalized superspace propagator as a lattice weight

3.3.1
The generalized superpropagator

Similar to the starting points of sections
3.1
and
3.2
, the generalized superpropagator is motivated from the non-local deformation of the kinetic term for chiral superfields, which is called the Kähler potential.
We restrict ourselves to the canonical one, see e. g.
[
151
,
152
,
153
]
, which is

\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\penalty\ \Phi^{\dagger}\square^{v}\Phi=\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\left[\phi^{\dagger}\square^{1+v}\phi-\mathrm{i}\bar{\psi}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\square^{v}\partial_{\mu}\psi+F^{\dagger}\square^{v}F\right]\penalty\ ,

(3.49)

with
\square=\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}
.
The fermionic coordinates
\theta^{\alpha}
and
\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}
are complex conjugated, spinor-valued Graßmann numbers that anti-commute,
\{\theta^{\alpha},\theta^{\beta}\}=0
.
In our applications, the chiral superfields and their components eventually are matrix-valued fields in the adjoint representation of a gauge group, at this point of the derivation, however, we treat them like scalars.
With the notation of the
†
we prepare for the use of derivation to superfields in the adjoint, yet a
∗
would be more precise here.
Furthermore, we showcase the derivation of the generalized superpropagator in four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace, but the derivation for three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
superspace is very similar.
It however differs in some signs due to the spinor conventions, e. g. see appendix
D.2
.
The chiral superfields are defined by the constraints
\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\Phi=0
and
D_{\alpha}\Phi^{\dagger}=0
.
Consequently, in four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace, they admit an superspace expansion

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=\phi(x_{+})+\sqrt{2}\;\theta\psi(x_{+})+\theta^{2}F(x_{+})=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\partial_{\mu}}\left[\phi(x)+\sqrt{2}\;\theta\psi(x)+\theta^{2}F(x)\right]

(3.50a)

\displaystyle=\phi(x)+\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\partial_{\mu}\phi(x)+\frac{1}{4}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\square\phi(x)+\sqrt{2}\;\theta\psi(x)-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\theta^{2}\partial_{\mu}\psi(x)\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}+\theta^{2}F(x)

(3.50b)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\phi^{\dagger}(x_{-})+\sqrt{2}\;\bar{\theta}\bar{\psi}(x_{-})+\bar{\theta}^{2}F^{\dagger}(x_{-})=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\partial_{\mu}}\left[\phi^{\dagger}(x)+\sqrt{2}\;\bar{\theta}\bar{\psi}(x)+\bar{\theta}^{2}F^{\dagger}(x)\right]

(3.50c)

\displaystyle=\phi^{\dagger}(x)-\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{\dagger}(x)+\frac{1}{4}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\square\phi^{\dagger}(x)-\sqrt{2}\;\bar{\theta}\bar{\psi}(x)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\psi}(x)+\bar{\theta}^{2}F^{\dagger}(x)

(3.50d)

with
x^{\mu}_{\pm}=x^{\mu}\pm\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}
.
The expansions (
3.50
) lead to the r. h. s. of the kinetic component action (
3.49
).
Here, the
\phi
denotes a complex scalar field, the
\psi
a Weyl fermion and
F
is a non-propagating auxiliary field.
In appendix
D.1
and
D.2
, the suppression of spinor indices is explained in detail.
The generalized superfield propagator can be deduced from the individual generalized propagators of the component fields.
In fact, using (
3.50
), we find

\begin{split}\left\langle\Phi(z_{1})\Phi^{\dagger}(z_{2})\right\rangle=&\penalty\ \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{1}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{1}\partial_{1,\mu}-\mathrm{i}\theta_{2}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{2}\partial_{2,\mu}}\cdot\\
&\left[\left\langle\phi(x_{1})\phi^{\dagger}(x_{2})\right\rangle+2\theta_{1}^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}}\left\langle\psi_{\alpha}(x_{1})\bar{\psi}_{\dot{\alpha}}(x_{2})\right\rangle+\theta_{1}^{2}\bar{\theta}_{2}^{2}\left\langle F(x_{1})F^{\dagger}(x_{2})\right\rangle\right]\penalty\ .\end{split}

(3.51)

Here,
z_{n}=(x_{n},\theta_{n},\bar{\theta}_{n})
are again supercoordinates.
The generalized propagators of the components are derived from the inverse of the kinetic operators in the generalized action (
3.49
).
For the fermion, it was done in (
3.34
) and for the boson and the auxiliary field we use the result of (
3.4
) to find

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi(x_{1})\phi^{\dagger}(x_{2})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\phantom{-\mathrm{i}}\;G_{1+v}(x_{12})\penalty\ ,

(3.52a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\psi_{\alpha}(x_{1})\bar{\psi}_{\dot{\alpha}}(x_{2})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\,\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{1,\mu}\;G_{1+v}(x_{12})\penalty\ ,

(3.52b)

\displaystyle\left\langle F(x_{1})F^{\dagger}(x_{2})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\phantom{-\mathrm{i}}\square_{1}\;G_{1+v}(x_{12})\penalty\ .

(3.52c)

Here we used the notation
x_{12}^{\mu}=x_{1}^{\mu}-x_{2}^{\mu}
and
G_{u}(x)
is the generalized scalar propagator defined in (
3.4
) for
D=4
.
For
u\rightarrow 0
,
G_{u}(x)
becomes proportional to a delta function, see the representation (
3.12
) in combination with (
3.4
).
This is in agreement with the expected delta function propagator for the non-dynamical auxiliary field (
3.52c
) in the undeformed theory.
Finally, by plugging (
3.52
) into (
3.51
), we obtain the generalized propagator of a chiral superfield

\begin{split}\left\langle\Phi(z_{1})\Phi^{\dagger}(z_{2})\right\rangle=(-4)^{-v}\frac{\Gamma(1-v)}{\Gamma(1+v)}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left[\theta_{1}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{1}+\theta_{2}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{2}-2\theta_{1}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{2}\right]\partial_{1,\mu}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{1-v}}=\frac{c_{0}(1+v)}{\left[x_{1\bar{2}}^{2}\right]^{1-v}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(3.53)

The exponential in (
3.53
) is a shift operator and produces the superconformal covariant interval

x_{1\bar{2}}^{\mu}:=x_{12}^{\mu}+\mathrm{i}\left[\theta_{1}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{1}+\theta_{2}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{2}-2\theta_{1}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{2}\right]
.
Note that even though we are in Minkowski spacetime we did not explicitly include the
\mathrm{i}\varepsilon
in the denominators of (
3.53
) for conciseness of notation.

Graphically the generalized superpropagator is represented by

\left\langle\Phi(z_{1})\Phi^{\dagger}(z_{2})\right\rangle=c_{0}(1+v)\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},

(3.54)

where the little arrow indicates the chiral end and
a_{0}(u)
is a normalization defined in (
3.5
).
Observe that by tuning all spectral parameters to
v=0
, the generalized superpropagator (
3.53
) reduces to the ordinary superpropagator
\nicefrac{{1}}{{x_{1\bar{2}}^{2}}}
derived in
[
151
]
because for
D=4
we have
c_{0}(1)=1
.
Similar to the bosonic and fermionic cases, the pre-factor in (
3.54
) is just the proper normalization of the super Feynman diagrams under investigation.
Accordingly, we will focus in the following on the weight function

\frac{1}{\left[x_{1\bar{2}}^{2}\right]^{u}}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(3.55)

which happens to be the superspace generalization of (
3.7
).
Here
u\in\mathbb{C}
is the spectral parameter needed to exploit the integrability of superspace Feynman diagrams.

The derivation of the
D=3

\mathcal{N}=2
superpropagator works analogously and we state the result

\begin{split}\left\langle\Phi(z_{1})\Phi^{\dagger}(z_{2})\right\rangle=(-1)^{v}2^{-\frac{1}{2}-2v}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-v)}{\Gamma(1+v)}\frac{1}{\left[x_{1\bar{2}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}-v}}=\frac{c_{0}(1+v)}{\left[x_{1\bar{2}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}-v}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(3.56)

In three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
superspace, the superconformal covariant interval is

x_{1\bar{2}}^{\mu}:=x_{12}^{\mu}+\mathrm{i}\left[\theta_{1}\gamma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{1}+\theta_{2}\gamma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{2}-2\theta_{1}\gamma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{2}\right]
, where the sigma matrices are replaced by three-dimensional gamma matrices, see appendix
D.2
.
Note that in the undeformed superfishnet theory, we will encounter factors
c_{0}(1)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}
.
Also for the three-dimensional superspace, we employ the graphical notation (
3.55
) for the superspace-dependent part of the propagator and write the spectral parameter next to it, while the dependence on the dimension
D
is implicit.

In both superspaces, whenever the spectral parameter next to a superpropagator (
3.55
) is not specified in the superspace diagrams below, we understand it to be set to the default value, which is
u=1
and
u=\frac{1}{2}
in four- and three-dimensional bosonic spacetime, respectively.
Partially, we will also affix a flavor index
i
to the superpropagator; we hope that the difference between a spectral parameter and a flavor index will be clear from the context.

Moreover, we stress that the superpropagator in both superspaces is invariant under the global
\mathrm{U}(1)
R-symmetry.
It acts on fermionic coordinates as
\theta\rightarrow\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}\theta
and
\bar{\theta}\rightarrow\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\bar{\theta}
, and on the chiral superfields as

\begin{array}[]{cc}\Phi\rightarrow\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\alpha}\,\Phi(x,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}\theta,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\bar{\theta})\penalty\ ,&\Phi^{\dagger}\rightarrow\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\alpha}\,\Phi^{\dagger}(x,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}\theta,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\bar{\theta})\penalty\ .\end{array}

(3.57)

Consequently, the chiral superfield and its conjugate are defined to have R-charge
+\tfrac{1}{2}
and
-\tfrac{1}{2}
, respectively.

Besides the propagator, we will encounter two other formal superspace two-point functions,

\frac{\theta_{12}^{2}}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u}}=\scalebox{0.7}[0.7]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\bar{\theta}_{12}^{2}}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u}}=\scalebox{0.7}[0.7]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(3.58)

which have R-charge
-2
and
2
, and represent chiral and anti-chiral delta functions on the fermionic subspace, respectively.
Here we introduced the abbreviation
\theta_{12}=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}
.
The dependence on the bosonic coordinates is that of a bosonic generalized propagator (
3.7
).
Therefore, there is the possibility that the spectral parameter approaches
\frac{D}{2}
.
Assuming the proper normalization, by the representation of the
D
-dimensional bosonic delta function (
3.12
), the two-point functions (
3.58
) can turn into chiral and anti-chiral superspace delta functions, respectively.
Graphically, we denote them with a dashed line,

\delta^{(2)}\left(\theta_{12}\right)\delta^{(D)}\left(x_{12}\right)=\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\hskip 5.69046pt\delta^{(2)}\left(\bar{\theta}_{12}\right)\delta^{(D)}\left(x_{12}\right)=\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.59)

3.3.2
Super chain relations

A handy tool in calculating supergraphs are the super chain relations.
They allow the reduction of the super-convolution of two two-point functions to a single one and are the superspace equivalents of the bosonic- and fermionic relations in sections
3.1.1
and
3.2
, respectively.
We compactly display the relations graphically for both superspaces, the three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
one and the four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
one.
At first, we consider the convolution of two generalized superpropagators (
3.55
).
We find their anti-chiral chain rule
5
5

5

Note that we can lift the restriction to chiral external superspace points by action with the operators
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{1}\gamma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{1}\partial_{1,\mu}}
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{2}\gamma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{2}\partial_{2,\mu}}
. Accordingly, the chiral chain rule can be lifted by similar operators.

\displaystyle\left.\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}_{0}\frac{1}{\left[x_{1\bar{0}}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{2\bar{0}}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{0}=0\\
\bar{\theta}_{1,2}=0\end{subarray}}=\frac{\theta_{12}^{2}}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}+u_{2}-\frac{D}{2}+1}}\cdot\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,r_{0}(2-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\\
-4\,r_{0}(3-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\end{matrix}\right.\penalty\ ,

(3.60a)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,r_{0}(2-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\\
-4\,r_{0}(3-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\end{matrix}\right.\penalty\ ,

(3.60b)

and its chiral counterpart

\displaystyle\left.\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta_{0}\frac{1}{\left[x_{0\bar{1}}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{0\bar{2}}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\theta}_{0}=0\\
\theta_{1,2}=0\end{subarray}}=\frac{\bar{\theta}_{12}^{2}}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}+u_{2}-\frac{D}{2}+1}}\cdot\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,r_{0}(2-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\\
-4\,r_{0}(3-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\end{matrix}\right.\penalty\ ,

(3.61a)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,r_{0}(2-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\\
-4\,r_{0}(3-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\end{matrix}\right.\penalty\ .

(3.61b)

Here, the upper case corresponds to the chain relation in
D=3

\mathcal{N}=2
superspace, while the lower case is related to
D=4

\mathcal{N}=1
superspace.
Respectively, the spinor conventions of appendix
D.2
and
D.1
have to be used, and their difference is the reason for the respective minus sign in the chain relations.
The chain relations (
3.60
) and (
3.61
) are proven in appendix
A.3.1
.
A comment on the color-coding is in place:
here and in the following we will denote internal, integrated, chiral (anti-chiral) vertices by a filled red (green) dot, which indicate the integration over the bosonic subspace in addition to the chiral (anti-chiral) fermionic subspace.
Note that the Graßmann delta functions in (
3.60
) and (
3.61
) annihilate the part of the fermionic integration whose chirality is opposite to the one of the vertex at hand.
Hence, propagators (
3.55
) always connect the chiral and anti-chiral subspaces of superspace, while R-charged two-point functions (
3.58
) connect the subspaces homogeneously.
When an external point in a super Feynman diagram is expected to be integrated by a chiral (anti-chiral) vertex in a later step according to the Feynman vertex rules of the QFT under examination, we denote this by a un-filled red (green) circle.

There exists a critical limiting case of (
3.60
) and (
3.61
), which is when
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are chosen to add up to
D-1
.
Then, the r. h. s. also becomes a delta function on the bosonic space, c. f. (
3.12
).
The resulting relations are

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

\displaystyle=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,\pi^{3}\cdot a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(2-u)\\
-4\,\pi^{4}\cdot a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(3-u)\end{matrix}\right.\penalty\ ,

(3.62a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

\displaystyle=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,\pi^{3}\cdot a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(2-u)\\
-4\,\pi^{4}\cdot a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(3-u)\end{matrix}\right.\penalty\ .

(3.62b)

Furthermore, we consider convolutions of a superpropagator (
3.55
) with an R-charged two-point function (
3.58
).
Starting with the anti-chiral version, we find

\displaystyle\left.\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}_{0}\frac{1}{\left[x_{1\bar{0}}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{\bar{\theta}_{02}^{2}}{\left[x_{20}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{0,2}=0\\
\bar{\theta}_{1}=0\end{subarray}}=r_{0}(D-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\;\frac{1}{\left[x_{1\bar{2}}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}+u_{2}-\frac{D}{2}}}\penalty\ ,

(3.63a)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\left\{\begin{matrix}r_{0}(3-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\\
r_{0}(4-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\end{matrix}\right.\penalty\ ,

(3.63b)

and its chiral counterpart

\displaystyle\left.\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta_{0}\frac{1}{\left[x_{0\bar{1}}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{\theta_{02}^{2}}{\left[x_{20}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\theta}_{0,2}=0\\
\theta_{1}=0\end{subarray}}=r_{0}(D-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\;\frac{1}{\left[x_{2\bar{1}}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}+u_{2}-\frac{D}{2}}}\penalty\ ,

(3.64a)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\left\{\begin{matrix}r_{0}(3-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\\
r_{0}(4-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\end{matrix}\right.\penalty\ .

(3.64b)

The proof is straightforward: after writing the superpropagator via a differential shift operator, we can perform the bosonic spacetime integral with the bosonic chain relation (
3.8
) and the fermionic space integral is trivially annihilated by the Graßmann delta function
\theta^{2}_{ij}
or
\bar{\theta}^{2}_{ij}
.

3.3.3
Super x-unity relation

The super x-unity relation is the pivotal auxiliary relation for calculating the critical coupling of supersymmetric QFTs in chapter
6
.
It allows us to find the inverse of the graph-building row-matrix of the super vacuum graphs and to apply the method of inversion relations.
The derivation of the super x-unity relation relies on the super chain relations in section
3.3.2
.

Let us make two observations before deriving the super x-unity relation.

•

Consider a three-spiked star integral, where the weights add up to
D-1
, and where we take one superpropagator weight to zero.
Then the propagator disappears in the limit, and we obtain the integral relation (
3.62a
).
This yields the relation

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,\pi^{3}a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(2-u)\\
-4\,\pi^{4}a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(3-u)\end{matrix}\right\}\cdot\penalty\ \scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.65)

•

We obtain another helpful relation when integrating one external point of a three-spiked star integral over the chiral subspace of superspace, in the case where the propagator weights add up to
D-1
:

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,\pi^{3}a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(2-u)\\
-4\,\pi^{4}a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(3-u)\end{matrix}\right\}\cdot\penalty\ \scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(3.66)

The derivation of the relation (
3.66
) is shown in detail in appendix
A.3.2
.
Therein, the use of the bosonic STR (
3.10
) is crucial, which is a direct imprint of integrability.
We observe that the whole expression reduces to a factor independent of
u_{2}
.

With the help of the relations (
3.62b
), (
3.65
) and (
3.66
) we can follow the steps of (
3.16
) to derive the super x-unity relations for the
D=3

\mathcal{N}=2
(upper case) and
D=4

\mathcal{N}=1
superspace (lower case) and we find, respectively,

\displaystyle\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,\pi^{3}a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(2-u)\\
-4\,\pi^{4}a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(3-u)\end{matrix}\right\}\cdot\hskip 14.22636pt\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(3.67a)

\displaystyle\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,\pi^{3}a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(2-u)\\
-4\,\pi^{4}a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(3-u)\end{matrix}\right\}\cdot\hskip 14.22636pt\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.67b)

The last equation is the chiral counterpart of the x-unity derived above and it is shown the same way by using the relations with inverted chirality.

3.3.4
Superconformal star integral and a super star-triangle relation?

To construct commuting transfer matrices, a STR is sufficient.
However, for superspace propagators, it seems to be missing.
It prevents us from building an integrable model from the superspace propagators.
This raises the question of how integrability is present in superspace vacuum diagrams.
We argue that superspace vacuum graphs still hide an integrable structure, since the super x-unity relations (
3.67
) still required the bosonic STR (
3.10
).
However, the explicit form of a superspace STR is still unclear.

We can think of the naive superspace generalization where the star side of the STR consists of three superspace propagators of the form (
3.55
) meet in an integrated chiral or anti-chiral vertex.
In four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace, there is Osborn’s star integral
[
154
,
155
]
.
It reads

\begin{split}&\mathrm{i}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{0}\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}_{0}\;\delta^{(2)}(\theta_{0})\;\frac{1}{\left[x_{1\bar{0}}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{2\bar{0}}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{3\bar{0}}^{2}\right]^{u_{3}}}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3}=3}}{{=}}-4\,r_{0}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\;\frac{\left(\theta_{12}\theta_{13}\right)x_{23,+}^{2}+\left(\theta_{23}\theta_{21}\right)x_{31,+}^{2}+\left(\theta_{31}\theta_{32}\right)x_{12,+}^{2}}{[x_{12,+}^{2}]^{2-u_{3}}[x_{23,+}^{2}]^{2-u_{1}}[x_{31,+}^{2}]^{2-u_{2}}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(3.68)

Here, the abbreviations
x_{ij,+}^{\mu}:=x_{i,+}^{\mu}-x_{j,+}^{\mu}
are used.
Note the similarity of (
3.68
) with a star-triangle relation (STR), see e. g.
[
96
,
32
,
33
]
.
Generally, a STR is a relation between a subgraph in the shape of a three-spiked star and a triangle, modulo some factor, which depends on the model under investigation.
However, the r.h.s. of (
3.68
) is not quite of the form of a triangle built from superpropagators of the type (
3.54
), due to the non-factorizing numerator.
Remarkably, despite this shortcoming, in the below chapter
6
we will provide exciting evidence for the model’s integrability by demonstrating that Zamolodchikov’s method of inversions
[
31
]
, see also
[
1
]
, may nevertheless be successfully applied.

3.3.5
Spinning generalized superpropagators

Similar to the fermionic generalized propagators in section (
3.2
), we can generalize the superpropagator by acting with
S
derivatives on it.
The same can be done with the R-charged two-point functions (
3.58
).
We require the derivatives to be contracted with a traceless and symmetric tensor and then find

\displaystyle\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}:=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(u-\frac{S}{2})}{\Gamma(u+\frac{S}{2})}\frac{\partial_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots\partial_{1}^{\mu_{S}}}{(-2)^{S}}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\frac{1}{\left[x_{1\bar{2}}^{2}\right]^{u}}\prod_{i=0}^{S}\frac{x_{1\bar{2}}^{\mu_{i}}}{|x_{1\bar{2}}|}\penalty\ ,

(3.69a)

\displaystyle\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}:=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(u-\frac{S}{2})}{\Gamma(u+\frac{S}{2})}\frac{\partial_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots\partial_{1}^{\mu_{S}}}{(-2)^{S}}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\frac{\theta_{12}^{2}}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u}}\prod_{i=0}^{S}\frac{x_{12}^{\mu_{i}}}{|x_{12}|}\penalty\ .

(3.69b)

Of course, a similar expression is obtained for the anti-chiral R-charged two-point function, but we focus here on those relations that are necessary for the diagonalization of the superfishnet graph-builder in section
6.1.5
.
From the definitions of the spinning superweights (
3.69a
), we observe that we can merge a spinning
S\neq 0
with a non-spinning
S=0
superweight, whenever they start and end at the same superspace points.
The resulting spinning superpropagator carries the sum of the two merged spectral parameters.

With the spinning super-two-point functions, we can generalize some of the three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
super chain relations in section
3.3.2
.
We find

\displaystyle=4\,r_{\frac{S}{2}}(u_{2},u_{1},2-u_{1}-u_{2})\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(3.70a)

\displaystyle=r_{\frac{S}{2}}(u_{1},u_{2},3-u_{1}-u_{2})\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(3.70b)

Notice that the only difference to the non-spinning super chain relations
3.3.2
is that the index of the
r_{\ell}
-factor (
3.9
) captures the dependence on
S
.
The proof of (
3.70
) is easily obtained by letting the differentials of (
3.69
) act on the non-spinning super chain relations
3.3.2
and rearranging the gamma functions in the
r_{\ell}
-factor.

The (super) propagators of this chapter can be thought of as weights in the bulk of a Feynman (super) graph.
Before taking the insights of this chapter into practice in chapters
5.2
and (
6
), we present another class of lattice weights in the next chapter, which relate to the concept of boundary integrability and are therefore called boundary weights.

Chapter 4
Boundary integrability in Feynman graphs

Establishing the integrability of a model frequently requires finding a suitable R-matrix that solves the Yang-Baxter equation (YBE).
We may then introduce the concept of spectral lines intersecting at an R-matrix.
If possible and as a first step, one postpones the question about boundary conditions and considers several spectral lines living on a doubly periodic torus.
This is instrumental for the construction of the transfer matrix:
a closed spectral line, with intersecting further spectral lines, and wrapping around one cycle of a torus.
The YBE implies the commutativity of the product of two transfer matrices by the train track argument; see (
2.28
).
Graphically, the “product” means that the intersecting spectral lines of the two transfer matrices are suitably contracted.
Commuting transfer matrices are a sufficient condition for integrability, since expanding the transfer matrix in its spectral parameter (graphically, the difference of slopes of the intersecting spectral lines) gives infinitely many conserved charges.

Aside from the doubly periodic boundary condition, one can imagine introducing one, or even multiple, boundaries for the spectral lines.
Some examples of such bounded spaces are the semi-infinite cylinder with one boundary, the finite cylinder with two boundaries and one periodic direction, and the box as introduced for the six-vertex model, see section
2.3
.
Consequently, one has to determine what happens when a spectral line hits a boundary.
Commonly, the spectral lines are assumed to be reflected by the boundary, or similarly the boundary serves as a source or sink for a pair of spectral lines.
The behavior is described by another object, the so-called K-matrix, which graphically sits at the point where the spectral lines bounce, spawn, or vanish.

Along a non-periodic direction, the construction of a periodic transfer matrix is impossible.
Hence, one might wonder how to construct commuting objects and how to maintain integrability.
In the case of two boundaries on opposite sides, Sklyanin showed that commuting double-row transfer matrices can be built
[
125
]
.
The commutativity is a consequence of the YBE and a novel relation, the boundary Yang-Baxter equation (bYBE).
Thereby, the bYBE has to be fulfilled by the K-matrix, while the R-matrix in the bulk is constrained by the requirement that the YBE holds.

In this chapter, we will introduce the notion of boundary integrability for generalized Feynman graphs.
First, we introduce reflected medial lines that have to be compatible with bulk integrability.
We use the map (
3.20
) to translate the graphical picture of reflecting medial lines satisfying a bYBE to Feynman diagrams.
Thus, the K-matrix is associated with the so-called
boundary weights
and we find two distinct versions of them.
Furthermore, we show that the bYBE gives rise to two boundary star-triangle relations (bSTR).
Based on this, we continue with deriving the fused K-matrix in analogy to (
3.26
) and we show that it solves a bYBE as well.
Recently, a similar K-matrix was derived from algebraic considerations of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})

[
156
,
157
]
, here we present a graphical approach.

4.1
The K-matrix and the boundary weights

We start by assigning a K-matrix to a medial line that bounces off a boundary.
First, we consider the case of a boundary to the left.
Of course, there is also a K-matrix for possible other boundaries and for every boundary that can be a different solution of the bYBE.
In contrast to the source/sink formulation in section
2.3
for the six-vertex model, we restrict ourselves to the case where the medial lines are reflected at the boundary.
Then the faces of the medial graph correspond to spacetime points, and since we do not introduce a shading yet, we obtain the factorized K-matrix analog of (
3.23
),

K^{y_{1}}_{y_{2}x}(p)=V_{p}(x)V_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})=\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(4.1)

We introduced some new graphical notations.
To obtain two independent Feynman graphs after shading the medial graph, we have two different boundary weights
V_{p}(x)
and
V_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})
.
They depend on one (red) and two (green) points, and we call them of types I and II, respectively.
We denote them by drawing a box around the spacetime point in the argument or by connecting the two spacetime points by a curly line.
Importantly, the boundary weights
V_{p}(x)
and
V_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})
are fundamentally different and cannot be related by a crossing transformation, as is the case for the bulk weights, which build up the R-matrix (
3.23
).
Furthermore, we denote the reflected medial line by a different arrowhead.
Its spectral parameter
p^{\prime}
has to be related to the original one,
p
, and it turns out that setting
p^{\prime}=-p
is a useful choice to avoid additional factors in the bYBEs.
Furthermore, the solutions we find below require this relation.
However, in the following, we keep using the prime-notation
p^{\prime}=-p
, since it allows for a concise notation.

The reflected medial lines have to satisfy the STR as well to preserve bulk integrability.
In chapter
3
the canonical directions of the medial lines were up and to the left, here we potentially will also encounter reflected lines, which point down or to the right.
Therefore we require the YBEs

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},&\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},&\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}},\end{array}

(4.2)

to hold.
In turn, we have to extend the relation between generalized propagators and medial lines in (
3.18
) to intersections involving reflected medial lines.
In accordance with the uniqueness relation (
3.11
) as a YBE in the sense of (
4.2
), we have additionally to (
3.18
)

\displaystyle W_{u}(x_{12})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u=q-p}}{{=}}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\bar{W}_{u}(x_{12})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u=q-p}}{{=}}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(4.3a)

\displaystyle W_{u}(x_{12})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u=p-q}}{{=}}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\bar{W}_{u}(x_{12})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u=p-q}}{{=}}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(4.3b)

In summary, whenever a medial line intersects with a reflected one, we have an exponent of the generalized propagator related to the
left minus right
spectral parameter.
Furthermore, we exchange the crossed weight with the ordinary one in this case.
If two reflected medial lines intersect, the exponent is related to a difference of the same order as if two ordinary medial lines meet,
right minus left
.
In addition, the crossed weights are, as usual, (
3.18
), related to a propagator passing in between the spectral lines.

The bYBE, expressed graphically, is the relation

\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\hskip 18.49988pt=\hskip 18.49988pt\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(4.4)

It is the boundary analog of (
3.24
) and since the both objects, the R-matrix and the K-matrix, factorize, we have two independent, but dual, generalized Feynman graphs
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{*}
in (
4.4
), drawn in red and green.
Algebraically, the diagram (
4.4
) encodes the equation

\begin{split}&\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\,\mathrm{d}^{D}y_{0}\penalty\ R_{x_{1}y_{2}}^{x_{0}y_{1}}(p_{12})K_{y_{0}x_{0}}^{y_{1}}(p_{1})R_{y_{0}x_{0}}^{y_{2}x_{2}}(p_{1^{\prime}2})K_{y_{3}x_{2}}^{y_{0}}(p_{2})\\
=&\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\,\mathrm{d}^{D}y_{0}\penalty\ K_{y_{0}x_{1}}^{y_{1}}(p_{2})R_{y_{0}x_{1}}^{y_{2}x_{0}}(p_{2^{\prime}1})K_{y_{3}x_{0}}^{y_{0}}(p_{1})R_{x_{0}y_{2}}^{x_{2}y_{3}}(p_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}})\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(4.5)

which is a face-type bYBE.
With the help of intertwining vectors
[
146
]
, we can turn it into the familiar vertex-type form due to Sklyanin
[
125
]
.
We formally expressed the solution (
4.1
) in terms of the boundary weights
V_{p}(x)
and
V_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})
.
Consequently, we wish to determine the boundary weights, and for this purpose, we first derive the necessary relations for them from (
4.4
).

Hence, due to the fundamental difference of the boundary weights in the K-matrix (
4.1
), we obtain two different bSTRs.
One corresponds to the generalized Feynman graph and the other to its dual.
This is in contrast to the bulk YBE (
4.1
), which implies only the STR to hold for the bulk weights on the Feynman graph and its dual.
The two bSTRs are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ &=\penalty\ \scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\\
V_{p_{2}}(x_{2})\int d^{D}x_{0}\;W_{\bar{p}_{12}}(x_{10})V_{p_{1}}(x_{0})&W_{p_{1^{\prime}2}}(x_{20})\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ V_{p_{2}}(x_{1})&\int d^{D}x_{0}\;W_{p_{2^{\prime}1}}(x_{10})V_{p_{1}}(x_{0})W_{\bar{p}_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}}(x_{20})\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(4.6a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ &=\penalty\ \scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\\
W_{p_{12}}(y_{12})\int d^{D}y_{0}\;V_{p_{1}}(y_{1},y_{0})W_{\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}2}}(y_{20})&V_{p_{2}}(y_{0},y_{3})\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ W_{p_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}}(y_{32})\int d^{D}y_{0}&\;V_{p_{2}}(y_{1},y_{0})W_{\bar{p}_{2^{\prime}1}}(y_{20})V_{p_{1}}(y_{0},y_{3})\penalty\ .\end{split}

(4.6b)

They separately only depend on one type of boundary weight,
V_{p}(x)
and
V_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})
, respectively.
Therefore, we have to find solutions to the bSTR which we can use to build integrable graphs with a integrability-preserving boundary.
Here and in the following, we use the abbreviations
p_{i^{\prime}j}=p_{i}^{\prime}-p_{j}
,
p_{ij^{\prime}}=p_{i}-p_{j}^{\prime}
and
p_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=p_{i}^{\prime}-p_{j}^{\prime}
.
In the field of statistical physics similar bSTR relations where obtained also for the chiral Potts model, among others
[
158
,
159
]
.

Before finding solutions to the bSTRs (
4.6
), we fuse a pair of medial lines.
This gives us a shading-preserving K-matrix
\mathbb{K}
, in analogy to the R-matrix
\mathbb{R}
in (
3.26
).
