However, the reflection of a medial line is not covariantly transforming under inversion of the shading, since the two types of boundary weights are fundamentally different.
Therefore, we make a choice and construct the K-matrix
\mathbb{K}
related to the boundary weight of type I.
Accordingly, the shaded medial- and the Feynman graph satisfy a fused boundary YBE that we denote by
\mathbb{bYBE}
,

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\hskip 18.49988pt=\hskip 18.49988pt\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(4.7)

It can be proven graphically by using the STR (
3.21
) and the red version of (
4.4
) multiple times for various medial lines, which help to shift the
(p_{1},p_{2})
-pair to the bottom.
We will show (
4.7
) on the Feynman graph level, using the STR (
3.11
) and the bSTR (
4.6a
).
We start with the Feynman graph on the l. h. s. of (
4.7
) and perform the steps

\begin{split}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bSTR_type1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bSTR_type1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bSTR_type1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bSTR_type1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(4.8)

such that we find the r. h. s. of (
4.7
).
As in the case of the
\mathbb{YBE}
, the steps (
4.8
) produce an overall factor.
It consists of all the
r_{0}
-factors that are produced every time we use the STR (
3.11
).
In the end they cancel, since the overall factor is

\frac{r_{0}(\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}2},\bar{p}_{24},p_{1^{\prime}4})\,r_{0}(\bar{p}_{2^{\prime}4},\bar{p}_{3^{\prime}2^{\prime}},p_{3^{\prime}4})\,r_{0}(\bar{p}_{3^{\prime}1^{\prime}},\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}4},p_{3^{\prime}4})\,r_{0}(\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}2},\bar{p}_{4^{\prime}1^{\prime}},p_{4^{\prime}2})\,}{r_{0}(\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}2},\bar{p}_{23},p_{1^{\prime}3})\,r_{0}(\bar{p}_{3^{\prime}2},\bar{p}_{24},p_{3^{\prime}4})\,r_{0}(\bar{p}_{14},\bar{p}_{3^{\prime}1},p_{3^{\prime}4})\,r_{0}(\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}2},\bar{p}_{3^{\prime}1^{\prime}},p_{3^{\prime}2})\,}\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{\prime}=-p}}{{=}}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ .

(4.9)

To show that this really collapses to one, we cancel
a_{0}
-factors in the
r_{0}
-abbreviations (
3.9
) in the nominator and denominator, use the the relation
a_{0}(v)a_{0}(\bar{v})=1
(c. f. (
3.5
)) and explicitly use the parametrization
p^{\prime}=-p
.

Within the
\mathbb{bYBE}
, we find the fused K-matrix
\mathbb{K}
related to the boundary weights of type I,

\mathbb{K}^{x_{1}}_{x_{2}}(p_{1},p_{2})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=V_{p_{1}}(x_{1})W_{p_{1^{\prime}2}}(x_{12})V_{p_{2}}(x_{2})\penalty\ .

(4.10)

Recall that the reflected medial lines must, in any case, carry a spectral parameter related to the unreflected one, hence, the K-matrix (
4.10
) only depends on two spectral parameters.

So far, we have paved the way for a notion of boundary integrability that yields a K-matrix (
4.10
) for the construction of boundary integrable Feynman graphs.
However, we still have to find a concrete solution of the type I boundary weight
V_{p}(x)
for the bSTR (
4.6a
).
We present two solutions and recall that
p^{\prime}=-p
:

•

The
trivial boundary weight
is
V_{p_{i}}(x)=1
or graphically
\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=1
.
It solves the bSTR (
4.6a
), because

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ChainRuleScalar}}}{{=}}r_{0}(\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}1},\bar{p}_{12},p_{1^{\prime}2})\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ChainRuleScalar}}}{{=}}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(4.11)

It can be seen by starting on each side of (
4.6a
) separately and applying the chain relation (
3.8
) to find the middle expression in (
4.11
).
Note that
r_{0}(\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}1},\bar{p}_{12},p_{1^{\prime}2})=r_{0}(\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}1},\bar{p}_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}},p_{2^{\prime}1})
holds.

•

The
canonical boundary weight
is another solution of the type I bSTR (
4.6a
).
It is
V_{p_{i}}(x_{j})=W_{\bar{p}_{i^{\prime}i}}(x_{\mathrm{L}j})
or graphically
\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}
.
The boundary weight depends on an external point
x_{\mathrm{L}}
, where the subscript refers to the position of the boundary on the left.
It has to be understood as a global parameter, common to all left-hand side boundary weights in some generalized Feynman graph.
We denote it by a cross instead of an unfilled circle to stress its special relation to the left boundary.
The bSTR is satisfied with the canonical boundary weight,

\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{=}}r_{0}(\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}1},\bar{p}_{12},p_{1^{\prime}2})\cdot\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:CFTUniqueness_Scalar_Diagram}}}{{=}}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(4.12)

thanks to the STR (
3.11
).
This explains its name; the bulk STR implies the bSTR for the particular choice of boundary weight.
For this weight the K-matrix
\mathbb{K}
(
4.10
) has the form of a triangle,

\mathbb{K}^{x_{1}}_{x_{2}}(p_{1},p_{2};x_{\mathrm{L}})=W_{\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}1}}(x_{1\mathrm{L}})W_{p_{1^{\prime}2}}(x_{12})W_{\bar{p}_{2^{\prime}2}}(x_{2\mathrm{L}})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(4.13)

It would be very interesting to continue the classification of solutions to the bSTR (
4.6a
) coming from integrable generalized Feynman graphs.
Usually, the “boundaries” of a Feynman graph are external spacetime points, which have their origin in considering QFT correlation functions of local operators.
The canonical boundary weight can be employed to model generalized Feynman graphs that contribute to these quantities.
Thus, each boundary is associated with one external point, which is called
x_{\mathrm{L}}
above.
Inserting Wilson lines into the correlation functions could yield Feynman diagrams, which admit a generalization in the exponents of the propagators
[
160
]
.
If the propagators connecting the Wilson line and the bulk of a Feynman diagram have a suitable generalization, which satisfies the bSTR, the whole toolbox of boundary integrability could be applied to the calculation of correlation functions invloving these objects.

4.2
K-matrix of a boundary on the right hand side

Next, we study the bYBE,
\mathbb{bYBE}
and K-matrices on a boundary on the right.
The methods are very similar to the previous left-boundary case.
However, requiring the medial lines to be reflected going upward changes the exponents one assigns to the generalized propagators.
Aside from this small difference, the derivation of the K-matrix
K
, the bYBE factorizing into the two types of bSTRs, the doubling of the medial lines for consistent shading, the
\mathbb{bYBE}
and the K-matrix
\mathbb{K}
is very similar to the left-boundary case.
Therefore, we present these results concisely for the right boundary.

We consider the reflection of a medial line on a boundary on the right and describe the process by the K-matrix

K^{\mathrm{R}y_{1}}_{\penalty\ y_{2}x}(p)=V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p}(x)V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(4.14)

The K-matrices and boundary weights carry the superscript
\mathrm{R}
to make clear that they correspond to the right boundary.
In the last section, consistently, we should have indicated the correspondence of objects to the left boundary by a superscript
\mathrm{L}
, but we did not for the sake of conciseness.
Their representation in Feynman graphs is the same as for the left boundary (
4.1
).
We denote the boundary weights of type I,
V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p}(x)
, as a box around the spacetime point
x
and those of type II,
V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})
, as a wiggled line that connects the points
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
.
For the sake of integrability, we require also the right K-matrix
K^{\mathrm{R}}
to satisfy a bYBE, which corresponds to the picture

\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\hskip 18.49988pt=\hskip 18.49988pt\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(4.15)

In terms of generalized Feynman graphs, we find two equations ensuring integrability as well.
They are the right hand side bSTRs,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ &=\penalty\ \scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\\
V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{2}}(x_{2})\int d^{D}x_{0}\;W_{\bar{p}_{21}}(x_{10})V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{1}}(x_{0})&W_{p_{21^{\prime}}}(x_{20})\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{2}}(x_{1})&\int d^{D}x_{0}\;W_{p_{12^{\prime}}}(x_{10})V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{1}}(x_{0})W_{\bar{p}_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}}(x_{20})\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(4.16a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ &=\penalty\ \scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\\
W_{p_{21}}(y_{12})\int d^{D}y_{0}\;V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{1}}(y_{1},y_{0})W_{\bar{p}_{21^{\prime}}}(y_{20})&V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{2}}(y_{0},y_{3})\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ W_{p_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}}(y_{32})\int d^{D}y_{0}&\;V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{2}}(y_{1},y_{0})W_{\bar{p}_{12^{\prime}}}(y_{20})V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{1}}(y_{0},y_{3})\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(4.16b)

of type I and II, respectively.
It is important to note that the solutions of the bSTRs on the left and the right do not have to be related in any way.
We are free to choose a particular solution on the left, e. g. the trivial one, and a solution of a different type on the right boundary.
Speaking of solutions, we focus again on the boundary weight of type I and (
4.16a
).
We find again the trivial one,
V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p}(x)=1
, and a canonical one.
However, the latter is slightly different from the left boundary solution, due to the different propagator exponents of (
4.16a
) compared to (
4.6a
).
It reads

V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{i}}(x_{j})=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=W_{\bar{p}_{ii^{\prime}}}(x_{\mathrm{R}j})=\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(4.17)

and proving that both solutions, the trivial one and the canonical one, satisfy the bSTR (
4.16a
) works in the same way as for the left boundary, (
4.11
) and (
4.12
), respectively.
Note that the introduction of an additional boundary also implies that the canonical solution depends on a different external point
x_{\mathrm{R}}
than the left-boundary canonical solution, which depends on
x_{\mathrm{L}}
.

Moreover, requiring a K-matrix with consistent shading of the medial graph leads to staggering two K-matrices
K^{\mathrm{R}}
into the K-matrix
\mathbb{K}^{\mathrm{R}}
, which is

\mathbb{K}^{\mathrm{R}x_{1}}_{\penalty\ \;x_{2}}(p_{1},p_{2})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{1}}(x_{1})W_{p_{21^{\prime}}}(x_{12})V^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{2}}(x_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{canonical}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(4.18)

and we show the K-matrix with the canonical solution of the bSTR.
It satisfies a staggered version of the bYBE on the right boundary, the right
\mathbb{bYBE}

\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\hskip 18.49988pt=\hskip 18.49988pt\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(4.19)

The proof works analogously to the left-boundary version (
4.7
) by performing similar steps to (
4.8
).

4.3
The double-row transfer matrix

The two
\mathbb{bYBE}
s (
4.7
) and (
4.19
) for the left and right boundary, respectively, together with the bulk
\mathbb{YBE}
(
3.28
) and the unitarity relations (
3.31
) are instrumental for the construction of commuting double-row transfer matrices.
This was first shown in
[
125
]
.
In the presence of boundaries, the double-row transfer matrix replaces the periodic transfer matrices (
5.52
) presented below as the generating object for conserved charges.
In the case of integrable, scalar generalized Feynman graphs, we can graphically represent the inhomogeneous, staggered, double-row transfer matrix as

\mathbb{T}_{N}(p_{a},p_{b},\{p_{i}\})=\scalebox{1.3}[1.3]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(4.20)

Two such transfer matrices with identical vertical medial lines and distinct horizontal medial lines commute, which can be shown by performing the same steps as for the six-vertex model in (
2.28
) with one pair of medial lines here replace a single line there.
The steps using bYBEs, YBE and unitarity should be adapted to the staggered relations
\mathbb{bYBE}
on the left (
4.7
),
\mathbb{bYBE}
on the right (
4.19
),
\mathbb{YBE}
(
3.28
) and unitarity (
3.31
).
The factors from unitarity cancel and hence we can show that

[\mathbb{T}_{N}(p_{a},p_{b},\{p_{i}\}),\mathbb{T}_{N}(q_{a},q_{b},\{p_{i}\})]=0\penalty\ .

(4.21)

The product of two transfer matrices is understood as a convolution over
N
spacetime points on the top of one factor and the bottom of the other one.
To remember this, we use the abbreviation
\circ
for the product.

We observe that the staggered double-row transfer matrix (
4.20
) can be factorized: we can disentangle the two horizontal medial lines
p_{a}
and
p_{b}
by
2N
-fold application of the STR (
3.21
) from one boundary to the other.
This way, we obtain

\mathbb{T}_{N}(p_{a},p_{b},\{p_{i}\})=\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{a_{0}(\bar{p}_{i+1,a^{\prime}})a_{0}(p_{i+1,b})}{a_{0}(\bar{p}_{ia^{\prime}})a_{0}(p_{ib})}\right]\cdot T_{N}(p_{a},\{p_{i}\})\circ T_{N}(p_{b},\{p_{i}\})\penalty\ ,

(4.22)

and the two factors are double-row transfer matrices themselves, however, not staggered anymore.
They are represented by

T_{N}(p_{a},\{p_{i}\})=\scalebox{1.5}[1.5]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(4.23)

and they are the fundamental commuting object, which can be shown again in analogy to the six-vertex model (
2.28
), now by employing the bYBEs (
4.4
) and (
4.15
), the STR (
3.21
) and unitarity (
3.22
).
We find

[T_{N}(p_{a},\{p_{i}\}),T_{N}(q_{a},\{p_{i}\})]=0\penalty\ ,

(4.24)

which of course implies (
4.21
).

In addition to the periodic transfer matrix (
5.52
), the double-row transfer matrix (
4.23
) allows us to construct other classes of integrable generalized Feynman graphs.
The specification of the boundary weights has to be adapted to the quantity under investigation, which admits an expansion in Feynman graphs generated by the double-row transfer matrix after tuning the spectral parameters to a particular value.
Performing this procedure with the canonical solutions on the left and right boundaries, periodic identification of a stack of
M
double row transfer matrices gives two-point functions depending on
x_{\mathrm{L}}
and
x_{\mathrm{R}}
.
Once we fix the vertical and horizontal spectral parameters,
\{p_{i}\}
and
\{p_{m}\}
, respectively, the remaining question is which QFT produces the Feynman graphs in the perturbative expansion of two-point functions.
A promising candidate for future research is the checkerboard theory
[
111
]
.

In the next chapter, we restrict our study to bulk integrability where the match to non-supersymmetric QFTs may be established and facilitate the calculation of exact results for the critical coupling, anomalous dimensions and OPE coefficient.
Similarly, we hope that the introduction of boundary integrability can lead to exact results of more observables in the future.

Chapter 5
Non-supersymmetric fishnet theories and their relatives

In this chapter it is shown how the double-scaling limit of the
\gamma
-deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM yields multiple simplified and integrable QFTs
[
75
]
.
Supersymmetry is generically broken and we obtain a family of integrable fishnet theories.
They consist of the
\chi
-CFT, which is also called dynamical fishnet theory
[
76
]
, the
\chi_{0}
-theory and the bi-scalar fishnet theory.
Here, we explicitly show their derivation starting from the
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace formulation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM and present a detailed review of the bi-scalar fishnet theory.
We consider its double-trace beta functions
[
77
,
78
,
79
]
, and a family of operators, whose scaling dimension can be determined exactly, as well as an all-loop result for a four-point correlation function
[
78
,
83
]
.

Moreover, we review Zamolodchikov’s computation of the critical coupling of the bi-scalar fishnet theory, the fishnet-deformation of ABJM, and a six-dimensional theory with hexagonal vacuum graphs
[
31
]
.
We present the generalization of his techniques, the method of inversion relations, to compute the critical couplings of fermionic theories as well.
We apply it to find the results for the brick wall theory and the three-dimensional fermionic fishnet theory
[
1
]
.

5.1
The bi-scalar fishnet theory

We review the derivation of bi-scalar fishnet theory from
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM and its quantization by regular Feynman graphs with R-matrix graph builders in the planar limit.
Some of the theory’s couplings run, but we show that they have a fixed point, which we determine perturbatively.
Working at the fixed point, we can use the methods of CFTs.
Furthermore, we present the integrability-based computations of the critical coupling and exact scaling dimensions of various operators.

5.1.1
Double-scaling limit of
\gamma
-deformed
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM

We present the derivation of the bi-scalar fishnet theory, starting from the
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace action of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM, then performing the
\gamma
-deformation and consecutively taking a double-scaling limit in the ’t Hooft coupling and the deformation parameter.
We present the actions of the
\chi
-,
\chi_{0}
-CFT and the bi-scalar fishnet theory with its Feynman rules.

The
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM action from a superspace formulation

Integrability is an important property of four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM in the planar limit.
The theory is a supersymmetric gauge theory and we take the gauge group to be
\mathrm{SU}(\mathrm{N})
, where the number of colors is
\mathrm{N}
and in the limit
\mathrm{N}\rightarrow\infty
planar diagrams dominate.
The supercharges generate the R-symmetry group
\mathrm{SU}(4)\cong\mathrm{SO}(6)
.
Therefore, the action of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM can be formulated in four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace, where the R-symmetry subgroup
\mathrm{U}(1)
is manifest.
It reads (see e. g.
[
161
,
68
]
)

\begin{split}S=&\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\sum_{i=1}^{3}\,\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-g\mathcal{V}}\Phi_{i}^{\dagger}\mathrm{e}^{g\mathcal{V}}\Phi_{i}\right)+\frac{1}{2g^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\;\mathrm{tr}\left(W^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}\right)\\
&+\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\;\mathrm{tr}\left(\Phi_{1}[\Phi_{2},\Phi_{3}]\right)+\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\mathrm{tr}\left(\Phi^{\dagger}_{1}[\Phi^{\dagger}_{2},\Phi^{\dagger}_{3}]\right)\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.1)

Its field content is a real vector superfield
\mathcal{V}
and three chiral superfields
\Phi_{i}
, all four in the adjoint representation of the gauge group
\mathrm{SU}(\mathrm{N})
, i. e.
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{A}T^{A}
and
\Phi_{i}=\Phi_{i,A}T^{A}
with the adjoint generators
T^{A}
(
A=1,...,\mathrm{N}^{2}-1
).
They satisfy

\begin{array}[]{ccc}[T^{A},T^{B}]=\mathrm{i}f^{ABC}T_{C}\penalty\ ,&\mathrm{tr}\left(T^{A}T^{B}\right)=\delta^{AB}\penalty\ ,&(T^{A})^{a}_{b}\left(T_{A}\right)^{c}_{d}=\delta^{a}_{d}\delta^{c}_{b}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{N}}\delta^{a}_{b}\delta^{c}_{d}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(5.2)

The supersymmetric field strength is
W_{\alpha}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\bar{D}^{2}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-g\mathcal{V}}D_{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{g\mathcal{V}}\right)
, with the covariant superderivatives defined in (
D.12a
).

We will show briefly how to obtain the component action of (
5.1
).
First, the real vector superfield in Wess-Zumino gauge admits the expansion

\mathcal{V}=-\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}A_{\mu}+\mathrm{i}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}\bar{\chi}-\mathrm{i}\bar{\theta}^{2}\theta\chi+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\mathrm{D}

(5.3)

with the gauge vectorfield
A_{\mu}
, the gaugino
\chi
and the auxiliary scalar
\mathrm{D}
.
By the vanishing of cubic Graßmann orders, we have
\mathcal{V}^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}A_{\mu}A^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{V}^{3}=0
.
This means the exponentials containing
\mathcal{V}
in the action (
5.1
) truncate after the second order.
Expanding the supersymmetric field strength gives the expression

W_{\alpha}=\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{4}\bar{D}^{2}D_{\alpha}\mathcal{V}-\frac{\mathrm{i}g^{2}}{8}\bar{D}^{2}[\mathcal{V},D_{\alpha}\mathcal{V}]

(5.4)

whose explicit component evaluation is lengthy.
It is handy to keep in mind that in the action the bilinear of
W
will be integrated only over chiral subspace, such that one can drop the
\bar{\theta}
-dependent terms eventually.
We find

W_{\alpha}|_{\bar{\theta}=0}=g\chi_{\alpha}+\,\mathrm{i}\,g\,\theta_{\alpha}\mathrm{D}+\frac{1}{2}\,g\,\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu,\dot{\alpha}\beta}\theta_{\beta}F_{\mu\nu}+\,\mathrm{i}\,g\,\theta^{2}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\bar{\chi}^{\dot{\alpha}}

(5.5)

with the field strength
F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}+\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{2}[A_{\mu},A_{\nu}]
and the gauge covariant derivative
\mathcal{D}_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{2}[A_{\mu},\cdot]
.
Therefore, the gauge part of the action (
5.1
) is in components

S_{\mathrm{g}}=\frac{1}{2g^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\;\mathrm{tr}\left(W^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}\right)=\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}\left[-\mathrm{i}\bar{\chi}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\chi-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8}F_{\mu\nu}F^{*,\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}^{2}\right]\penalty\ .

(5.6)

We find the kinetic term for the gaugino, the kinetic term for the non-Abelian field strength as well as the quadratic term of the auxiliary scalar.
The third term is the contraction of the field strength with its Hodge-dual, which is a total derivative and thus topological.

Next, we expand the first term in (
5.1
), containing the canonical Kähler potential, in its components to obtain

\begin{split}S_{\mathrm{K}}&=\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\sum_{i=1}^{3}\,\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-g\mathcal{V}}\Phi_{i}^{\dagger}\mathrm{e}^{g\mathcal{V}}\Phi_{i}\right)\\
&=\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\sum_{i=1}^{3}\,\mathrm{tr}\left[-\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}^{\mu}\phi_{i}-\mathrm{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\psi_{i}+\frac{1}{2}g\mathrm{D}[\phi_{i}^{\dagger},\phi_{i}]+F_{i}^{\dagger}F_{i}\right.\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\left.+\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\chi}\phi_{i}+\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\chi\psi_{i}-\bar{\chi}\bar{\psi}_{i}\phi_{i}-\chi\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\psi_{i}\right)\right]\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.7)

At this point, we can integrate out the auxiliary field
\mathrm{D}
by eliminating it through its equation of motion that reads

\mathrm{D}=\mathrm{D}_{A}T^{A}=\frac{g}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}[\phi_{i},\phi_{i}^{\dagger}]\penalty\ .

(5.8)

Since it is on-shell expressed by a commutator, potential double-trace terms by the last relation in (
5.2
) vanish.
This will be not the case anymore in the deformed theory
[
68
]
, as we will see below in section
6.2.1
.
Note that, once replaced in
S_{\mathrm{K}}
and
S_{\mathrm{g}}
, the field will on-shell generate quartic scalar interaction terms in the component action.
So does the superpotential in (
5.1
), after integrating out the auxiliary fields.
It solely contains (anti) chiral superfields, thus, the expansion (
3.50
) gives the component action of the superpotential

\begin{split}&S_{\mathrm{pot}}=\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\;\mathrm{tr}\left(\Phi_{1}[\Phi_{2},\Phi_{3}]\right)\\
&=\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}\Bigl(\phi_{1}[\phi_{2},F_{3}]+\phi_{1}[F_{2},\phi_{3}]+F_{1}[\phi_{2},\phi_{3}]-\phi_{1}[\psi_{2},\psi_{3}]-\psi_{1}[\phi_{2},\psi_{3}]-\psi_{1}[\psi_{2},\phi_{3}]\Bigr)\end{split}

(5.9)

and similarly for the hermitian conjugated superpotential action
\bar{S}_{\mathrm{pot}}
(replace fields by their daggered/barred partners).
The auxiliary fields are integrated out by their on-shell expression obtained by their equations of motion

\begin{aligned} F_{1}=\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}[\phi_{3}^{\dagger},\phi_{2}^{\dagger}]\penalty\ ,\\
F^{\dagger}_{1}=\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}[\phi_{3},\phi_{2}]\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} F_{2}=\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}[\phi_{1}^{\dagger},\phi_{3}^{\dagger}]\penalty\ ,\\
F^{\dagger}_{2}=\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}[\phi_{1},\phi_{3}]\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} F_{3}=\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}[\phi_{2}^{\dagger},\phi_{1}^{\dagger}]\penalty\ ,\\
F^{\dagger}_{3}=\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}[\phi_{2},\phi_{1}]\penalty\ .\end{aligned}

(5.10)

Again, this procedure potentially can produce double-trace terms, but, as it was the case for the auxiliary field
\mathrm{D}
, replacing the fields
F_{i}
by the commutators (
5.10
) and using (
5.2
) would give the trace of a commutator, which is zero.
Note that the sesquilinear terms of
F_{i}
in
S_{\mathrm{K}}
yield additional quartic scalar interactions.
So does the superpotential (
5.9
) after the replacements (
5.10
), it is

S_{\mathrm{pot}}=\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}\left[\frac{g^{2}}{2}\sum_{i<j}[\phi_{i},\phi_{j}][\phi_{i}^{\dagger},\phi_{j}^{\dagger}]-\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon^{ijk}\psi_{i}\phi_{j}\psi_{k}\right]

(5.11)

and the hermitian conjugated superpotential is again obtained by the replacement of fields by their daggered/barred partners.
We see that the first term is the same in both,
S_{\mathrm{pot}}
and
\bar{S}_{\mathrm{pot}}
.

Before reassembling the component action, we have a closer look on the quartic scalar interaction terms in the Lagrange density.
When we deform the theory in section
5.1.1
, it is useful to collect interaction terms with zero charge under R-symmetry subgroups.
The quartic terms in the Lagrangian are

\mathrm{tr}\left[\frac{g^{2}}{2}\sum_{i<j}[\phi_{i},\phi_{j}][\phi_{i}^{\dagger},\phi_{j}^{\dagger}]-\frac{g^{2}}{8}\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}[\phi_{i},\phi_{i}^{\dagger}][\phi_{j},\phi_{j}^{\dagger}]\right]\penalty\ ,

(5.12)

where the first term comes from the
F_{i}^{\dagger}F_{i}
-terms and from the chiral and anti-chiral superpotential, which yields the factor
-\frac{g^{2}}{2}+2\cdot\frac{g^{2}}{2}=\frac{g^{2}}{2}
.
The second terms is due to the terms from the auxiliary field,
\frac{1}{2}g\mathrm{D}[\phi_{i}^{\dagger},\phi_{i}]
and
\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}^{2}
, yielding the factor
-\frac{g^{2}}{4}+\frac{g^{2}}{8}=-\frac{g^{2}}{8}
.
We split the second sum in (
5.12
) into the diagonal and off-diagonal parts,
i=j
,
i<j
and
j<i
, respectively.
Obeying the symmetry properties of permutation of the indices, we can be combine it with the first term in (
5.12
) and we find the expression

\frac{g^{2}}{4}\mathrm{tr}\left[\sum_{i<j}\left(2\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\phi_{j}^{\dagger}+2\phi_{j}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}^{\dagger}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}-\{\phi_{i}^{\dagger},\phi_{i}\}\{\phi_{j}^{\dagger},\phi_{j}\}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\left(2\phi_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\phi_{i}^{\dagger},\phi_{i}\}^{2}\right)\right]\penalty\ .

(5.13)

Since the anti-commutator-term is symmetric under
i\leftrightarrow j
, we can combine the two sums and obtain the quartic terms

\frac{g^{2}}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\phi_{j}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{4}\{\phi_{i}^{\dagger},\phi_{i}\}\{\phi_{j}^{\dagger},\phi_{j}\}\right]\penalty\ .

(5.14)

The anti-commutator-term has the handy property that the fields
\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{i}^{\dagger}
are next to each other within the trace, which implies that they are unaltered by R-charge deformations.

Finally, we perform two rescalings: first, for convenience, we redefine
g\rightarrow-\sqrt{2}\;g
and second, for the sake of the ’t Hooft expansion, we rescale all fields by
\sqrt{\mathrm{N}}
and introduce the ’t Hooft coupling
\lambda:=g\cdot\sqrt{\mathrm{N}}
.
Effectively, the rescaling multiplies the action by the rank of the gauge group
\mathrm{N}
, whereas the coupling
g
gets replaced by
\lambda
, i. e.
S(g)\rightarrow\mathrm{N}\cdot S(\lambda)
.
We find the component action of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM to take the form

\begin{split}S=\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}&\left[-\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}^{\mu}\phi^{i}-\mathrm{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\psi^{i}-\mathrm{i}\bar{\chi}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\chi-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8}F_{\mu\nu}F^{*,\mu\nu}\right.\\
&\penalty\ -\mathrm{i}\lambda\left(\bar{\chi}\phi^{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}+\psi^{i}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\chi-\bar{\psi}_{i}\phi^{i}\bar{\chi}-\chi\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\psi^{i}-\varepsilon^{ijk}\psi_{i}\phi_{j}\psi_{k}-\varepsilon^{ijk}\bar{\psi}_{i}\phi_{j}^{\dagger}\bar{\psi}_{k}\right)\\
&\penalty\ +\left.\lambda^{2}\left(\phi^{i}\phi^{j}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\phi_{j}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{4}\{\phi_{i}^{\dagger},\phi^{i}\}\{\phi_{j}^{\dagger},\phi^{j}\}\right)\right]\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.15)

We changed to the Einstein sum-convention for the indices
i,j,k=1,2,3
.
The action (
5.15
) will be studied in the planar limit, where the coupling
g
goes to zero, while the rank of the gauge group,
\mathrm{N}
, is taken to infinity.
This is the planar limit and in summary we have

\begin{array}[]{ccc}g\rightarrow 0\penalty\ ,&\mathrm{N}\rightarrow\infty\penalty\ ,&\lambda^{2}=g^{2}\cdot\mathrm{N}\penalty\ \mathrm{finite.}\end{array}

(5.16)

Let us have a closer look at the component action (
5.15
).
The degrees of freedom are the gauge vector field
A_{\mu}
, three complex scalars
\phi_{i}
, three chiral fermions
\psi_{i}
and the chiral gaugino
\chi
.
All of them are in the adjoint representation of the gauge group
\mathrm{SU}(\mathrm{N})
, see (
5.2
), and they are represented by
\mathrm{N}\times\mathrm{N}
matrices.
Due to the overall factor of
\mathrm{N}
in the action (
5.15
), we can determine the overall scaling of
\mathrm{N}
of a particular Feynman graph in a perturbation expansion after quantization.
Interaction vertices contribute with a factor
N^{b_{0}}
, where
b_{0}
is the number of vertices of the graph.
A single propagator contributes
\frac{1}{\mathrm{N}}
to a Feynman graph, such that the overall propagator contribution is
\mathrm{N}^{-b_{1}}
, where
b_{1}
is the number of edges in the Feynman graph.
Lastly, since the fields are matrix valued, contracting interaction terms with propagators potentially yield traces of multiple fields.
Every trace is of the order
\mathrm{N}
.
To find the total number of such traces, the so-called
fat graph
notation, which we introduce for the fishnet theory in (
5.26
).
It allows to identify every face of the Feynman graph with a trace.
Therefore, the faces of the Feynman graph contribute
\mathrm{N}^{b_{2}}
.
Putting all the factors together, we see that the Feynman graphs can be organized according to their Euler characteristic (not to be confused with the gaugino)

\chi=b_{0}-b_{1}+b_{2}

(5.17)

with overall factor
\mathrm{N}^{\chi}
.
This means the diagrams with the lowest Euler characteristic dominate the perturbation expansion in the planar limit and we can neglect higher contributions.

Moreover, the action of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM (
5.15
) has many symmetries.
First, the action is invariant under the
\mathcal{N}=4
super Poincaré algebra, which is generated by translations, Lorentz transformation and four supercharges.
The
\mathcal{N}=1
action (
5.1
) makes the invariance under one of them, by grouping the fields into the chiral (
3.50d
) and anti-chiral superfields (
3.50d
), as well as the real vector superfield (
5.3
).
Second, the action (
5.15
) does not contain masses and all interaction terms are marginal (i. e. the coupling is dimensionless).
This implies that the theory is classically conformal invariant.
And even after quantization,
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM maintains this property
[
162
]
, because the contributions of divergent loop diagrams cancel each other, when summing over all possible fields, which can run in the diagrams’ loops
[
42
]
.
Hence, no masses or quantum corrections to the coupling are generated, and the beta function vanishes.
Third, for the superalgebra to close,
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM is invariant under a global
\mathrm{SU}(4)\cong\mathrm{SO}(6)
R-symmetry.
It acts as the fundamental representation
\mathbf{4}
on the fermions
(\psi_{i},\chi)
and as the
\mathbf{6}
on the six scalars, which are the real- and imaginary part of the three complex scalars
\phi_{i}
.
We can use the global R-symmetry transformations to rotate the scalars into a diagonal basis of the three Cartan generators of the
\mathfrak{su}(4)_{\mathrm{R}}
.
Doing so, we can obtain the frame used in the literature
[
68
]
, and the corresponding charges are displayed in table
5.1
.
Altogether, the fields of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM describe representations of the
\mathcal{N}=4
superconformal algebra
\mathfrak{psu}(2,2|4)
.

\psi_{1}

\psi_{2}

\psi_{3}

\chi

\phi_{1}

\phi_{2}

\phi_{3}

A_{\mu}

q_{1}

\phantom{-}\frac{1\mathstrut}{2}

-\frac{1}{2}

-\frac{1}{2}

\phantom{-}\frac{1}{2}

1

0

0

0

q_{2}

-\frac{1\mathstrut}{2}

\phantom{-}\frac{1}{2}

-\frac{1}{2}

\phantom{-}\frac{1}{2}

0

1

0

0

q_{3}

-\frac{1\mathstrut}{2}

-\frac{1}{2}

\phantom{-}\frac{1}{2}

\phantom{-}\frac{1}{2}

0

0

0

Table 5.1:
The charges of the component fields of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM under the Cartan-generators of the R-symmetry
\mathfrak{su}(4)_{\mathrm{R}}
. The conjugated fields transform with the negative charge under the Cartan generators.

The
\gamma
-deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM

Starting from the component action of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM (
5.15
), we will perform the so-called
\gamma
-deformation
[
65
,
68
]
.
It deforms the pointwise product of fields to a non-commutative
star product
.
In the case of the
\gamma
-deformation, the star product is meant to deform the R-symmetry by introducing a phase which depends on the R-charge of the factors.
For two fields
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
with
\mathfrak{su}(4)
-charges
q^{\varphi_{1}}
and
q^{\varphi_{2}}
, respectively, it is of the form

\varphi_{1}\cdot\varphi_{2}\rightarrow\varphi_{1}\star\varphi_{2}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\varepsilon^{ijk}\gamma_{i}\,q_{j}^{\varphi_{1}}q_{k}^{\varphi_{2}}}\varphi_{1}\cdot\varphi_{2}

(5.18)

and depends on three complex parameters
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{3}
.
We observe that we recover the undeformed, regular point-wise product when we set all three parameters to zero.
Next, we replace the products in all terms of the
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM action (
5.15
) by the star product.
Its definition (
5.18
) involves two factors, but the action also contains interactions, which have more factors.
For them we use the useful relation

\varphi_{1}\star\cdots\star\varphi_{p}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{m<n}\varepsilon^{ijk}\gamma_{i}\,q_{j}^{\varphi_{m}}q_{k}^{\varphi_{n}}}\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{p}\penalty\ ,

(5.19)

which follows from (
5.18
) if we recall that the Cartan-charges of a product combine additively.

We find the
\gamma
-deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
[
65
,
68
]
with the action

\begin{split}S=\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}&\left[-\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}^{\mu}\phi^{i}-\mathrm{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\psi^{i}-\mathrm{i}\bar{\chi}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\chi-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8}F_{\mu\nu}F^{*,\mu\nu}\right.\\
&\penalty\ -\mathrm{i}\lambda\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\gamma_{i}^{-}}\bar{\chi}\phi^{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}+\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\gamma_{i}^{-}}\psi^{i}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\chi-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\gamma_{i}^{-}}\bar{\psi}_{i}\phi^{i}\bar{\chi}-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\gamma_{i}^{-}}\chi\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\psi^{i}\right.\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -\left.\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\varepsilon_{ijk}\gamma_{j}^{+}}\varepsilon^{ijk}\psi_{i}\phi_{j}\psi_{k}-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\varepsilon_{ijk}\gamma_{j}^{+}}\varepsilon^{ijk}\bar{\psi}_{i}\phi_{j}^{\dagger}\bar{\psi}_{k}\right)\\
&\penalty\ +\left.\lambda^{2}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_{ijk}\gamma_{k}}\phi^{i}\phi^{j}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\phi_{j}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{4}\{\phi_{i}^{\dagger},\phi^{i}\}\{\phi_{j}^{\dagger},\phi^{j}\}\right)\right]\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.20)

Indices, which might appear more than two times are understood to be implicitly summed over.
We observe that for the different interaction terms, different combinations of the deformation parameters enter the action.
To write them compactly we use the abbreviations
[
75
]

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\gamma_{1}^{\pm}=-\frac{\gamma_{3}\pm\gamma_{2}}{2}\penalty\ ,&\gamma_{2}^{\pm}=-\frac{\gamma_{1}\pm\gamma_{3}}{2}\penalty\ ,&\gamma_{3}^{\pm}=-\frac{\gamma_{2}\pm\gamma_{1}}{2}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(5.21)

The double-scaling limit and fishnet theories

For the sake of simplifying the action of the
\gamma
-deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM dramatically, while preserving integrability, in
[
75
]
the double-scaling limit is proposed.
Conceptually similar to the planar limit, the double-scaling limit assumes the combinations
q_{i}:=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\gamma_{i}}
(not to be confused with the Cartan-charges) to tend to infinity at the same rate, and, in parallel, the ’t Hooft coupling
\lambda
is taken to zero.
These limits are carefully related to keep the three quantities
\xi_{i}:=q_{i}\cdot\lambda
at a finite value, i. e.

\begin{array}[]{ccc}q_{i}\rightarrow\infty\penalty\ ,&\lambda\rightarrow 0\penalty\ ,&\mathrm{while}\penalty\ \xi_{i}=q_{i}\cdot\lambda\penalty\ \mathrm{finite.}\end{array}

(5.22)

In the
\gamma
-deformed action (
5.15
), we find deformed interaction terms with an overall factor
\lambda
(by the gauge covariant derivative),
\lambda\sqrt{q_{k}/q_{j}}
for the Yukawa couplings with the gaugino,
\lambda\sqrt{q_{i}q_{k}}
for Yukawa terms with an even permutation
(i,j,k)
of
(1,2,3)
,
\lambda/\sqrt{q_{i}q_{k}}
for the odd permutations, and
\lambda^{2}q_{i}^{2}
,
\lambda^{2}/q_{i}^{2}
and
\lambda^{2}
for the quartic scalar interactions.
In the limit (
5.22
), only the even-permutation Yukawa terms and some quartic terms do not go to zero.
Additionally, we see that the gauge fields
A_{\mu}
and the gaugino
\chi
decouple from the theory, since all their interaction terms with the scalars and the remaining fermions vanish.
Hence, we neglect their contributions in the limit (
5.22
), which are their kinetic terms and interactions among themselves.
Finally, we are left with the double-scaled
\gamma
-deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM,

\begin{split}&S^{\chi}=\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}\left[-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\partial^{\mu}\phi_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi_{i}\right.\\
&+\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}}\left(\psi_{2}\phi_{3}\psi_{1}+\bar{\psi}_{2}\phi_{3}^{\dagger}\bar{\psi}_{1}\right)+\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\xi_{1}\xi_{3}}\left(\psi_{1}\phi_{2}\psi_{3}+\bar{\psi}_{1}\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\bar{\psi}_{3}\right)+\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\xi_{2}\xi_{3}}\left(\psi_{3}\phi_{1}\psi_{2}+\bar{\psi}_{3}\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\bar{\psi}_{2}\right)\\
&+\left.\xi_{1}^{2}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\phi_{3}^{\dagger}+\xi_{2}^{2}\phi_{3}\phi_{1}\phi_{3}^{\dagger}\phi_{1}^{\dagger}+\xi_{3}^{2}\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\right]\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.23)

This theory depends on the three parameters
\xi_{1}
,
\xi_{2}
and
\xi_{3}
.
It is known as
\chi
-CFT or dynamical fishnet theory, because the interaction vertices only allow for Feynman graphs, which resemble fishing nets.
Crucially, the double-scaling limit (
5.22
) renders the theory (
5.23
) non-Hermitian.
The reason is that the hermitian conjugated interaction terms vanished in the limit.
Commonly, when aiming for phenomenological applications, non-Hermitian theories yield negative-norm states, which spoil causality.
However, here, the motivation to study theories such as (
5.23
) is conceptual, since the simplified Lagrangian allows us to study the integrable structure in more detail
[
75
,
124
,
76
]
.
Yet, the many interaction terms and the many different fields make it very tedious to actually consider all the Feynman diagrams for an interesting quantity.
This motivates further simplifications of the dynamical fishnet theory (
5.23
).

The dynamical fishnet theory is the starting point for the construction of many theories which are obtained by tuning the
\xi_{i}
’s to particular values.
For example, we will show in section
6.2.1
that for the case
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=\xi_{3}
, the theory (
5.23
) is
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric.
We show therein that superspace techniques for Feynman diagrams help to grasp the many possible Feynman graphs produced by the theory (
5.23
).
For a general value of
\xi_{i}
’ s, the supersymmetry is broken.
Another special case, which simplifies the action even more, is the case where one
\xi_{i}
is set to zero.
Thereby, four of the Yukawa interactions and one quartic interaction drop out of the action (
5.23
) and we obtain the so-called
\chi_{0}
-CFT

\begin{split}S^{\chi_{0}}=\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}&\left[-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\partial^{\mu}\phi^{i}-\sum_{i=2}^{3}\mathrm{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi^{i}\right.\\
&\left.\penalty\ +\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\xi_{2}\xi_{3}}\left(\psi_{3}\phi_{1}\psi_{2}+\bar{\psi}_{3}\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\bar{\psi}_{2}\right)+\xi_{2}^{2}\phi_{3}\phi_{1}\phi_{3}^{\dagger}\phi_{1}^{\dagger}+\xi_{3}^{2}\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\right]\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(5.24)

where we set
\xi_{1}=0
in (
5.23
) and dropped the decoupled kinetic term of
\psi_{1}
.
We can continue the idea of turning off couplings by setting
\xi_{2}
to zero as well.
This gives a theory

S^{\mathrm{FN}}=\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}\left[-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial_{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\partial^{\mu}\phi^{i}+\xi^{2}\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\right]\penalty\ ,

(5.25)

of two complex scalars, which are coupled through a single quartic, non-Hermitian interaction.
Furthermore, we rename
\xi:=\xi_{3}
.
This is the bi-scalar fishnet theory
[
75
]
.

The Feynman rules can be read off the action (
5.25
).
We recall that the fields in the bi-scalar fishnet theory are in the adjoint representation of the former gauge group
\mathrm{SU}(\mathrm{N})
, that is, they are matrix-valued
(\phi_{i})^{a}_{b}=\phi_{i,A}(T^{A})^{a}_{b}
.
Hence, we can represent the Feynman diagrams as fatgraphs, which facilitates the determination of the order of a graph in
\mathrm{N}
by its Euler characteristic (
5.17
) in the planar limit
\mathrm{N}\rightarrow\infty
.
In the fatgraph formulation, each index of the field
a,b,...
is related to a
color line
.
This means that a propagator is blown up to two color lines and the trace-structure of interaction is reflected in the wiring of the color lines at a vertex.
The Feynman rules of (
5.25
) are

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{N}}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\penalty\ ,&\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{N}}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\penalty\ ,&\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{N}(4\pi)^{2}\xi^{2}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(5.26)

The momentum-space propagator in four Euclidean dimensions is derived before (
3.4
).
Whenever it is informative, we draw the
\phi_{1}
propagators in black and the
\phi_{2}
propagators in red.
Note that the vertex is
chiral
, not in a particle physics sense, but in the way it is oriented on a surface.
This is symbolized by the small black and red arrows next to the vertex in (
5.26
).
They should remind us that the Hermitian-conjugated interaction is missing such that we are only allowed to rotate the vertex for the construction of Feynman graphs, but we must not flip it.

5.1.2
Double-trace terms, renormalization and conformal fixed-points

One immediate question arises when quantizing the bi-scalar fishnet theory (
5.25
):
do quantum corrections, in the form of Feynman graphs with loops, generate a mass term for the scalars and does the coupling
\xi^{2}
get corrections?
In both cases, the answer is negative.
In the planar limit, it is not possible to draw Feynman graphs for correlation functions with a single-trace structure.
For example, one-loop bubbles would require the existence of the Hermitian conjugate counterpart of the chiral vertex in (
5.25
).
Therefore, the coupling
\xi^{2}
does not receive radiative corrections
[
89
]
.
The same argument also holds for diagrams, which would yield mass corrections to the propagators in the planar limit.
To illustrate this, we show the non-existing diagrams

\begin{array}[]{cc}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,&\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(5.27)

where the light-red shading signals the non-existing Hermitian-conjugate vertex.
The left diagram would contribute to radiative corrections of the coupling
\xi^{2}
and the right one would generate a mass.

However, there exist diagrams which are in accordance with the Feynman rules (
5.26
) and which are divergent
[
77
]
.
Consider the diagrams

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,&\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,&\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,&\scalebox{0.6}[0.6]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(5.28)

They are one-loop diagrams and proportional to
\xi^{4}
.
For the sake of simplicity, we do neither show the color line directions, nor the chirality indicators in the last two diagrams.
Following the color lines in each of them, we observe that a pair of them is connecting only two external points with each other.
Hence, for the sake of renormalization, we have to add double-trace counter terms to the action of the bi-scalar fishnet theory (
5.25
).
They are, corresponding to the diagrams (
5.28
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dt}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ +\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{2}\penalty\ \mathrm{tr}\left(\phi_{1}\phi_{1}\right)\mathrm{tr}\left(\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\right)\penalty\ +\penalty\ \tilde{\alpha}_{1}^{2}\penalty\ \mathrm{tr}\left(\phi_{2}\phi_{2}\right)\mathrm{tr}\left(\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\right)

(5.29a)

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}^{2}\penalty\ \mathrm{tr}\left(\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\right)\mathrm{tr}\left(\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\right)\penalty\ -\penalty\ \alpha_{3}^{2}\penalty\ \mathrm{tr}\left(\phi_{1}\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\right)\mathrm{tr}\left(\phi_{2}\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\right)\penalty\ .

(5.29b)

The double-trace couplings
\alpha_{1}^{2}
,
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}^{2}
,
\alpha_{2}^{2}
and
\alpha_{3}^{2}
are counter terms, which are running.
Double-trace counter terms are already present in the
\gamma
-deformation (
5.20
) and consequently also in the bi-scalar fishnet theory
[
77
,
68
]

To be able to draw all the contributing diagrams for the calculation of the beta-function of these couplings, we introduce the Feynman rules for the double-trace couplings,

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[0.7]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \mathrm{i}\cdot\left(4\pi\right)^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[0.7]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \mathrm{i}\cdot\left(4\pi\right)^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{1}^{2}\penalty\ ,

(5.30a)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[0.7]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \mathrm{i}\cdot\left(4\pi\right)^{2}\alpha_{2}^{2}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[0.7]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \mathrm{i}\cdot\left(4\pi\right)^{2}\alpha_{3}^{2}\penalty\ .

(5.30b)

The graphical representation should resemble joints, since within one of the two traces of the double-trace couplings one can permute the fields freely.

For the sake of conformal symmetry, the double-trace couplings have to be tuned to a value where their beta functions vanish.
This is the conformal fixed-point.
We start with the determination of the conformal fixed-points of the couplings (
5.29b
).
The divergences, which have to be renormalized by the couplings
\alpha_{2}^{2}
and
\alpha_{3}^{2}
, are a chain of bubble integrals.
In the case of
\alpha_{3}^{2}
, they look like

\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(5.31)

where we use the skeleton graph (i. e. the ordinary Feynman graph, in contrast to the fatgraph) for a concise graphic.
Each vertex could contribute the coupling
\xi^{2}
or the coupling
\alpha_{3}^{2}
.
In momentum space, the diagrams of type (
5.31
) are proportional to the integral of the four-dimensional scalar massless bubble, raised to the power of the number of loops.
This is the length of the diagram (
5.31
).
Furthermore, a momentum-space bubble diagram is proportional to the scalar chain relation in (
3.8
).
In dimensional regularization with
D=4-2\varepsilon
, a bubble diagram is therefore proportional to
r_{0}(2\varepsilon,2-2\varepsilon,2-2\varepsilon)
.
The divergent part of the factor is
\Gamma(2\varepsilon)
in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
and for a
L
-loop diagram, which is the product of
L
bubbles, it is
\Gamma(2\varepsilon)^{L}
.
Therefore, we only have to do the combinatorial sum over the
\xi^{2}
and
\alpha_{3}^{2}
couplings.
Hence, a
L
-loop diagram of the form (
5.31
) is proportional to
\Gamma(\varepsilon)^{L}(\xi^{2}-\alpha_{3}^{2})^{L+1}
.
This immediately implies that no correction is generated if
\xi^{2}-\alpha_{3}^{2}=0
.
Hence, the all-loop conformal fixed-point is
\alpha_{3,*}^{2}=\xi^{2}
.
The same argument applies to the coupling
\alpha_{2}^{2}
, and the fixed-point is
\alpha_{2,*}^{2}=\xi^{2}
as well
[
78
]
.

The beta function for the couplings (
5.29a
) is shown in the example of
\alpha_{1}^{2}
in appendix
B.1
up to three loops.
Unfortunately, the fixed-point is only known perturbatively and for weak coupling
\xi^{2}
it is the result (
B.42
).
We repeat it here,

\alpha_{1*}^{2}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \pm\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\xi^{2}-\dfrac{\xi^{4}}{2}\mp\dfrac{3\mathrm{i}}{4}\xi^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{8})\penalty\ .

(5.32)

An analog calculation yields the same fixed point for
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}^{2}
, with the above expansion.
In the literature, it is even determined up to seven loops
[
78
,
79
]
.

5.1.3
Exact all-loop anomalous dimensions and correlation function

Traditionally, integrability manifests itself in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM in the anomalous dimensions of single-trace operators, see e. g.
[
41
]
.
These quantities can be calculated by solving the spectral problem of spin chain Hamiltonians, which are constructed from perturbative data.
However, instead of in a perturbative Hamiltonian, the integrable properties of the theories come in the form of STRs (
3.21
) and (
3.40
), which are YBEs on the medial lattice.
Especially the chain relations (
3.8
) and (
3.42b
), the STR’s special case, is enabling the computation alongside the theory’s conformal symmetry.
Here, we present the exact computation of the anomalous dimension of the length-two single-trace operator
\mathcal{O}=\mathrm{tr}(\phi_{1}\phi_{1})
and its spinning generalization, where derivatives may act on the fields.
Furthermore, we compute the exact four-point function, where these operators are exchanged in the operator product expansion (OPE)
[
83
]
.

Instead of calculating the two-point function perturbatively, which is done to three loops in appendix
B.2
, we follow the strategy of
[
78
,
100
,
83
,
76
]
.
It consists of considering the four-point function, where the operator
\mathcal{O}=\mathrm{tr}(\phi_{1}\phi_{1})
is exchanged in the OPE expansion.
It is crucial that its Feynman diagrams admit a ladder structure in terms of a repetitive graph-building operator, such that one can sum up the perturbative expansion in a geometric series.
Next, the knowledge of a suitable eigenfunction is necessary to diagonalize the graph-building operators and to express the correlation function in a spectral integral representation.
It develops poles for each exchanged operator and we can read off the operators’ exact scaling dimension from their position.
In coordinate-space Feynman diagrams and in planar limit, the four-point correlation function has the perturbative expansion

\begin{split}\left\langle\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}(x_{1})\phi_{1}(x_{2})\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}^{\dagger}(x_{3})\phi_{1}^{\dagger}(x_{4})\right]\right\rangle=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}+\xi^{4}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
+\xi^{8}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}+\xi^{12}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
+\cdots+(x_{3}\leftrightarrow x_{4})+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{1}^{2})\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.33)

We see that its structure is very regular.
Increasing the loop order just adds a new, red, vertically running
\phi_{2}
-bubble to the diagram.
Of course, there are suppressed double-trace terms.
They ensure the finiteness and the conformal properties of (
5.33
), when tuned to the fixed-point (
5.32
), but they do not alter the poles of exchanged operators.
The graphs appearing in (
5.33
) are referred to as “zero-magnon” graphs
[
83
]
.
The name corresponds to closed vertical subgraphs made up of red
\phi_{2}
propagators, which run in circles and do not contract with the operators at the external points of the diagrams.
They are also called mirror-magnons
[
91
,
92
]
.

The perturbative expansion consists of the convolution of two different kinds of integral operators

\begin{aligned} \mathbb{H}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}

(5.34)

which we will also call graph-building operators.
We define the product
\circ
between two integral kernels as the convolution in two spacetime points, so that we can use the integral operators to build the diagrams in (
5.33
).
The correlation function can be resumed by usage of the geometric series and we have the expression

\begin{split}&\left\langle\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}(x_{1})\phi_{1}(x_{2})\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}^{\dagger}(x_{3})\phi_{1}^{\dagger}(x_{4})\right]\right\rangle\\
&=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\circ\left[1+\xi^{4}\mathbb{H}+\xi^{8}\mathbb{H}\circ\mathbb{H}+\xi^{12}\mathbb{H}\circ\mathbb{H}\circ\mathbb{H}+\cdots\right]\left[1+\mathbb{P}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{1}^{2})\\
&=x_{12}^{4}\mathbb{H}\circ\left[\frac{1+\mathbb{P}}{1-\xi^{4}\mathbb{H}}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{1}^{2})\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.35)

We observe that the permutation operator
\mathbb{P}
accounts for the terms in (
5.33
), where
x_{3}
and
x_{4}
are exchanged.
The excess propagators on the left can be expressed by the graph-building operator, which is multiplied by
x_{12}^{4}
to eliminate the vertical propagators.

Conformal triangles

In order to evaluate the expression (
5.35
), we take inspiration from quantum mechanics and insert a complete set of eigenvectors into (
5.35
) and diagonalize the operators.
In
[
147
,
163
,
164
]
such a two-point completeness relation was shown to exist and it reads

\delta^{(D)}\left(x_{13}\right)\cdot\delta^{(D)}\left(x_{24}\right)=\frac{1}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{D-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}}\mathrlap{\displaystyle\int_{\Delta,S}}\mathrlap{\textstyle\sum}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ \ket{\bar{\Omega}^{\Delta,S}_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{S}}{\scriptstyle(x_{1},x_{2};x_{0})}}\;\bra{\Omega_{\Delta,S}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{S}}{\scriptstyle(x_{3},x_{4};x_{0})}}

(5.36)

The integration/summation measure is defined as
1
1

1

In the following, we use the abbreviations
[
147
,
78
,
83
]

\displaystyle c_{1}(\Delta,S)

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{S+1}S!}{\pi^{-\frac{3D}{2}-1}}\frac{(\Delta-1)(D-\Delta)}{(\frac{D}{2}+S-1)^{2}-(\Delta-\frac{D}{2})^{2}}\frac{a_{0}(\Delta)a_{0}(D-\Delta)}{\Gamma(\frac{D}{2}+S)}

(5.37a)

\displaystyle c_{2}(\Delta,S)

\displaystyle=2\pi^{D+1}(-1)^{S}S!\frac{\Gamma(\Delta-\frac{D}{2})\Gamma(S+\Delta-1)\Gamma(\frac{D+S-\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}{2})\Gamma(\frac{D+S-\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}{2})}{\Gamma(\Delta-1)\Gamma(\frac{D}{2}+S)\Gamma(D+S-\Delta)\Gamma(\frac{S+\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}{2})\Gamma(\frac{S+\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}{2})}

(5.37b)

and the conformal blocks in four dimensions are defined as
[
78
,
83
,
76
]

\displaystyle g_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})=(-1)^{S}\frac{z\,z^{*}}{z-z^{*}}\left[h(\Delta+S,z)h(\Delta-S-2,z^{*})-h(\Delta+S,z^{*})h(\Delta-S-2,z)\right]

(5.38a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ h(t,z):=z^{\frac{t}{2}}\penalty\ _{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{t-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}{2},\frac{t+\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{4}}{2},t,z\right)\penalty\ ,

(5.38b)

and
{}_{2}F_{1}
denotes the hypergeometric function.
The four-dimensional cross-ratios
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are defined as

r_{1}=z\,z^{*}=\frac{x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2}}{x_{13}^{2}x_{24}^{2}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ r_{2}=(1-z)(1-z^{*})=\frac{x_{14}^{2}x_{23}^{2}}{x_{13}^{2}x_{24}^{2}}\penalty\ .

(5.39)

[
78
,
83
,
76
]

\mathrlap{\displaystyle\int_{\Delta,S}}\mathrlap{\textstyle\sum}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ =\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{S=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{S+1}\int_{\frac{D}{2}}^{\frac{D}{2}+\mathrm{i}\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\Delta}{c_{1}(\Delta,S)}

(5.40)

and it captures the integrating/summing over the Cartan labels of the conformal algebra in
D
dimensions.
The factor
a_{0}
is given in (
3.5
) and is a ratio of two gamma functions.
The eigenvectors are so-called
conformal triangles
that furnish a non-compact representation of the conformal group and they read

\Omega_{\Delta,S}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{S}}{\scriptstyle(x_{1},x_{2};x_{0})}=\frac{\prod_{i=0}^{S}\left[\frac{2x_{02}^{\mu_{i}}}{x_{02}^{2}}-\frac{2x_{01}^{\mu_{i}}}{x_{01}^{2}}\right]}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-(\Delta-S)}{2}}\left[x_{10}^{2}\right]^{\frac{\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+(\Delta-S)}{2}}\left[x_{20}^{2}\right]^{\frac{-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+(\Delta-S)}{2}}}

(5.41)

Their form is fixed by conformal symmetry and they are symmetric and traceless in the Lorentz indices
\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{S}
.
They equal the three-point function of the operators
\mathcal{O}_{1}
,
\mathcal{O}_{2}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,S}
and the completeness relation (
5.36
) describes the exchange of the operators
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,S}
.
In our case at hand, we have the scaling dimensions
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=1
for the operators
\mathcal{O}_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{2}=\phi_{1}
and the fishnet theory is in four dimensions,
D=4
.
Diagonalizing the operators (
5.34
) on these three-point functions is unnecessarily hard, and we simplify
\Omega_{\Delta,S}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{S}}{\scriptstyle(x_{1},x_{2};x_{0})}
by sending
x_{0}\rightarrow\infty
.
This gives the two-point function

\Omega_{\Delta,S}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{S}}{\scriptstyle(x_{1},x_{2};x_{0})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x_{0}\rightarrow\infty}}{{\sim}}\Psi_{\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta}{2},\frac{S}{2}}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{S}}(x_{1},x_{2}):=W_{u}^{\frac{S}{2},\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{S}}(x_{12})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,

(5.42)

which is a generalized higher-spin weight (
3.37
).

Furthermore, the conformal triangles can be expressed in terms of conformal blocks
g_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})

[
76
]
and in four dimensions the relation is exactly the spacetime integral in (
5.36
),

\begin{split}\int&\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ \ket{\bar{\Omega}^{\Delta,S}_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{S}}{\scriptstyle(x_{1},x_{2};x_{0})}}\;\bra{\Omega_{\Delta,S}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{S}}{\scriptstyle(x_{3},x_{4};x_{0})}}\\
&=\left(\frac{1}{x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}{2}}\left(\frac{x_{24}^{2}}{x_{13}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}{2}}\left[\frac{c_{1}(\Delta,S)}{c_{2}(\Delta,S)}g_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})+\frac{c_{1}(\Delta^{*},S)}{c_{2}(\Delta^{*},S)}g_{\Delta^{*},S}(r_{1},r_{2})\right]\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.43)

The abbreviations
c_{1}(\Delta,S)
,
c_{2}(\Delta,S)
and the conformal blocks are defined in (
5.37
) in the footnote.

Eigenvalues and correlation function

We can decompose the correlation function (
5.35
) into two parts

\left\langle\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}(x_{1})\phi_{1}(x_{2})\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}^{\dagger}(x_{3})\phi_{1}^{\dagger}(x_{4})\right]\right\rangle=G(x_{1},x_{2}|x_{3},x_{4})+G(x_{1},x_{2}|x_{3},x_{4})\circ\mathbb{P}

(5.44)

and focus on the calculation of
G(x_{1},x_{2}|x_{3},x_{4})
, since the second term is obtained by interchanging
x_{3}\leftrightarrow x_{4}
.
When we insert the completeness relation (
5.36
) into (
5.35
), we can replace the graph-building operator
\mathbb{H}
by its eigenvalue
\bra{\Omega}\circ\mathbb{H}=\bra{\Omega}\cdot E(\Delta,S)
.
Using (
5.43
), we obtain the integral representation
[
78
,
83
]

\begin{split}G(x_{1},x_{2}|x_{3},x_{4})=\frac{1}{x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{S=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{S+1}\int_{2-\mathrm{i}\infty}^{2+\mathrm{i}\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\Delta}{c_{2}(\Delta,S)}\frac{E(\Delta,S)}{1-\xi^{4}E(\Delta,S)}\;g_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.45)

The integral was completed to a infinite line into the imaginary direction, by anticipating the property
E(4-\Delta,S)=E(\Delta,S)
, which we show below.
The second line in (
5.45
) will be denoted by
\mathcal{G}(r_{1},r_{2})
and its integral can be evaluated by the residue theorem.
Because spurious poles are absent
[
83
]
, we find

\mathcal{G}(r_{1},r_{2})=\sum_{S,\Delta}C_{\Delta,S}\,g_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})=\sum_{S,\Delta}(-1)^{S+1}\pi\,\underset{\Delta}{\mathrm{Res}}\left[\frac{1}{c_{2}(\Delta,S)}\frac{E(\Delta,S)}{1-\xi^{4}E(\Delta,S)}\right]\,g_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})\penalty\ ,

(5.46)

which has the form of a conformal partial wave expansion and when we know the values
\Delta
of the poles, we can calculate the residues and read off the OPE coefficient
C_{\Delta,S}
.

We can calculate the eigenvalue of the operator
\mathbb{H}
, when acting on the simplified eigenfunction (
5.42
).
After merging the vertical propagators, the spinning chain relation (
3.42b
) is crucial and allows to derive the eigenvalue,

\begin{split}\bra{\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}}\circ\mathbb{H}=&\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=r_{\frac{S}{2}}(1,u+2,1-u)\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
=&r_{\frac{S}{2}}(1,u+2,1-u)\,r_{\frac{S}{2}}(1,u+1,2-u)\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
=&\penalty\ \mathrm{E}(u,\tfrac{S}{2})\cdot\bra{\Psi_{u,\tfrac{S}{2}}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.47)

Hence, the eigenvalue
\mathrm{E}(u)
is a combination of gamma functions, by the factor (
3.9
).
According to (
5.42
), we should use a specific value for
u
, which involves the scaling dimension of the exchanged operator
\Delta
and the dimensions of the external operators
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=1
.
We define the parameterized eigenvalue as
E(\Delta,S)=\mathrm{E}(\frac{2-\Delta}{2},\frac{S}{2})
and find that the gamma functions are reduced to a factor due to their functional relation
\Gamma(z+1)=z\cdot\Gamma(z)
.
Thus, the eigenvalue of
\mathbb{H}
when acting on the eigenfunction (
5.42
) is

E(\Delta,S)=\frac{16\pi^{4}}{(-\Delta+S+2)(-\Delta+S+4)(\Delta+S-2)(\Delta+S)}\penalty\ .

(5.48)

We can now solve the pole condition of (
5.46
), which is
1=\xi^{4}E(\Delta,S)
, for the position of the poles, which coincides with the scaling dimensions of the exchanged operators in (
5.45
).
They read

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}

\displaystyle=2\pm\sqrt{(S+1)^{2}+1-2\sqrt{(S+1)^{2}+4\pi^{4}\xi^{4}}}\penalty\ ,

(5.49a)

\displaystyle\Delta_{4}

\displaystyle=2\pm\sqrt{(S+1)^{2}+1+2\sqrt{(S+1)^{2}+4\pi^{4}\xi^{4}}}

(5.49b)

and correspond to operators, which have the classical scaling dimensions
\Delta_{2}\rightarrow 2
and
\Delta_{4}\rightarrow 4
when
\xi^{2}\rightarrow 0
.
The operators have the form
\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}\partial^{S}\phi_{1}\right]
and
\mathrm{tr}\left[\square\phi_{1}\partial^{S}\phi_{1}\right]+...
and the dots denote operators where the derivatives are unevenly distributed on the two fields.
The derivatives should be contracted with a symmetric traceless tensor.
In particular, we can obtain the exact scaling dimension of the length-two operator
\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}\phi_{1}\right]
by setting
S=0
and expanding in
\xi
.
After the rescaling
\xi\rightarrow\xi/\pi
, the seven-loop result is

\Delta=2-2i\xi^{2}+i\xi^{6}-\frac{7i\xi^{10}}{4}+\frac{33i\xi^{14}}{8}+\mathcal{O}\left(\xi^{18}\right)

(5.50)

and we obtain the three-loop result (
B.58
) of the perturbative calculation in appendix
B.2
.
In section
6.1.5
, we derive the scaling dimension of similar operators by the superspace generalization of the method presented here.

Finally, we determine the last missing piece for the exact correlation function (
5.35
), the residues in (
5.46
).
We obtain the OPE coefficient
[
83
]

C_{\Delta,S}=\frac{4^{3-\Delta}(S+1)}{(S^{2}+2S-2-\Delta^{2}+4\Delta)}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{S-\Delta+5}{2})\Gamma(\frac{S+\Delta}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{S-\Delta+4}{2})\Gamma(\frac{S+\Delta-1}{2})}\penalty\ ,

(5.51)

and we can obtain the perturbative expansion by expanding in the coupling, where
\Delta
is implicitly dependent on
\xi^{2}
by (
5.49
).
In
[
83
]
, other correlation functions and scaling dimensions of the bi-scalar fishnet theory are also calculated exactly in the presented way.

5.2
Integrable vacuum diagrams and the critical coupling

Remarkably, from the results of the previous section, we see that integrability, in the form of the chain relation (
3.8
) or its spinning generalization (
3.42b
), allows for exact determination of the scaling dimensions of single-trace operators.
However, so far, chain relations have only been used as a tool to enable these computations.
In this section, we conceptually follow another way: based on the integrable propagator weights from chapter
3
, we construct a partition function in the spirit of statistical physics.
Therein, a convenient choice of boundary conditions are doubly-periodic ones; the model lives on a torus.
We adapt these boundary conditions and find that the model of propagator weights describes an integrable generalization of vacuum Feynman diagrams.
One standard calculation, as presented in the case of the eight-vertex model in section
2.2.2
, is the derivation of the thermodynamic limit of the model’s partition function.
In our case of vacuum Feynman diagrams, the thermodynamic limit corresponds to the critical coupling of the theory
[
31
,
30
]
.
That is, the radius of divergence of the free energy of the theory which produces the vacuum graphs under examination when the sum of all vacuum graphs is considered as a function of the coupling.
The eminent question is which theories allow for the vacuum graphs describing such an integrable propagator model.
In the previous section (
5.1
), we present such a theory as a double-scaling limit of
\gamma
-deformed
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM, the bi-scalar fishnet theory.
But there exist also other theories, which might not even come from prominent examples of integrable QFTs.
As an instance, we will present the brick wall theory and a three-dimensional fishnet theory that contain fermions and we calculate their critical coupling
[
1
]
.

5.2.1
Integrable vacuum diagrams and the generalized partition function

The fundamental building block of an integrable lattice model is the R-matrix.
In the model made from generalized Feynman propagators, the R-matrix is (
3.47
).
We use it to construct a periodic, inhomogeneous transfer matrix, by intersecting a pair of scalar horizontal medial lines with
N
pairs of fermionic vertical ones.
On the level of the Feynman graphs, this yields a chain of rhombuses,

\begin{split}\mathbb{T}_{N}(p_{a},p_{b}|\{\mathbf{p}_{j}\})&=\mathrm{tr}\left[\mathbb{R}(p_{a},p_{b}|\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{p}_{2})\mathbb{R}(p_{a},p_{b}|\mathbf{p}_{3},\mathbf{p}_{4})\cdots\mathbb{R}(p_{a},p_{b}|\mathbf{p}_{2N-1},\mathbf{p}_{2N})\right]\\
&=\mathbb{R}^{x_{1}^{\prime}y_{2}}_{x_{1}y_{1}}(p_{a},p_{b}|\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{p}_{2})\mathbb{R}^{x_{2}^{\prime}y_{3}}_{x_{2}y_{2}}(p_{a},p_{b}|\mathbf{p}_{3},\mathbf{p}_{4})\cdots\mathbb{R}^{x_{N}^{\prime}y_{1}}_{x_{N}y_{N}}(p_{a},p_{b}|\mathbf{p}_{2N-1},\mathbf{p}_{2N})\\
&=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.52)

The spectral parameters for the vertical lines contain the spin label.
In the second line, we explicitly show the spacetime arguments of the
N
R-matrices.
We used the integration convention that we have to integrate over spacetime coordinates, which appear upstairs and downstairs.
Here, the integrated coordinates are
y_{1},...,y_{N}
.
For the spectral parameters, we have
\mathbf{p}_{j}=(p_{j},\ell_{j})
for
j
odd and
\mathbf{p}_{j}=(p_{j},m_{j})
for
j
even.
The periodicity in (
5.52
) due to the trace is indicated by the left and right boundary and the double-arrows indicate that they should be identified.
Respectively, the integration point on the left and right boundary,
y_{1}
, is the same and also the generalized Feynman graph is periodically identified.
The transfer matrix commutes with another transfer matrix for different values of
p_{a}
and
p_{b}
and we have

\mathbb{T}_{N}(p_{a},p_{b}|\{\mathbf{p}_{j}\})\circ\mathbb{T}_{N}(q_{a},q_{b}|\{\mathbf{p}_{j}\})=\mathbb{T}_{N}(q_{a},q_{b}|\{\mathbf{p}_{j}\})\circ\mathbb{T}_{N}(p_{a},p_{b}|\{\mathbf{p}_{j}\})\penalty\ .

(5.53)

The product
\circ
means that we stack the two factors on top of each other and convolute them by integrating over their common spacetime points.
The proof is carried out easily on the medial lattice, we just have to repeat the train track argument from (
2.18
) with pairs of medial lines.
The fermionic version of unitarity (
3.31
) and the
\mathbb{YBE}
(
3.28
) are sufficient to do so.
As usual, the commutativity of the transfer matrices implies the existence of conserved charges, which are the coefficients in an expansion of the transfer matrix in the spectral parameters.
When we switch to the Feynman graph picture, we can read off the propagator exponents from (
5.52
) via (
3.39
).
We drop the shading and the medial lines and find the transfer matrix as a generalized Feynman graph,

\mathbb{T}_{N}(p_{a},p_{b}|\{\mathbf{p}_{j}\})=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(5.54)

In order to build a toroidal partition function, we stack
M
transfer matrices on top of each other, using the product
\circ
and eventually convolute the top and bottom of this stack.
The last operation can be understood as a trace, since the stack of transfer matrices has two “indices”, the top and bottom row of external spacetime points with
N
external vertices each.
Contracting the indices by convolution means to take the trace, and we obtain the toroidal partition function

Z_{MN}(\{q_{i}\}_{i=1,...,2M}|\{\mathbf{p}_{j}\}_{j=1,...,2N})=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\prod_{I=1}^{M}\mathbb{T}_{N}(q_{2I-1},q_{2I}|\{\mathbf{p}_{j}\})\right]\penalty\ .

(5.55)

We called the spectral parameters of the horizontal lines
q_{i}
and the spectral parameters of the vertical ones
p_{j}
.
Thus, the spectral parameters are so far inhomogeneous in both directions.
We restrict ourselves to the vertically homogeneous case, which is
q_{2I-1}\equiv p_{a}
and
q_{2I}\equiv p_{b}
for all
I=1,..,M
.
Furthermore, we complete the transition to the Feynman graph formulation, by changing the parametrization in terms of the spectral parameters of the medial lines, to a parametrization in terms of the values of the exponent of the propagators in the transfer matrix (
5.54
).
We define

U_{J}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{u}^{+}_{J}&\mathbf{v}^{+}_{J}\\
\mathbf{u}^{-}_{J}&\mathbf{v}^{-}_{J}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}(u^{+}_{J},\ell_{J})&(v^{+}_{J},\bar{m}_{J})\\
(u^{-}_{J},\bar{\ell}_{J})&(v^{-}_{J},m_{J})\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}(p_{(2J-1)a},\ell_{J})&(\bar{p}_{(2J)a},\bar{m}_{J})\\
(\bar{p}_{(2J-1)b},\bar{\ell}_{J})&(p_{(2J)b},m_{J})\end{pmatrix}

(5.56)

for
J=1,...,N
and the matrix
U_{J}
contains all the information of the propagators in the R-matrix at site
J
.
We recall that the four spectral parameters at each R-matrix are not independent: the sum of them has to be equal to
D
, which is
u^{+}_{J}+u^{-}_{J}+v^{+}_{J}+v^{-}_{J}=D
, as we see from the parametrization of the medial lines (
5.54
).
Note that our convention to express the differences between spectral parameters of medial lines is e. g.
p_{(2J-1)a}=p_{2J-1}-p_{a}
and the bracket in the subscript helps to specify the first term.
The transfer matrix (
5.54
) in this parametrization is

\mathbb{T}_{N}\left(\left\{U_{J}\right\}\right)=\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(5.57)

From the transfer matrix (
5.57
), we can build the vertically homogeneous, but horizontally inhomogeneous partition function

Z_{MN}\left(\left\{U_{J}\right\}\right)=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{N}\left(\left\{U_{J}\right\}\right)^{M}\right]\penalty\ .

(5.58)

The partition function
Z_{MN}
consists of vacuum Feynman graphs with generalized propagator exponents, which possess a toroidal topology.
Therefore we call them generalized vacuum graphs.
By keeping the spectral parameters in form of the parameter matrix (
5.56
) generic, we are able to employ the toolbox of integrability.
Specifically we will calculate the thermodynamic limit of (
5.58
) in section
5.2.2
.
A critical comment is in place: vacuum diagrams in field theory are generally proportional to the spacetime volume, and thus, the overall free energy is usually infrared divergent.
Since we are only interested in the free energy density, this divergence is best fixed by leaving one of the points in the vacuum graphs unintegrated, which fixes the zero mode.
By this regularization, our results for the thermodynamic limit will be well-defined.

However, we first want to understand which physical vacuum graphs of which QFTs are generalized by (
5.58
).
Therefore, we turn to the question of how to choose the values of
U_{J}
to construct graphs of physical theories.
First, we need to normalize the transfer matrix (
5.57
) because it is built from the propagator weights (
3.7
) and (
3.35
) instead of the physical propagators (
3.4
) and (
3.34
), respectively.
When specifying the exponents and spin labels of the generalized propagators in (
5.57
), we obtain one of the three different physical objects
2
2

2

Note that there are more choices than the four presented ones, if one considers theories with fields of higher spin.
.

•

Scalar propagator in
D
dimensions.
If we set the spin label
\ell_{i}
or
m_{i}
to zero, we obtain the scalar propagator weights
W_{u}(x)
from (
3.7
).
To obtain a scalar propagator in
D
dimensions,
G_{v}(x)
, we have to adjust for the factor that appears in (
3.4
) and is defined in (
3.6
).
Let us repeat their relation here,

G_{\frac{D}{2}-u}(x)=c_{0}(\bar{u})\;W_{u}(x)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ c_{\ell}(v)=-(-1)^{v}2^{\frac{D}{2}-2v}a_{\ell}(v)\penalty\ .

(5.59)

Furthermore, to find the scalar propagator of a local QFT in
D
dimensions, we have to tune the exponent to
u=\frac{D-2}{2}
.

•

Fermionic propagator in
D
dimensions.
Comparing the propagator weight
W_{u,\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(x)
from (
3.35
) to the fermionic propagator in
D
dimensions, we find a match up to the factor
c_{\ell}(v)
and a shift in the exponent.
We have

G_{\frac{D}{2}-u-\frac{1}{2},\alpha\dot{\beta}}(x)=c_{\frac{1}{2}}(\bar{u})\;W_{u,\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(x)

(5.60)

and the factor
c_{\ell}(u)
is defined in (
3.6
) and repeated in (
5.59
).
At the point
u=\frac{D-1}{2}
, we obtain the fermionic propagator of an undeformed
D
-dimensional QFT.

•

D
-dimensional delta function.
If we multiply the scalar generalized propagator (i. e.
\ell_{i}=0
or
m_{i}=0
) by
\pi^{-\frac{D}{2}}a_{0}(\varepsilon)
, tune the exponent to
u=\frac{D}{2}-\varepsilon
and take eventually the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
we obtain a delta function based on (
3.12
).
Within a vacuum graph, the delta function can increase the number of edges that are adjacent to a vertex.
It implies that graphs
