In the non-supersymmetric case, we encountered a similar set of eigenfunctions, the conformal triangles (
5.41
).
We assume the existence of a superspace generalization of their construction, which means that there exists a superconformal three-point function

\Omega_{\Delta,S,R}{\scriptstyle(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})}=\left\langle\mathrm{tr}\left[\mathcal{O}_{1}(z_{1})\mathcal{O}_{2}(z_{2})\right]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,S,R}(z_{0})\right\rangle\penalty\ ,

(6.27)

and the Cartan labels
\Delta
,
S
and
R
of the superconformal algebra in three dimensions.
The labels correspond to the scaling dimension, the spin and the R-charge of the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,S,R}
, respectively.
The functions
\Omega_{\Delta,S,R}
should form a complete basis in the vector space of a non-compact representation of the three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal group
\mathrm{OSP}(2|4)
.
Accordingly, we formally write the completeness relation of the eigenfunctions as

\mathbb{1}=\delta^{(7)}\left(z_{13}\right)\cdot\delta^{(7)}\left(z_{24}\right)=\mathrlap{\displaystyle\int_{\Delta,S}}\mathrlap{\textstyle\sum}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \int\mathrm{d}^{7}z_{0}\penalty\ \ket{\bar{\Omega}_{\Delta,S,R}{\scriptstyle(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})}}\;\bra{\Omega_{\Delta,S,R}{\scriptstyle(z_{3},z_{4};z_{0})}}

(6.28)

and we can insert the resolution of unity into supergraphs like (
6.26
) and replace the graph-builders with their eigenvalues.
We introduce the shorthand for the chiral and anti-chiral superspace integration measure
\mathrm{d}^{5}z=\mathrm{d}^{3}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\penalty\ \bar{\theta}^{2}
and
\mathrm{d}^{5}\bar{z}=\mathrm{d}^{3}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\penalty\ \theta^{2}
, respectively, and
\mathrm{d}^{7}z=\mathrm{d}^{3}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}
for the full superspace measure for a concise notation.

For the zero-magnon case at hand, we require the superconformal triangle
\Omega_{\Delta,S,R}{\scriptstyle(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})}
to be the three-point function of
\mathcal{O}_{1}=\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}
,
\mathcal{O}_{2}=\Phi_{3}
, both with scaling dimension
\Delta_{\Phi}=\frac{1}{2}
, and the exchanged operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,S,0}
, which must have zero R-charge.
For this case, the superconformal triangles were found in
[
175
]
, and they read

\Omega_{\Delta,S,0}{\scriptstyle(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})}=\frac{C_{\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{3}\mathcal{O}}}{\left[x_{2\bar{1}}^{2}\right]^{\Delta_{\Phi}-\frac{\Delta}{2}+\frac{S}{2}}\left[x_{0\bar{1}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{\Delta}{2}+\frac{S}{2}}\left[x_{2\bar{0}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{\Delta}{2}+\frac{S}{2}}}\left[X_{3,-}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots X_{3,-}^{\mu_{S}}-\mathrm{traces}\right]\penalty\ .

(6.29)

Thereby,
X_{3,-}^{\mu}=X_{3}^{\mu}-\mathrm{i}\Theta_{3}\gamma^{\mu}\bar{\Theta}_{3}
contains the supersymmetric three-point structures
X_{3}^{\mu}
and
\Theta_{3}
, which are given in
[
175
]
.
The traces are all possible contractions of
X_{3,-}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots X_{3,-}^{\mu_{S}}
with the metric to render the superconformal triangle traceless and symmetric.
Furthermore, the conformal triangles admit an expansion in three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal blocks
\mathsf{g}_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})

[
175
]
,

\begin{split}\int&\mathrm{d}^{7}z_{0}\penalty\ \ket{\bar{\Omega}_{\Delta,S,0}{\scriptstyle(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})}}\;\bra{\Omega_{\Delta,S,0}{\scriptstyle(z_{3},z_{4};z_{0})}}\\
&=\left(\frac{1}{x_{1\bar{2}}^{2}x_{4\bar{3}}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left[\frac{\mathsf{c}_{1}(\Delta,S)}{\mathsf{c}_{2}(\Delta,S)}\mathsf{g}_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})+\frac{\mathsf{c}_{1}(\Delta^{*},S)}{\mathsf{c}_{2}(\Delta^{*},S)}\mathsf{g}_{\Delta^{*},S}(r_{1},r_{2})\right]\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(6.30)

which is the superspace analog
2
2

2

The superspace coefficients were found in
[
175
]
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}(\Delta,S)

\displaystyle=16\pi^{3}\frac{(-1)^{2S}(\Delta-S-1)^{2}(\Delta+S)}{(2\Delta-1)(2S+1)(-\Delta+S+1)}\;\mathrm{tan}(\pi\Delta)

(6.31a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}(\Delta,S)

\displaystyle=\pi^{3}(-1)^{S}2^{2\Delta-S+2}\frac{\Gamma(S+1)\Gamma(\Delta-\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(\frac{S-\Delta+3}{2})\Gamma(\frac{S+\Delta+1}{2})}{\Gamma(S+\frac{3}{2})\Gamma(\Delta)\Gamma(\frac{S-\Delta+2}{2})\Gamma(\frac{S+\Delta}{2})}\penalty\ ,

(6.31b)

and the superconformal blocks
\mathsf{g}_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})
may be found in
[
176
]
.
The cross-ratios
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
can be related to the four external points by using superconformal symmetry to set
x_{1}=(0,0,0)
,
x_{2}=(\frac{r_{1}+r_{2}}{2},\frac{r_{1}-r_{2}}{2\mathrm{i}},0)
,
x_{3}=(1,0,0)
and
x_{4}=\infty
for the bosonic coordinates and
\bar{\theta}_{1}=\theta_{2}=\theta_{3}=\bar{\theta}_{4}=0
for the fermionic ones.

of (
5.43
).

We can denote the eigenvalues of the zero-magnon graph-building kernels in (
6.26
) as

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{5}\bar{z}_{1}\mathrm{d}^{5}z_{2}\penalty\ \Omega_{\Delta,S,0}{\scriptstyle(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})}\left[\mathbb{H}\circ\bar{\mathbb{H}}\right]{\scriptstyle(z_{1},z_{2};z_{3},z_{4})}

\displaystyle=E_{0}(\Delta,S)^{2}\cdot\Omega_{\Delta,S,0}{\scriptstyle(z_{3},z_{4};z_{0})}\penalty\ ,

(6.32a)

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{5}\bar{z}_{1}\mathrm{d}^{5}z_{2}\penalty\ \Omega_{\Delta,S,0}{\scriptstyle(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})}\left[\mathbb{H}\circ\mathbb{P}\right]{\scriptstyle(z_{1},z_{2};z_{3},z_{4})}

\displaystyle=E_{0}(\Delta,S)\cdot\Omega_{\Delta,S,0}{\scriptstyle(z_{3},z_{4};z_{0})}\penalty\ .

(6.32b)

The subscript of
E_{0}(\Delta,S)
signals that it corresponds to the zero-magnon case.
In (
5.42
), we see that the computation of the eigenvalue can be facilitated by considering the limit
x_{0}\rightarrow\infty
and in the superconformal case at hand, we additionally set
\theta_{0},\bar{\theta}_{0}=0
, which can be inverted by superconformal transformations.
The asymptotic expression for the corresponding eigenfunction gives

\Omega_{\Delta,S,0}(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\begin{subarray}{c}x_{0}\rightarrow\infty\\
\theta_{0},\bar{\theta}_{0}=0\end{subarray}}}{{\sim}}\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\Delta}{2},\frac{S}{2}}(z_{1},z_{2}):=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ .

(6.33)

We notice that
\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}
has the form of a generalized spinning superpropagator (
3.69a
).
The calculation of the eigenvalue
E_{0}(\Delta,S)
on
\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\Delta}{2},\frac{S}{2}}(z_{1},z_{2})
of
\mathbb{H}\circ\bar{\mathbb{H}}
and
\mathbb{H}\circ\mathbb{P}
starts by making the following observation:
the generalized zero-magnon graph building operator
\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
, which corresponds to the generalized superfishnet theory
3
3

3

Turning to the non-locally deformed theory (
6.19
) is not necessary at this point, but we find it curious that the resulting eigenvalue admits only in the undeformed case the form of an inverse of an polynomial in
\Delta
, see (
6.38
).
(
6.19
), has a valuable action on the zero-R-charge eigenfunction from (
6.33
).
It is not an eigenfunction, however, after merging the vertical superpropagators we can use the spinning chain rules (
3.69
) of section
3.3.5
to obtain

\begin{split}&\bra{\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}}\circ\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=\scalebox{1.2}[1.2]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{1.2}[1.2]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{4}c_{0}(1+\omega_{i})\\
=&4\,r_{\frac{S}{2}}(\tfrac{1}{2}-\omega_{3},u+1-\omega_{2}-\omega_{4},\tfrac{1}{2}-u-\omega_{1})(-1)^{S}\cdot\scalebox{1.2}[1.2]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{4}c_{0}(1+\omega_{i})\\
=&4\,(-1)^{S}r_{\frac{S}{2}}(\tfrac{1}{2}-\omega_{3},u+1-\omega_{2}-\omega_{4},\tfrac{1}{2}-u-\omega_{1})r_{\frac{S}{2}}(\tfrac{1}{2}-\omega_{1},u+1+\omega_{1},\tfrac{3}{2}-u)\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{4}c_{0}(1+\omega_{i})\\
=&:\penalty\ \bra{\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}^{\dagger}}\cdot(-1)^{S}\,\mathrm{E}_{0,\boldsymbol{\omega}}(u,\tfrac{S}{2})\penalty\ .\end{split}

(6.34)

Here, the first diagram is equipped with the flavor indices of the superfields, whose propagators make up the generalized graph building operator
\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
.
We recall the restriction on the deformation parameters
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}+\omega_{4}=0
and the definition of the factor
c_{0}(v)
in (
3.6
).
The factor
\mathrm{E}_{0,\boldsymbol{\omega}}(u,\tfrac{S}{2})
is a combination of gamma functions involving the deformation parameters
\omega_{i}
and the label of the eigenfunction
u
.
After using their functional relation, we find

\mathrm{E}_{0,\boldsymbol{\omega}}(u,\tfrac{S}{2})=\pi^{3}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{S}{2}+u)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\omega_{2})\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\omega_{4})\Gamma(\frac{S}{2}-u+\omega_{2}+\omega_{4}+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{S}{2}-u+\frac{3}{2})\Gamma(\omega_{2}+1)\Gamma(\omega_{4}+1)\Gamma(\frac{S}{2}+u-\omega_{2}-\omega_{4}+1)}\penalty\ .

(6.35)

Note that
\mathrm{E}_{0,\boldsymbol{\omega}}(u,\tfrac{S}{2})
only depends on
\omega_{2}
and
\omega_{4}
.
From (
6.34
), and its hermitian conjugate

\bra{\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}^{\dagger}}\circ\bar{\mathbb{H}}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=\bra{\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}}\cdot(-1)^{S}\mathrm{E}_{0,\boldsymbol{\omega}}(u,\tfrac{S}{2})\penalty\ ,

(6.36)

we find that
\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\circ\bar{\mathbb{H}}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
has the eigenfunction
\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}
.
Furthermore, we observe that the permutation operator
\mathbb{P}
, which permutes start- and end-point, maps
\Psi_{u}
to its hermitian conjugate
\Psi_{u}^{\dagger}
times
(-1)^{S}
.
We can combine it with
\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
and find the eigenvalue equations

\displaystyle\bra{\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}}\circ\left(\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\circ\bar{\mathbb{H}}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\right)

\displaystyle=\bra{\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}}\cdot\mathrm{E}_{0,\boldsymbol{\omega}}(u,\tfrac{S}{2})^{2}\penalty\ ,

(6.37a)

\displaystyle\bra{\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}}\circ\left(\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\circ\mathbb{P}\right)

\displaystyle=\bra{\Psi_{u,\frac{S}{2}}}\cdot\mathrm{E}_{0,\boldsymbol{\omega}}(u,\tfrac{S}{2})\penalty\ .

(6.37b)

Setting the deformation parameters to zero,
\boldsymbol{\omega}=0
, and substituting
u=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\Delta}{2}
gives the zero-magnon eigenvalue of the superfishnet theory (
6.18
)

E_{0}(\Delta,S)=\mathrm{E}_{0,\boldsymbol{\omega}=0}(\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{\Delta}{2},\tfrac{S}{2})=\frac{4\pi^{4}}{(1+S-\Delta)(S+\Delta)}\penalty\ .

(6.38)

We observe that the eigenvalue is invariant under a transformation
E_{0}(1-\Delta,S)=E_{0}(\Delta,S)
.
Now we can return to the four-point function (
6.26
) and insert unity in the form of the completeness relation (
6.28
).
Then the action of
\mathbb{H}\circ\bar{\mathbb{H}}
and
\mathbb{H}\circ\mathbb{P}
on the eigenfunctions will give the eigenvalues by (
6.32
).
The decomposition into superconformal blocks (
6.30
) gives a similar spectral decomposition as in
[
175
]
and we obtain the superspace analog of (
5.45
)

\begin{split}&\left\langle\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}(z_{1})\Phi_{3}^{\dagger}(z_{2})\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}(z_{3})\Phi_{3}(z_{4})\right]\right\rangle\\
&=\sum_{S=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{S+1}\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\infty}\frac{\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}\Delta}{2\pi\,\mathsf{c}_{1}(\Delta,S)}\frac{E_{0}(\Delta,S)}{1-\xi^{2}E_{0}(\Delta,S)}\int\mathrm{d}^{7}z_{0}\penalty\ \ket{\bar{\Omega}_{\Delta,S,0}{\scriptstyle(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})}}\;\bra{\Omega_{\Delta,S,0}{\scriptstyle(z_{3},z_{4};z_{0})}}\\
&=\left(\frac{1}{x_{1\bar{2}}^{2}x_{4\bar{3}}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{S,\Delta}(-1)^{S}\,\underset{\Delta}{\mathrm{Res}}\left[\frac{1}{\mathsf{c}_{2}(\Delta,S)}\frac{E_{0}(\Delta,S)}{1-\xi^{2}E_{0}(\Delta,S)}\right]\,\mathsf{g}_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})\\
&=\left(\frac{1}{x_{1\bar{2}}^{2}x_{4\bar{3}}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{S,\Delta}\mathsf{C}_{\Delta,S}\,\mathsf{g}_{\Delta,S}(r_{1},r_{2})\penalty\ .\end{split}

(6.39)

Here, we use
E_{0}(1-\Delta,S)=E_{0}(\Delta,S)
to extend the contour integral to the infinite line in the imaginary direction.
Using the residue theorem in combination with the assumption that spurious poles are absent gives an expression for the OPE coefficients
\mathsf{C}_{\Delta,S}
.
Therefore, we have to determine the positions of the poles in the
\Delta
-integrant, which boils down to solve
1=\xi^{2}E_{0}(\Delta,S)
for
\Delta
.
Additionally, this signals the exchange of the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,S,0}
and we obtain their exact scaling dimensions

\Delta=1+\frac{1}{2}\left(-1\pm 2\sqrt{(S+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}-4\pi^{4}\xi^{2}}\right)\penalty\ .

(6.40)

The upper expression is the scaling dimension of the operators
\mathrm{tr}[\Phi_{1}\partial^{S}\Phi_{3}^{\dagger}]
with classical dimension
\Delta\rightarrow\Delta_{0}=1+S
in the limit
\xi\rightarrow 0
.
It is remarkable to find a closed form of the scaling dimension at any value of the coupling.
In view of the AdS/CFT-correspondence, the result (
6.40
) bridges between the weak-coupling expansion of the double-scaled
\beta
-deformation of ABJM theory and the strong-coupling expansion of string masses.
We observe that the form is a single square-root in contrast to the double-square-root of the bi-scalar fishnet theory (
5.49
).

In particular, we obtain the exact scaling dimension of the operator
\mathrm{tr}[\Phi_{1}\Phi_{3}^{\dagger}]
by the solution corresponding to
\Delta_{0}=1
with
S=0
, which is
[
3
]

\Delta|_{S=0}=1+\gamma=1+\frac{1}{2}\left(-1+\sqrt{1+16\pi^{4}\xi^{2}}\right)\penalty\ .

(6.41)

Therefore, we can confirm the result for the dispersion relation of the length-2 operator, which was obtained by Bethe ansatz in
[
124
]
and by re-summation of ladder diagrams in
[
177
]
for the undeformed ABJM theory.

Finally, we are able to compute the residues and eventually the OPE coefficient from (
6.39
).
We find the new result

\mathsf{C}_{\Delta,S}=-2^{S-1-2\Delta}\pi\frac{\Gamma(S+\frac{3}{2})\Gamma(\Delta)\Gamma(\frac{S-\Delta+2}{2})\Gamma(\frac{S+\Delta}{2})}{\Gamma(S+1)\Gamma(\Delta+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(\frac{S-\Delta+3}{2})\Gamma(\frac{S+\Delta+1}{2})}\penalty\ ,

(6.42)

which is exact and yields the all-loop result for the correlation function by (
6.39
).
Expanding in
\xi
, where the dependence is hidden in
\Delta
by (
6.40
), gives its perturbative corrections.

Two-Magnon case

After the successful diagonalization of the graph-builders appearing in the zero-magnon case in section
6.1.5
, we consider the supergraph expansion of another correlation function, which is

\begin{split}&\left\langle\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{2}(z_{1})\Phi_{1}(z_{1})\Phi_{2}(z_{2})\Phi_{1}(z_{2})\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}(z_{3})\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}(z_{3})\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}(z_{4})\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}(z_{4})\right]\right\rangle\\
=&\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}+\xi^{4}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}+\xi^{8}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
&+\cdots+(z_{3}\leftrightarrow z_{4})\penalty\ .\end{split}

(6.43)

We call the study of this correlation function the two-magnon case because there are two strands of propagators composed by alternating flavor-two and flavor-four superpropagators.
They wind around the cylindrical supergraphs, but unlike in the zero-magnon case (
6.24
), they are not closed, but rather start and end in the external operators of the correlation function.
At this point, one might wonder if there is a one-magnon case, much like in bi-scalar fishnet theory
[
83
]
.
However, the alternating chirality of the vertices in the horizontal direction rules out the corresponding supergraphs.
Furthermore, we could have considered the correlation function with flavors
(\mathrm{1},\mathrm{2})\leftrightarrow(\mathrm{3},\mathrm{4})
exchanged, which would give an equivalent derivation of the scaling dimensions.

In (
6.43
), we can identify the graph-builders

\begin{aligned} \mathbb{H}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \bar{\mathbb{H}}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\begin{aligned} \hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\mathbb{P}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}

(6.44)

and rewrite the correlation function (
6.43
) as

\left\langle\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{2}(z_{1})\Phi_{1}(z_{1})\Phi_{2}(z_{2})\Phi_{1}(z_{2})\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}(z_{3})\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}(z_{3})\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}(z_{4})\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}(z_{4})\right]\right\rangle=\frac{\mathbb{H}\circ(1+\mathbb{P})}{1-\xi^{4}\mathbb{H}\circ\bar{\mathbb{H}}}\penalty\ .

(6.45)

Unfortunately, we are unaware of the precise form of the conformal triangle (
6.27
) for the operators
\mathcal{O}_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{2}=\Phi_{4}\Phi_{3}
.
However, since both operators are charged under the R-symmetry (
6.8
) with charge
R_{1}=R_{2}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}=1
, the superconformal triangle corresponds to an operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,S,R}
with non-trivial R-charge
R=-2
.
We want to employ the same trick as in the zero-magnon case (
6.33
) and amputate the superspace point
z_{0}
where the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,S,R}
sits.
Therefore, we again consider the limit
x_{0}\rightarrow\infty
and set the fermionic coordinates to zero
\theta_{0},\bar{\theta}_{0}=0
.
The two-point function that we should obtain will carry R-charge
-2
, hence, regarding (
3.58
), we assume the eigenfunction to have the form

\Omega_{\Delta,0,-2}(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\begin{subarray}{c}x_{0}\rightarrow\infty\\
\theta_{0},\bar{\theta}_{0}=0\end{subarray}}}{{\sim}}\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\Delta}{2}}(z_{1},z_{2}):=\frac{\theta_{12}^{2}}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\Delta}{2}}}=\penalty\ \scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(6.46)

for the spinless case
S=0
.
We may anticipate the form of the spinning eigenfunction, it has the form of the spinning auxiliary two-point function (
3.69b
).
However, we are unable to find the eigenvalue in the case
S>0
.
To find the exponent of
\Psi_{u}
, we had to use the more general relation for its scaling dimension
\Delta_{\Psi_{u}}=1-2u=\Delta-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+R_{1}+R_{2}
also considering the R-charges of the operators in
\Omega_{\Delta,0,R}(z_{1},z_{2};z_{0})
.

Next, diagonalization of the graph-builder
\mathbb{H}
on the eigenfunction (
6.46
) may be carried out by explicitly doing the superspace integration.
The eigenvalue of the two-magnon graph-builder can be determined by reducing the superspace integral to the three-dimensional bosonic kite integral
[
178
]
.
Since the kite integral is more complicated in the case of arbitrary propagator powers
[
179
]
, we proceed with the calculation in the case of the undeformed superfishnet theory (
6.18
).
Acting with the graph-builder
\mathbb{H}
of (
6.44
) on the eigenfunction, we find

\begin{split}\Psi_{u}\circ\mathbb{H}&=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=-\left[\int\mathrm{d}^{5}z_{0}\;\mathrm{d}^{5}z_{0^{\prime}}\penalty\ \frac{c_{0}(1)}{\left[x_{0\bar{1}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{c_{0}(1)}{\left[x_{0^{\prime}\bar{1}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\theta_{00^{\prime}}^{2}}{\left[x_{00^{\prime}}^{2}\right]^{u}}\frac{c_{0}(1)}{\left[x_{0\bar{2}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{c_{0}(1)}{\left[x_{0^{\prime}\bar{2}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right]_{\theta_{1,2}=0}\\
&=-c_{0}(1)^{4}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta_{0}\penalty\ \mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}\theta_{0}\gamma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{12}\partial_{1,\mu}}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}x_{0}\;\mathrm{d}^{3}x_{0^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{10}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{10^{\prime}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{00^{\prime}}^{2}\right]^{u}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{20}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{20^{\prime}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
&=c_{0}(1)^{4}\cdot\bar{\theta}_{12}^{2}\square_{1}\;\mathsf{kite}^{(3)}(x_{12}^{2},u)\penalty\ .\end{split}

(6.47)

Here, we have evaluated the Graßmann delta function
\theta_{00^{\prime}}^{2}
by the
x_{0^{\prime}}
-integration, which allows us to factor out the common
\theta_{0}
-dependent exponential with the differential operators.
The integral over bosonic space is the three-dimensional kite diagram, which can be determined as
\mathsf{kite}^{(3)}(x_{12}^{2},u)=\frac{I^{(3)}(u)}{\left[x^{2}\right]^{u-1}}

[
178
]
, with the function

I^{(3)}(u)=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}\left(\frac{3}{2}-u\right)\left(u-1\right)}\frac{1}{32\pi^{2}}\int_{1}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\penalty\ \frac{s^{\frac{1}{2}-u}+s^{-2+u}}{\sqrt{1+s}}\;\mathrm{log}\left[\frac{\sqrt{1+s}+1}{\sqrt{1+s}-1}\right]\penalty\ .

(6.48)

The integral representation can be expressed in terms of the hypergeometric
{}_{3}F_{2}
-function at unit argument; the corresponding expression for the eigenvalue is presented in (
6.51
).
The eigenvalue, corresponding to the eigenvalue equation
\Psi_{u}\circ\mathbb{H}=\mathrm{E}_{2}(u)\cdot\Psi_{u}^{\dagger}
, is obtained after performing the derivatives in (
6.47
) and reads

\mathrm{E}_{2}(u)=-4c_{0}(1)^{4}\cdot\left(u-1\right)\left(\tfrac{3}{2}-u\right)I^{(3)}(u)\penalty\ .

(6.49)

The calculation presented here works analogously for the conjugated graph-builder
\bar{\mathbb{H}}
with the same eigenvalue,
\Psi_{u}^{\dagger}\circ\bar{\mathbb{H}}=\mathrm{E}_{2}(u)\cdot\Psi_{u}
.
Hence, we obtain the eigenvalue equation

\Psi_{u}\circ\left(\mathbb{H}\circ\bar{\mathbb{H}}\right)=\mathrm{E}_{2}(u)^{2}\cdot\Psi_{u}

(6.50)

where the eigenvalue upon action on the eigenfunction is
E_{2}(\Delta)=\mathrm{E}_{2}(\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{\Delta}{2})
and from (
6.48
) and (
6.49
) we find

E_{2}(\Delta)=\frac{\csc(\pi(\frac{\Delta}{2}+1))\,\Gamma(\frac{\Delta}{2}+1)}{32\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\frac{\Delta}{2}+\frac{3}{2})}-\frac{\,{}_{3}F_{2}\left(1,1,\frac{\Delta}{2}+\frac{3}{2};\frac{\Delta}{2}+2,\frac{\Delta}{2}+\frac{5}{2};1\right)}{16\pi^{2}\left(\Delta+3\right)\left(\frac{\Delta}{2}+1\right)}\penalty\ .

(6.51)

We observe that the action of the graph-builder
\mathbb{P}
is trivial, since
\Psi_{u}
is symmetric under exchange of the external points,
\Psi_{u}\circ\mathbb{P}=\Psi_{u}
.
The condition of (
6.45
) for having a pole relates the coupling with the square of
E_{2}(\Delta)
and reads

1=\xi^{4}E_{2}(\Delta)^{2}\penalty\ .

(6.52)

In the weak-coupling limit, the function
E_{2}(\Delta)^{2}
has to have poles at the values of classical scaling dimensions
\Delta_{0}
of the exchanged operators.
We deduce them at even, positive integers, including zero,
\Delta_{0}\in 2\mathbb{N}_{0}
, see figure
6.1
.
Contrary to the zero-magnon case, we cannot invert the function
E_{2}(\Delta)^{-2}
to access the anomalous dimension of the exchanged operator
\Delta
analytically.
However, we can expand the function
E_{2}(\Delta)^{-2}
around the zeros
\Delta_{0}
and invert the series perturbatively, where we obtain two solutions for each branch, related by
\xi^{2}\rightarrow-\xi^{2}
.
The perturbative inversion can be performed to an arbitrary order in the coupling.
For the lowest three scaling dimensions, up to eight loops and after rescaling
\xi\rightarrow\pi\cdot\xi
we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta^{(2)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ &2\pm\frac{\xi^{2}}{12}-\frac{\xi^{4}}{576}\pm\frac{18\pi^{2}-97}{248832}\xi^{6}\\
&+\frac{3803-2268\zeta_{3}+18\pi^{2}(\log(4096)-19)}{35831808}\xi^{8}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{10})\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(6.53a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta^{(4)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ &4\pm\frac{4\xi^{2}}{15}-\frac{\xi^{4}}{7200}\pm\frac{28800\pi^{2}-191191}{777600000}\xi^{6}\\
&\frac{191678057-145152000\zeta_{3}+28800\pi^{2}(480\log(2)-421)}{5598720000000}\xi^{8}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{10})\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(6.53b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta^{(6)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ &6\pm\frac{2\xi^{2}}{35}-\frac{4\xi^{4}}{2940}\pm\frac{352800\pi^{2}-2244421}{15126300000}\xi^{6}\\
&+\frac{2972114029-2074464000\zeta_{3}+117600\pi^{2}(1680\log(2)-1801)}{148237740000000}\xi^{8}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{10}).\end{split}

(6.53c)

The scaling dimension
\Delta^{(2)}
corresponds to the exchanged operator
\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\right]
, which matches with the classical values of the charges
\Delta^{(2)}=2
and
R=-2
.
The other solutions with higher classical scaling dimensions correspond to insertions of bosonic space derivatives, like e. g.
\square\;\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\right]
.

Figure 6.1:
The plot shows the function
\xi^{4}=E_{2}(\Delta)^{-2}
for
\Delta\in[0,9]
. We observe that the zeros
\Delta_{0}
occur at
0
,
2
,
4
,
6
,
8
, … . Unfortunately, the approximations in (
6.53
) are of too low order to be accurate in the shown range of the coupling. Each operator’s scaling dimension will collide with the neighboring operators at a local maximum for increasing coupling. The critical value for the coupling increases with the classical dimension of the operators.

6.2
The super brick wall theory

The super brick wall theory is the double-scaled
\beta
-deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM theory.
The
\beta
-deformation is a special case of the
\gamma
-deformation, which is presented in section
5.1.1
, where all three deformation parameters have the same value, i. e.
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=\gamma_{3}=\beta
.
Therefore, the deformation preserves one supercharge and
\beta
-deformed SYM is
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric.
It implies that the deformed theory can be written as a superspace action like
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM and the latter does not have to be decomposed in the spacetime component action (
5.15
) prior to the deformation, as it was the case for the
\gamma
-deformation at generic
\gamma_{i}
.
Hence, the plan of this section is to start with the
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace formulation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM and to perform the
\beta
-deformation in a
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry covariant manner, which results in the superspace action of the super brick wall theory.
Nevertheless, we will expand the superspace action in components as well, since it yields some of the double-trace terms, which we had to include in the case of the bi-scalar fishnet theory by quantum corrections (
5.29b
), automatically.
Furthermore, we present a non-local deformation of the super brick wall theory.
In addition, we use superspace integral relations, such as the chain relations from section
3.3.2
, to reproduce the result for the length-two operator
\mathrm{tr}\left(\Phi_{1}^{2}\right)
from the literature on dynamical fishnet theory
[
76
]
.

We copy the
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace action of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM from (
5.1
),

\begin{split}S=&\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\sum_{i=1}^{3}\,\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-g\mathcal{V}}\Phi_{i}^{\dagger}\mathrm{e}^{g\mathcal{V}}\Phi_{i}\right)+\frac{1}{4g^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\;\mathrm{tr}\left(W^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}\right)\\
&+\mathrm{i}g\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\;\mathrm{tr}\left(\Phi_{1}[\Phi_{2},\Phi_{3}]\right)+\mathrm{i}g\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\mathrm{tr}\left(\Phi^{\dagger}_{1}[\Phi^{\dagger}_{2},\Phi^{\dagger}_{3}]\right)\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(6.54)

where we have rescaled coupling
g\rightarrow\sqrt{2}g
.
In the spirit of section
5.1.1
, we perform the
\beta
-deformation of the R-symmetry
\mathrm{SU}(4)
of (
6.54
) by replacing the ordinary products of fields with the star product
\Phi_{i}\star\Phi_{j}:=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\;\mathrm{det}\left(\boldsymbol{\gamma}|\mathbf{q}_{i}|\mathbf{q}_{j}\right)}\Phi_{i}\Phi_{j}
, c. f. (
5.18
) and (
5.19
), and setting the three deformation parameters to the same value
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\beta,\beta,\beta)

[
121
]
.
The
\mathbf{q}_{i}
are the charges of the chiral superfields
\Phi_{i}
under the Cartan elements of the R-symmetry
\mathrm{SU}(4)
.
They are
\mathbf{q}_{1}=\left(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}\right)
,
\mathbf{q}_{2}=\left(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}\right)
, and
\mathbf{q}_{3}=\left(-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)
for the three chiral superfields and zero for the gauge field
[
180
]
.
The superfields share the R-charges with their component fields, but note that in table
5.1
, we use a different R-symmetry basis for the scalar fields
\phi_{i}
.
This deformation leaves us with the superpotential of the
\beta
-deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
[
63
,
180
,
72
]

\mathrm{i}g\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\penalty\ \mathrm{tr}\left[q\;\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\Phi_{3}-q^{-1}\Phi_{1}\Phi_{3}\Phi_{2}\right]+\mathrm{h.c.}\penalty\ ,

(6.55)

where we introduced the abbreviation
q=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\beta}
.

Next, we perform the planar limit (
5.16
), by sending
g\rightarrow 0
and
\mathrm{N}\rightarrow\infty
, while keeping the ’t Hooft coupling
\lambda=g\sqrt{\mathrm{N}}
fixed.
After appropriate rescalings of the fields by
\sqrt{\mathrm{N}}
, as indicated above (
5.15
), superspace Feynman graphs in double-line notation with the smallest genus will dominate.
In a second step, we perform the double-scaling limit consisting of
\lambda\rightarrow 0
and
\beta\rightarrow-\mathrm{i}\infty\Rightarrow q\rightarrow\infty
, while the product
\xi:=\lambda\cdot q
remains finite.
After this operation the gauge fields decouple and two out of the four terms of the deformation (
6.55
) vanish.
We consider the obtained theory in the planar limit
\mathrm{N}\rightarrow\infty
, where the leading order is described by toroidal double-line Feynman graphs.
We obtain the concise action

\begin{split}S\penalty\ =\penalty\ &S_{\mathrm{kin}}+S_{\mathrm{int}}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{i}^{\dagger}\Phi_{i}\right]+\mathrm{i}\xi\cdot\bar{\theta}^{2}\,\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\Phi_{3}\right]+\mathrm{i}\xi\cdot\theta^{2}\,\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\Phi_{3}^{\dagger}\right]\right\}\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(6.56)

where the squares
\theta^{2}
and
\bar{\theta}^{2}
in general act as delta function in the fermionic coordinates, see appendix
D.1.2
.
Accordingly, we can read off the two Feynman supergraph vertices in Minkowski space

\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\sim-\mathrm{N}\,\xi\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\delta^{(2)}(\bar{\theta})\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\sim-\mathrm{N}\,\xi\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\delta^{(2)}(\theta)\penalty\ ,

(6.57)

after performing a Wick rotation.

6.2.1
Component action

Let us now make contact with the component field action of the
\mathcal{N}=1
double-scaled
\beta
-deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM, which is the
\chi
-CFT in (
5.23
) with the couplings identified, i. e.
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=\xi_{3}=\xi
.
We may expand (
6.56
) in Graßmann components.
The kinetic terms from the canonical Kähler potential are

S_{\mathrm{kin}}=\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{i}^{\dagger}\Phi_{i}\right]=\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\square\phi_{i}-\mathrm{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi_{i}+F_{i}^{\dagger}F_{i}\right]\penalty\ ,

(6.58)

while the interaction terms are

\begin{split}S_{\mathrm{int}}&=\mathrm{N}\cdot\mathrm{i}\xi\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\;\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\Phi_{3}\right]_{\bar{\theta}=0}+\mathrm{N}\cdot\mathrm{i}\xi\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi^{\dagger}_{1}\Phi^{\dagger}_{2}\Phi^{\dagger}_{3}\right]_{\theta=0}\\
&=\mathrm{N}\cdot\mathrm{i}\xi\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}\phi_{2}F_{3}+\phi_{1}F_{2}\phi_{3}+F_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}-\phi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}-\phi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{1}-\phi_{3}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\right]\\
&\phantom{=}+\mathrm{N}\cdot\mathrm{i}\xi\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi^{\dagger}_{1}\phi^{\dagger}_{2}F^{\dagger}_{3}+\phi^{\dagger}_{1}F^{\dagger}_{2}\phi^{\dagger}_{3}+F^{\dagger}_{1}\phi^{\dagger}_{2}\phi^{\dagger}_{3}-\phi^{\dagger}_{1}\bar{\psi}_{2}\bar{\psi}_{3}-\phi^{\dagger}_{2}\bar{\psi}_{3}\bar{\psi}_{1}-\phi^{\dagger}_{3}\bar{\psi}_{1}\bar{\psi}_{2}\right]\penalty\ .\end{split}

(6.59)

The auxiliary fields
F_{i}
and
F^{\dagger}_{i}
are not dynamical and we may eliminate them with the help of their equations of motion:

\begin{aligned} F_{1}^{A}=-\mathrm{i}\xi\,\phi^{*}_{2,B}\phi^{*}_{3,C}\cdot\mathrm{tr}\left[T^{A}T^{B}T^{C}\right]\penalty\ ,\\
F_{2}^{A}=-\mathrm{i}\xi\,\phi^{*}_{3,B}\phi^{*}_{1,C}\cdot\mathrm{tr}\left[T^{A}T^{B}T^{C}\right]\penalty\ ,\\
F_{3}^{A}=-\mathrm{i}\xi\,\phi^{*}_{1,B}\phi^{*}_{2,C}\cdot\mathrm{tr}\left[T^{A}T^{B}T^{C}\right]\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} F^{*,A}_{1}=-\mathrm{i}\xi\,\phi_{2,B}\phi_{3,C}\cdot\mathrm{tr}\left[T^{A}T^{B}T^{C}\right]\penalty\ ,\\
F^{*,A}_{2}=-\mathrm{i}\xi\,\phi_{3,B}\phi_{1,C}\cdot\mathrm{tr}\left[T^{A}T^{B}T^{C}\right]\penalty\ ,\\
F^{*,A}_{3}=-\mathrm{i}\xi\,\phi_{1,B}\phi_{2,C}\cdot\mathrm{tr}\left[T^{A}T^{B}T^{C}\right]\penalty\ .\end{aligned}

(6.60)

We observe that the r. h. s. do not have the form of commutators anymore, in comparison to the equations of motions from the undeformed action of
\mathcal{N}=4
in (
5.10
).
As shown in
[
68
]
, it implies that integrating out the auxiliary fields may produce double-trace terms by the completeness relation of the generators of the adjoint representation of the gauge group
\mathrm{SU}(\mathrm{N})
, as displayed in (
5.2
).
Thus, we obtain the on-shell component action

\begin{split}S=\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{tr}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{3}\left[\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\square\phi_{i}-\mathrm{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi_{i}\right]+\xi^{2}\left[\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi^{\dagger}_{1}\phi^{\dagger}_{2}+\phi_{3}\phi_{1}\phi^{\dagger}_{3}\phi^{\dagger}_{1}+\phi_{2}\phi_{3}\phi^{\dagger}_{2}\phi^{\dagger}_{3}\right]\right.\\
\left.-\mathrm{i}\xi\left[\phi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}+\phi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{1}+\phi_{3}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\right]-\mathrm{i}\xi\left[\phi^{\dagger}_{1}\bar{\psi}_{2}\bar{\psi}_{3}+\phi^{\dagger}_{2}\bar{\psi}_{3}\bar{\psi}_{1}+\phi^{\dagger}_{3}\bar{\psi}_{1}\bar{\psi}_{2}\right]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dt}}\right\}\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(6.61)

and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dt}}
describes the double-trace interaction terms, which survive in the double-scaling limit.
They have the form

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dt}}=-\frac{\xi^{2}}{\mathrm{N}}\left\{\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\right]+\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}\phi_{3}\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\phi_{3}^{\dagger}\right]+\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{2}\phi_{3}\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\phi_{3}^{\dagger}\right]\right\}\penalty\ .

(6.62)

It is satisfying to see that they do not need to be added due to radiative corrections to the single-trace component action, as seen on the example of the bi-scalar fishnet theory in (
5.29
); instead they naturally and directly emerge from the
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace formulation.
However, we also notice that not all double-trace terms appear from the superspace approach, but only the first one in (
5.29b
).
The double-trace terms are however not needed in our below analysis, since they do not appear in the thermodynamic limit of vacuum diagrams and the superconformal symmetry makes them redundant in the computation of exact scaling dimensions, similar to section
5.1.3
.
Furthermore, we stress that the single-trace action (
6.61
) is equivalent to the
\chi
-CFT
[
75
]
, when we identify all couplings at the same value
\xi
, after changing the convention
\xi\rightarrow-\xi
.
In
[
76
]
, it was shown that the couplings do not run at the supersymmetric point of the
\chi
-CFT and thus the super brick wall theory (
6.61
) with double-trace terms (
6.62
) is free of divergences.

6.2.2
Introducing a spectral parameter into the
\mathbf{\mathcal{N}=1}
action

The double-scaled
\chi
-CFTs are expected to inherit integrability from their
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM “parent theory”
[
75
]
, which should include the special case (
6.56
).
However, putting quantum integrability to good use always requires the introduction of a suitable spectral parameter.
In general, it is not easy to find it in an integrable, planar, conformal QFT.
This is certainly the case for
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM, where it somewhat mysteriously first appears when converting local composite operators into quantum spin chains.
Impressively, in the much simpler setting of the fishnet model, it has recently been shown in
[
100
]
that the correct spectral parameter may be directly introduced by deforming the model’s action, at the cost of giving up the locality of the resulting “QFT”.
Unfortunately, this construction has not yet been achieved for general
\chi
-CFTs.
We will now show that in the special case of the
\mathcal{N}=1

\chi
-CFT, a suitable deformation may nevertheless be found by deforming its
\mathcal{N}=1
action.
We propose

\begin{split}S_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ &S_{\mathrm{kin},\boldsymbol{\omega}}+S_{\mathrm{int},\boldsymbol{\omega}}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{i}^{\dagger}\square^{\omega_{i}}\Phi_{i}\right]+\mathrm{i}\xi\cdot\bar{\theta}^{2}\;\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\Phi_{3}\right]+\mathrm{i}\xi\cdot\theta^{2}\;\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\Phi_{3}^{\dagger}\right]\right\}\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(6.63)

where
\boldsymbol{\omega}
is a shorthand notation for the deformation parameters
\omega_{1}
,
\omega_{2}
and
\omega_{3}
.
It will be related to the model’s spectral parameters, see (
3.54
) below.
The mass dimension of the chiral superfield gets deformed to
\left[\Phi_{i}\right]=[\Phi^{\dagger}_{i}]=\frac{(D-2\mathcal{N})-2\omega_{i}}{2}|_{D=4,\mathcal{N}=1}=1-\omega_{i}
. For the interaction to be marginal, we require
3-(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3})=D-\mathcal{N}
, which is for
D=4
,
\mathcal{N}=1
equivalent to the relation
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}=0
.
One recovers the original theory (
6.56
) by setting all
\omega_{i}=0
.
The kinetic terms of the component fields are

\begin{split}S_{\mathrm{kin},\boldsymbol{\omega}}&=\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{i}^{\dagger}\square^{\omega_{i}}\Phi_{i}\right]\\
&=\mathrm{N}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\square^{1+\omega_{i}}\phi_{i}-\mathrm{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\square^{\omega_{i}}\partial_{\mu}\psi_{i}+F_{i}^{\dagger}\square^{\omega_{i}}F_{i}\right]\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(6.64)

and we use their non-local kinetic operators to construct the superpropagator according to the derivation in section
3.3.1
.

6.2.3
Exact all-loop anomalous dimension

The exact computation of the anomalous dimension via the diagonalization of graph-building operators using the super chain relations is also possible in one instance for the super brick wall theory.
The program already presented in sections
5.1.3
and
6.1.5
, where (super) conformal symmetry played a decisive role for the construction of the eigenfunctions, the (super) conformal triangles.
For the superconformal algebra
\mathfrak{sl}(4|1)
in four dimensions with
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry, some of the superconformal triangles are constructed in
[
181
,
182
,
183
]
.
However, the triangles with the R-charge we need in the example below are not know to us.
Thus, we are in the same situation as in the two-magnon case of the superfishnet theory, which is studied in section
6.1.5
.
There, the assumption of the existence of a complete basis of superconformal eigenfunctions of the graph-building operator admittedly seems ad-hoc.
Therefore, we provide here more evidence that the conjectured completeness relation and the resulting anomalous dimensions are correct, by reproducing a result from the literature
[
76
]
on the dynamical fishnet theory, or
\chi
-CFT, from the assumptions on R-charged superconformal triangles applied to the super brick wall theory (
6.56
).
Hence, we switch from the three-dimensional superconformal setup of the superfishnet theory to the four-dimensional superconformal brick wall theory
[
2
]
.
This theory is the double-scaled
\beta
-deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM, which is the supersymmetric case of the so-called dynamical fishnet theory
[
76
]
.

We use the conventions of
[
2
]
for four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace and consider the supergraph expansion of the correlator

\begin{split}&\left\langle\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}(z_{1})\Phi_{1}(z_{2})\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}(z_{3})\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}(z_{4})\right]\right\rangle\\
=&\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}+\xi^{4}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}+\xi^{8}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
&+\xi^{12}\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}+\cdots+\left(z_{3}\leftrightarrow z_{4}\right)\end{split}

(6.65)

in the large-
\mathrm{N}
limit.
The super brick wall theory’s chiral, non-unitary, cubic vertices allow only ladder-like, cylindrical supergraphs.
Note that in (
6.65
), the upper-left and lower-right vertex of each rung is drawn with the wrong flavor orientation.
This is for illustrational reasons only; to be more precise, the connecting superpropagator should close after winding around the compact direction of the cylinder and respect the super Feynman rules (
6.57
).

The graph-building operators in the diagrams (
6.65
) are

\begin{aligned} \mathbb{H}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}

(6.66)

and we will show that
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathbb{H}\circ\mathbb{P}
have the same eigenvalue on the eigenfunction of our interest.
With the graph-building operators, we can formally write the correlation function (
6.65
) as

\left\langle\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}(z_{1})\Phi_{1}(z_{2})\right]\mathrm{tr}\left[\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}(z_{3})\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}(z_{4})\right]\right\rangle=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\circ\left[\frac{\left(1+\mathbb{P}\right)}{1-\xi^{4}\mathbb{H}}\right]\penalty\ .

(6.67)

The eigenvalues of
\mathbb{H}
corresponding to the eigenfunction
\Psi_{u}=\tfrac{\bar{\theta}_{12}^{2}}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u}}
can be calculated similarly to (
6.47
), with the difference that we are in four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace and that we have to convolute the four-dimensional kite diagram
\mathsf{kite}^{(4)}(x^{2},u)=\frac{I^{(4)}(u)}{\left[x^{2}\right]^{u}}

[
83
]
with the two more superpropagators.
We observe that the permutation graph-builder
\mathbb{P}
has a trivial action on the eigenfunction
\Psi_{u}
since the eigenfunction is symmetric under the exchange of the external points.
Furthermore, the eigenfunction carries R-charge such that we expect the parameter
u
to be related to the scaling dimension of the eigenfunction, which is as in the two-magnon case given by
\Delta_{\Psi_{u}}=\Delta-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+R_{1}+R_{2}
.
Now the R-charge of
\mathcal{O}_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{2}=\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}
is
R_{1}=R_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}
, which implies
u=2-\frac{\Delta}{2}
.
For the graph-builder
\mathbb{H}
we find the following eigenvalue equation,

\begin{split}\Psi_{u}\circ\mathbb{H}\penalty\ &=\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}=-4u\left(1-u\right)I^{(4)}(u)\cdot\scalebox{1}[1]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\\
&=16\,u\left(1-u\right)\;I^{(4)}(u)\;r(2-u,u+1,1)\,r(2-u,u,1)\cdot\Psi_{u}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(6.68)

In the last step, we used the four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
versions of the super chain relations (
3.64
) and (
3.61
).
The eigenvalue corresponding to
\Psi_{u}
is therefore

\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{SBW}}_{0}(u)=16\,u\left(1-u\right)\cdot I^{(4)}(u)\cdot r(2-u,u+1,1)\,r(2-u,u,1)\penalty\ ,

(6.69)

where the factor from the kite diagram reads
[
83
]

I^{(4)}(u)=\frac{1}{2u-2}\left[\psi^{(1)}\left(\tfrac{u-1}{2}\right)-\psi^{(1)}\left(\tfrac{1-u}{2}\right)+\psi^{(1)}\left(\tfrac{2-u}{2}\right)-\psi^{(1)}\left(\tfrac{u}{2}\right)\right]\penalty\ ,

(6.70)

with the second derivative of the logarithm of the gamma function
\psi^{(1)}(z)=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}z^{2}}\mathrm{log}\,\Gamma(z)
.
We observe again that the action of
\mathbb{P}
on
\Psi_{u}
is trivial.
Using the functional relation of
\psi^{(1)}(z)
and rescaling the coupling of the super brick wall theory
\xi\rightarrow\frac{\xi}{2\pi}
, the pole condition of (
6.67
),
1=\xi^{4}\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{SBW}}_{0}(2-\tfrac{\Delta}{2})
, is equivalent to the result (4.28) in
[
76
]
of the dynamical fishnet theory (
5.23
) with
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=\xi_{3}=\xi
, specified to the supersymmetric, spinless case
\omega,S=0
and
\Delta=2+2\mathrm{i}\nu
therein.

6.3
Vacuum graphs in the thermodynamic limit

Figure 6.2:
The toroidal super vacuum graphs exemplify a contribution to the free energy of the superfishnet theory (
6.18
) (left) and to the super brick wall theory (
6.56
) (right).
One notices that the regular fishnet- (left) and brick wall pattern (right).
In the planar limit
\mathrm{N}\rightarrow\infty
, the leading order results in such toroidal diagrams, with arbitrary height and length.
The doubly periodic boundary conditions of the torus are implemented into the figure:
top and bottom lines are identified, and so are the left and right boundaries.

We turn to the study of super vacuum diagrams of the superfishnet (
6.18
) and super brick wall theory (
6.56
) and their non-local deformations (
6.19
) and (
6.63
), respectively.
Examples of a vacuum supergraphs in the planar limit are shown in fig.
6.2
.
The regular pattern is due to the highly constraining Feynman rules, notably, the interaction vertices (
6.23
) and (
6.57
).
The vertices are chiral and demand a very particular order of the flavor of the superfields.
Diagrammatically, the non-local deformations (
6.19
) and (
6.63
) reproduce the same graphs as in fig.
6.2
because the superpotential is the same as in the undeformed theories.
However, the propagator weights differ by their exponent and the propagators have to be multiplied by the prefactor
c_{0}(1+\omega_{i})
as indicated in (
3.54
).
Our goal is to repeat the analysis of section
5.2
for super vacuum graphs and obtain their thermodynamic limit and the critical couplings of the supersymmetric theories.

In the non-supersymmetric case, we argued in section
5.2.1
that vacuum diagrams are infrared divergent because they scale proportional to the volume of spacetime.
Similarly, superspace vacuum graphs are proportional to the a priori ill-defined
\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\cdot\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}=\infty\cdot 0
.
(The zero stemming from the fermionic integration is just a manifestation of the well-known statement that the vacuum energy of supersymmetric field theories is zero.)
In our supersymmetric models at hand, we proceed in the same way as in the non-supersymmetric case and leave one of the superspace points in the vacuum supergraphs un-integrated in order to obtain a well-defined density.

Starting from the super weight (
3.55
), a superpropagator without the factor
c_{0}(1+\omega_{i})
, we can identify
4
4

4

Note that we could have chosen another row matrix kernel with the chiralities interchanged, i. e. with anti-chiral (green) external vertices and chiral (red) internal ones.

a generalized row-matrix

T_{N}(U)=\scalebox{0.7}[0.7]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(6.71)

It is building up the vacuum diagrams in fig.
6.2
after e. g. fixing the parameter matrix, c. f. (
5.56
) to
U^{\mathrm{SFN}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{+}&v_{+}\\
u_{-}&v_{-}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-\omega_{1}&\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-\omega_{4}\\
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-\omega_{2}&\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-\omega_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for the non-locally deformed superfishnet theory and
U^{\mathrm{SBW}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{+}&v_{+}\\
u_{-}&v_{-}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1-\omega_{1}\\
1-\omega_{3}&1-\omega_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for the deformed super brick wall theory.
In the limit
\omega_{i}\rightarrow 0
, we find the vacuum graphs of the undeformed theories.
In contrast to the non-supersymmetric case in section
5.2.1
, we only consider homogeneous row-matrices here.
Due to the lack of a superspace STR, see the discussion in section
3.3.4
, the row-matrices cannot be shown to commute, and this is why we do not call them super transfer matrices, but row-matrices.

Formally, we may write a generalized
M\times N
toroidal vacuum supergraph as

Z_{MN}(\mathbf{u})=\mathrm{tr}\left[T_{N}(\mathbf{u})^{M}\right],

(6.72)

which graphically represents
M
generalized row matrices of length
N
stacked on top of each other and identified periodically by the trace.
However, the normalization factors
c_{0}(1+\omega_{i})
are not included in (
6.72
) and we will have to include them to describe physical superspace vacuum diagrams.
To reinstate them, we consider a normalized row matrix
5
5

5

Remember that
\sum_{i}\omega_{i}=0
, where we have four
\omega_{i}
’s in the deformed superfishnet theory and three in the deformed super brick wall theory.

, indicated by the hat,

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\hat{T}_{N}^{\mathrm{SFN}}=\left[\prod_{i=1}^{4}c_{0}(1+\omega_{i})\right]^{N}T_{N}(U^{\mathrm{SFN}})&\mathrm{and}&\hat{T}_{N}^{\mathrm{SBW}}=\left[\prod_{i=1}^{3}c_{0}(1+\omega_{i})\right]^{N}T_{N}(U^{\mathrm{SBW}})\penalty\ .\end{array}

(6.73)

Thus, we obtain the physical vacuum supergraphs of the supersymmetric theories by staking up the row-matrices (
6.71
) and taking the trace.
We find
\hat{Z}_{MN}^{\mathrm{SFN}}=\mathrm{tr}(\hat{T}_{N}^{\mathrm{SFN}})^{M}
and
\hat{Z}_{MN}^{\mathrm{SBW}}=\mathrm{tr}(\hat{T}_{N}^{\mathrm{SBW}})^{M}
.

We aim to calculate the critical coupling
\xi_{\mathrm{cr}}
. It is defined as the radius of convergence of the expansion of the free energy of the superfishnet and super brick wall theory and their series are

\hat{Z}^{\mathrm{SFN}}=\sum_{M,N=1}^{\infty}\hat{Z}_{MN}^{\mathrm{SFN}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)^{2MN}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \hat{Z}^{\mathrm{SBW}}=\sum_{M,N=1}^{\infty}\hat{Z}_{MN}^{\mathrm{SBW}}(-\xi)^{2MN}\penalty\ .

(6.74)

The different signs in front of the coupling have their origin in the Feynman rules (
6.23
) and (
6.57
).
The critical couplings are the radii of convergence

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{cr}}^{\mathrm{SFN}}

\displaystyle=\left[\lim_{M,N\rightarrow\infty}\left|-\hat{Z}_{MN}^{\mathrm{SFN}}\right|^{\frac{1}{MN}}\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\left[\prod_{i=1}^{4}c_{0}(1+\omega_{i})\lim_{M,N\rightarrow\infty}\left|Z_{MN}(U^{\mathrm{SFN}})\right|^{\frac{1}{MN}}\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\penalty\ ,

(6.75a)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{cr}}^{\mathrm{SBW}}

\displaystyle=\left[\lim_{M,N\rightarrow\infty}\left|\hat{Z}_{MN}^{\mathrm{SBW}}\right|^{\frac{1}{MN}}\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\left[\prod_{i=1}^{3}c_{0}(1+\omega_{i})\lim_{M,N\rightarrow\infty}\left|Z_{MN}(U^{\mathrm{SBW}})\right|^{\frac{1}{MN}}\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\penalty\ .

(6.75b)

The only yet unknown quantity is the thermodynamic limit of the bare vacuum graphs, which are defined in (
6.72
).
Hence, we wish to determine the thermodynamic limit

K(U)\penalty\ :=\penalty\ \lim_{M,N\rightarrow\infty}|Z_{MN}(U)|^{\frac{1}{MN}}

(6.76)

and then evaluate them at
U^{\mathrm{SFN}}
and
U^{\mathrm{SBW}}
.
We will calculate the limit (
6.76
) using the method of inversion relations, in relation to the non-supersymmetric case in section (
5.2.2
).

6.3.1
Inversion relations

The super x-unity relation (
3.67
) allows us to find four different forms of the inverse of the row matrix
T_{N}(U)
by the same moves as explained in (
5.86
).
We find

T_{N}(U)\circ T_{N}(U_{\mathrm{inv}})=F_{N}\cdot\mathbb{1}_{N}

(6.77)

to hold for

\displaystyle{\scriptstyle U_{\mathrm{inv}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-u_{-}&D-1-v_{-}\\
D-1-u_{+}&-v_{+}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle{\scriptstyle\mathrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ F_{N}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \left[16\pi^{2D}\;a_{0}(u_{+})\,a_{0}(D-1-u_{+})\,a_{0}(v_{-})\,a_{0}(D-1-v_{-})\right]^{N}}\penalty\ ,

(6.78a)

\displaystyle{\scriptstyle U_{\mathrm{inv}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-u_{-}&D-1-v_{-}\\
-u_{+}&D-1-v_{+}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle{\scriptstyle\mathrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ F_{N}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \left[16\pi^{2D}\;a_{0}(v_{+})\,a_{0}(D-1-v_{+})\,a_{0}(v_{-})\,a_{0}(D-1-v_{-})\right]^{N}}\penalty\ ,

(6.78b)

\displaystyle{\scriptstyle U_{\mathrm{inv}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D-1-u_{-}&-v_{-}\\
D-1-u_{+}&-v_{+}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle{\scriptstyle\mathrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ F_{N}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \left[16\pi^{2D}\;a_{0}(u_{+})\,a_{0}(D-1-u_{+})\,a_{0}(u_{-})\,a_{0}(D-1-u_{-})\right]^{N}}\penalty\ ,

(6.78c)

\displaystyle{\scriptstyle U_{\mathrm{inv}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D-1-u_{-}&-v_{-}\\
-u_{+}&D-1-v_{+}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle{\scriptstyle\mathrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ F_{N}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \left[16\pi^{2D}\;a_{0}(u_{-})\,a_{0}(D-1-u_{-})\,a_{0}(v_{+})\,a_{0}(D-1-v_{+})\right]^{N}}\penalty\ .

(6.78d)

We recall that
D
is the dimension of the bosonic part of superspace, i. e. we have
D=3
for the superfishnet theory and
D=4
for the super brick wall theory.
For example, (
6.78d
) for
D=4
can be obtained from the super x-unity relation (
3.67
) by the steps

\displaystyle T_{N}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{+}&v_{+}\\
u_{-}&v_{-}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\circ T_{N}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}3-u_{-}&-v_{-}\\
-u_{+}&3-v_{+}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}

(6.79a)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:SuperXUnity_red}}}{{=}}\penalty\ \scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\left[-4\pi^{4}a_{0}(u_{-})a_{0}(3-u_{-})\right]^{N}

(6.79b)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phantom{\eqref{eq:SuperXUnity_green}}}}{{=}}\penalty\ \scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\left[-4\pi^{4}a_{0}(u_{-})a_{0}(3-u_{-})\right]^{N}

(6.79c)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:SuperXUnity_green}}}{{=}}\penalty\ \scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\cdot\left[16\pi^{8}\;a_{0}(u_{-})a_{0}(3-u_{-})\;a(v_{+})a(3-v_{+})\right]^{N}

(6.79d)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phantom{\eqref{eq:SuperXUnity_green}}}}{{=}}\left[16\pi^{8}\;a_{0}(u_{-})a_{0}(3-u_{-})\;a(v_{+})a(3-v_{+})\right]^{N}\cdot\mathbb{1}_{N}\penalty\ ,

(6.79e)

and the other equations of (
6.78
) can be obtained by using variations of (
3.67
) with left- and right external points interchanged.

Projecting (
6.72
) on the eigenvector corresponding to the maximal eigenvalue dominating the thermodynamic limit
[
1
]
,

K(\mathbf{u})=\lim_{M,N\rightarrow\infty}|\mathrm{tr}\left[T_{N}(\mathbf{u})^{M}\right]|^{\frac{1}{MN}}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}|\Lambda_{\mathrm{max},N}(\mathbf{u})|^{\frac{1}{N}}\penalty\ ,

(6.80)

we can turn (
6.78
) into four functional relations for
K(U)
, which are of the form
K(U)K(U_{\mathrm{inv}})=F_{N}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{N}}}
.
Based on the observation that
K(U)
corresponds to a rhombus of four superpropagators, according to (
6.76
) and (
6.72
), we make the ansatz
K(U)=\kappa(u_{+})\kappa(u_{-})\kappa(v_{+})\kappa(v_{-})
.
This is in analogy to (
5.92
).
We find that
\kappa(u)
has to satisfy

\kappa(u)\kappa(-u)=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \kappa(u)\kappa(D-1-u)=4\pi^{D}\;a_{0}(u)\,a_{0}(D-1-u)\penalty\ .

(6.81)

We can construct a solution with the help of appendix
C
, where we plug the two functional relations (
6.81
) iteratively into each other and require the solution to have no poles in the physical interval
\left[0,\frac{D}{2}\right)
, which indicates the maximal eigenvalue
[
35
]
.
We find

\displaystyle\kappa(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D=3}}{{=}}2^{\frac{3u}{2}+1}\pi^{\frac{3u}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-u\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{u}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(2k-u+\frac{3}{2})\Gamma(2k+u)\Gamma(2k-\frac{3}{2})}{\Gamma(2k+u-\frac{3}{2})\Gamma(2k-u)\Gamma(2k+\frac{3}{2})}\penalty\ ,

(6.82a)

\displaystyle\kappa(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D=4}}{{=}}12^{\frac{u}{3}}\pi^{\frac{4u}{3}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{u+1}{3}\right)\Gamma(2-u)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)}

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(3k-u+2)\Gamma(3k+u)\Gamma(3k-2)}{\Gamma(3k+u-2)\Gamma(3k-u)\Gamma(3k+2)}\penalty\ .

(6.82b)

The expressions resemble a lot the findings of the non-supersymmetric case (
5.94
), despite having the one here is derived from a more exotic superspace computation.
The only imprint of supersymmetry is the factor in front of the counting parameter in the gamma functions: it is
D-1
, which is the scaling dimension of the superspace measure
\mathrm{d}^{D}x\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}
.
A special value is
\kappa(0)=1
for any
D
, which reflects the trivial statement that a superpropagator with exponent zero should not contribute to the critical coupling.
For the case of the four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
super brick wall result (
6.82b
), we make an interesting observation.
With the help of the functional relation of the gamma function, we observe that the infinite product collapses to the expression

\kappa(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D=4}}{{=}}2^{\frac{2u}{3}}3^{\frac{4u}{3}-2}\pi^{\frac{4u}{3}}\frac{\Gamma(2-u)\Gamma\left(\frac{u}{3}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{u+1}{3}\right)}{\Gamma(u)\Gamma\left(1-\frac{u}{3}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}-\frac{u}{3}\right)}

(6.83)

with the special value
\kappa(1)=\left(\frac{2\pi^{2}}{3}\right)^{2/3}\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})
.
This is a rare case, where we are able to study a function
\kappa(u)
analytically as a function of
u
, since the general representation as an infinite product is unpractical in this regard.
A plot of the function (
6.83
) is presented in fig.
6.3
.
It has a characteristic pole at the value of the crossing parameter
u=2
, which is the value for which the bosonic part of the superpropagator becomes close to a delta function, up to the damping factor
a(\varepsilon)
, c. f. (
3.12
).

Figure 6.3:
The plot shows the magnitude of the function
\kappa(u)
from (
6.83
) for four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace and complex
u
. It contributes to the critical coupling of the super brick wall theory. We observe that there is a characteristic pole at
u=2
. This is an expected property for a free energy in the thermodynamic limit, which has a pole at the value of the crossing parameter. For four dimensions, this is
\frac{D}{2}=2
. Furthermore, the coloring indicates the phase of the complex number
\kappa(u)
. In the physical strip
[0,2)
, the graph is red, which corresponds to a zero-phase and real values of
\kappa(u)
. For the application to the super brick wall theory, we related the critical coupling to the value at
u=1
.

Finally, we find the critical coupling for the non-locally deformed theories to be

\xi_{\mathrm{cr}}^{\mathrm{SFN}}=\left[\frac{1}{4}\prod_{i=1}^{4}a_{0}(1-\omega_{i})\kappa(\tfrac{1}{2}-\omega_{i})\right]^{-1/2}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \xi_{\mathrm{cr}}^{\mathrm{SBW}}=\left[\prod_{i=1}^{3}a_{0}(1+\omega_{i})\,\kappa\left(1-\omega_{i}\right)\right]^{-1/2}\penalty\ ,

(6.84)

where we plugged the function
\kappa(u)
into
K(U)
and eventually into (
6.75
).
We used the definition of the factor
c_{0}(v)
from (
3.6
).
For generic deformation parameters
\omega_{i}
, it is difficult to evaluate the infinite product (
6.82
).
However, by the constraint
\sum_{i}\omega_{i}=0
, we are able to construct some cases where we turn some elements in
U^{\mathrm{SFN}}
or
U^{\mathrm{SBW}}
to zero, so that the contribution of some propagators vanishes and the graphical link disappears.

Of course, of most importance are the undeformed superfishnet and super brick wall theory, which we access by choosing all deformation parameters to be zero,
\omega_{i}=0
.
In this case, the critical couplings

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{cr}}^{\mathrm{SFN}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})^{2}}\cdot\kappa(\tfrac{1}{2})^{-2}=\frac{\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\right)^{3/2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{\pi^{3}\sqrt{2}\,|\eta(\mathrm{i})|^{2}}\penalty\ ,

(6.85a)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{cr}}^{\mathrm{SBW}}

\displaystyle=\kappa\left(1\right)^{-3/2}=\frac{3}{2\pi^{2}\;\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})^{3/2}}=\frac{3^{9/8}}{4\pi^{3}\,|\eta(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{3}})|}

(6.85b)

follow from (
6.84
).
The expression in terms of the Dedekind eta values is obtained by the identities of the gamma function values under (
5.97
).
In comparison to (
5.97
), we notice that the exponents of the Dedekind eta values are significantly lower.

Chapter 7
Conclusions and outlook

To conclude, we give a summary over the results of this thesis and comment on future research directions.
By using the correspondence to integrable lattice models, we were able to derive several exact results in integrable quantum field theories.
On the one hand, we applied the method of inversion relations to obtain the critical coupling of the bi-scalar fishnet theory, the fishnet-deformation of ABJM, a six-dimensional cubic scalar theory, the brick wall theory, the fermionic fishnet theory, and of two supersymmetric theories, the superfishnet and super brick wall model.
We found the numerical curiosity that all the values of the critical couplings are related to values of the Dedekind eta function.
Furthermore, employing the chain relations, we were able to diagonalize the supergraph-building operators for three classes of four-point correlation functions, which were the zero- and two-magnon cases in the superfishnet and zero-magnon cases of the super brick wall theory.
This gave us access to the exact anomalous dimension of various single-trace operators, and in the zero-magnon superfishnet case, we could even find the all-loop scaling dimension of operators containing derivatives.
Moreover, this allowed us to determine the OPE coefficient associated to the four-point function exactly, and we found the latter to all orders in the coupling.
Additionally, we made conceptual progress by extending the notion of integrable Feynman graphs to boundary integrability.
With the two boundary-star-triangle relations, we found two criteria for integrable boundary conditions of Feynman graphs.
The two solutions we presented allow us to model a trivial boundary as well as an external spacetime point.
Finally, we report novel box-type boundary conditions for the six-vertex model.
In the case of a square box, we demonstrated that its bulk- and boundary-integrability allow us to deduce a recursion relation for the partition function.
We found a function satisfying it and conjecture that this is describing the partition function for box-boundary conditions at any lattice size.

Future research directions

Based on our findings, there are many future research directions.
First, the conjecture of the partition function for the box-boundary conditions of the six-vertex model has to be proven or discarded.
Thanks to Monte-Carlo simulations, one can check the numerical predictions of the conjectured expression and perform valuable preliminary cross-checks
[
184
]
.
A proof could follow the lines of the domain-wall boundary conditions
[
134
,
135
]
and examine the degree of the partition function in the spectral parameter, since its dependence is polynomial.
The degree of the polynomial determines the number of coefficients which have to be fixed by the recursion relation.
The box boundary can be used to model numerous different boundary conditions by individually choosing the K-matrix of each side.
Here, we only looked at the arrow-reflecting box, but one can also solve the bYBE for arrow-sourcing or -sinking walls and combine the boundary conditions of all four sides.
Furthermore, one can extend the conjecture to a rectangular box or study the thermodynamic limit of the partition function, similar to the domain-wall boundary conditions and its connection to matrix models
[
185
,
186
]
.

Second, the framework of boundary-integrable Feynman graphs should be applied to a physical, integrable QFT.
Possible applications might be the study of two- and four-point functions in the checkerboard theory
[
111
]
, which could be achieved by the canonical solution presented in this thesis.
The existence of other solutions of the bYBE is also an interesting question.
Imaginable are Wilson lines or other defects in the context of fishnet theories
[
160
]
, which couple to the bulk Feynman diagrams, when computing quantities like the quark-antiquark potential.
Furthermore, one could combine the box-boundary conditions with boundary-integrable Feynman diagrams.
Using the canonical solution on all four walls, one can hope to find an explanation by lattice models of why Basso-Dixon integrals can be expressed in determinant form
[
84
,
85
]
.

Third, the study of the superfishnet- and super brick wall theory has just begun and many follow-up investigations are possible.
Most importantly is the quest to find a supersymmetric star-triangle relation in order to construct an R-matrix and explicitly construct commuting transfer matrices, similar to the fishnet case
[
96
,
82
]

One can approach the problem systematically by deriving an R-matrix from non-compact representations of the theories’ superconformal algebra
\mathfrak{osp}(2|4)
or
\mathfrak{sl}(4|1)
, respectively.
For the one-dimensional superconformal algebra, this was done in
[
187
,
188
,
189
]
.
Instrumentally, a supersymmetric analog of the principle series representation of the superconformal algebra should be constructed to find an Lax operator, which can be used to solve the RLL relations and obtain the R-matrix.
Using non-compact oscillator representations for the superalgebras provides another angle of attack to this problem
[
190
]
.
After having established the R-matrix, many investigations of the bi-scalar theory could be uplifted to the superfishnet theory.
Interesting would be the TBA for two-point functions
[
91
,
92
]
, supersymmetric Basso-Dixon diagrams
[
84
,
87
,
95
,
88
,
191
]
, the construction of a holographic superfishchain
[
105
,
106
,
107
,
160
,
192
]
, the Yangian bootstrap of supergraphs
[
81
,
94
]
and the interplay of super Feynman graphs with supersymmetric curves
[
193
,
194
,
195
]
.

Fourth, the idea of the superspace formalism for integrable Feynman diagrams could be extended to the full
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM.
The theory can be formulated on a different kind of superspace, the harmonic superspace
[
196
,
197
]
.
The Feynman rules have been established in
[
198
]
and one can imagine that the generalization of the propagator exponents yields useful integral relations such as the chain relation or even the STR.
This could be a promising attempt, despite the appearance of gauge supermultiplets.

Fifth, the appearance of Dedekind eta values in the critical couplings’ results might hint to the existence of a string theory computation via the AdS/CFT correspondence.
The toroidal partition functions of two-dimensional worldsheet CFTs typically give rise to Dedekind eta functions
[
169
]
, which depend on the modulus of the worldsheet torus and could reproduce our findings for the critical coupling.
At first glance, this intuitively makes sense: the toroidal vacuum (super) graphs become dense in the thermodynamical limit and form a continuous surface.
This surface should be interpreted as the string’s worldsheet.
Moreover, the Dedekind eta functions obtained in this dissertation are evaluated at two very special points of the complex structure modulus of the torus, the orbifold singularities in the fundamental domain
\tau=\mathrm{i}
and
\tau=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi/3}
.
In our integrability-based analysis, these values are determined by the physical values of the spectral parameters of the vacuum graphs.
However, in a two-dimensional CFT computation, we expect an integral over the whole fundamental domain of moduli space.
This would suggest the existence of a mechanism that stabilizes the modulus of the complex structure at the particular values.

Sixth, the whole program of identifying a statistical lattice model within double-scaled
\gamma
-deformations of holographic theories could be applied to the
\mathrm{AdS}_{3}\mathrm{/CFT}_{2}
-correspondence.
Its integrability on both sides of the AdS/CFT-correspondence is reviewed in
[
199
]
.
One can imagine to deform the Higgs-Branch of the two-dimensional CFT
[
200
]
or to perform the deformation on the dual symmetric-orbifold CFT directly.
This would yield a unique window into the triality of integrable models, conformal field theories, and holographic dual string theories, since for particular examples of
\mathrm{AdS}_{3}
-backgrounds the holographic duality is made manifest on the level of the partition function
[
201
,
202
]
.

To end this thesis, we would like to speculate about the extension of the ideas which were reviewed and newly formulated in this dissertation into the final goal, an explicit triality between gauge theory, string theory, and statistical models.
On the level of strongly deformed versions of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM, the lattice model perspective can be made explicit.
One of this dissertation’s novel contributions is to initiate the study of the duality by supersymmetric Feynman graphs.
However, besides the fishchain theory, the relations to a holographic string theory are still unclear, especially from the lattice model side.
A major challenge is to undo the double-scaling limit, which seems to be the fundamental for the emergence of the lattice structure in Feynman graphs.
Therefore, we expect that overcoming this problem is the long-term priority.
It could lead to a remarkable triality between supersymmetric gauge theories, superstring theories and statistical models with spins valued in a non-compact representation of a superconformal algebra on a regular lattice.
Possibly, the latter can be thought of as some highly generalized version of ice.

Chapter A
Proof of integral relations

In this appendix, we give a detailed derivation of the fundamental integral relations.
They are the scalar STR, the fermionic STR, the super chain relations and an auxiliary relation for the proof of the super x-unity relation.

A.1
Proof of the scalar uniqueness relation

Fist, we proof the bosonic uniqueness relation (
3.10
), which is interpreted as a STR in (
3.11
).
We begin on the l. h. s. and the star-integral

\mathsf{star}:=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ \frac{1}{\left[x_{10}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{20}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{30}^{2}\right]^{u_{3}}}\penalty\ .

(A.1)

For the moment, we leave
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
unconstrained and we will find below that they have to add up to the dimension
D
in order to be able to find the equality to a triangle graph.
We introduce Schwinger parameters by the relation

\frac{1}{\left[x^{2}\right]^{u}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(u)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\penalty\ s^{u-1}\mathrm{e}^{-s\,x^{2}},

(A.2)

which follows from the integral representation of the gamma function.
We find for the star-integral the expression

\mathsf{star}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(u_{1})\Gamma(u_{2})\Gamma(u_{3})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{3}\penalty\ s_{1}^{u_{1}-1}s_{2}^{u_{2}-1}s_{3}^{u_{3}-1}\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ \mathrm{e}^{-s_{1}\,x_{10}^{2}-s_{2}\,x_{20}^{2}-s_{3}\,x_{30}^{2}}\penalty\ .

(A.3)

It allows us to perform the Gaussian
x_{0}
-integration after expanding the exponent.
Concretely, we evaluate the integral as

\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ \mathrm{e}^{-s_{1}\,x_{10}^{2}-s_{2}\,x_{20}^{2}-s_{3}\,x_{30}^{2}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{S}\left(s_{1}s_{2}x_{12}^{2}+s_{1}s_{3}x_{13}^{2}+s_{2}s_{3}x_{23}^{2}\right)}\left(\tfrac{\pi}{S}\right)^{\frac{D}{2}}

(A.4)

where we used the abbreviation
S:=s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}
.
In order to perform the integration over the Schwinger parameters, we substitute them by
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
, and
t_{3}
that are related to
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
, and
s_{3}
by

\begin{array}[]{lll}s_{i}=\frac{T}{t_{i}}\penalty\ ,&S=s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}=\frac{T^{2}}{t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}}\penalty\ ,&\mathrm{det}\left(\frac{\partial s_{i}}{\partial t_{j}}\right)=\frac{T^{3}}{t_{1}^{2}t_{2}^{2}t_{3}^{2}}\penalty\ ,\\
t_{j}=\frac{s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}}{S\cdot s_{j}}\penalty\ ,&T=t_{1}t_{2}+t_{2}t_{3}+t_{1}t_{3}=s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}\penalty\ .&\end{array}

(A.5)

After the substitution the star-integral reads

\begin{split}\mathsf{star}\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\frac{\pi^{\frac{D}{2}}}{\Gamma(u_{1})\Gamma(u_{2})\Gamma(u_{3})}\\
&\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{3}\penalty\ T^{u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3}-D}\;t_{1}^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{1}-1}t_{2}^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{2}-1}t_{3}^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{3}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{3}x_{12}^{2}-t_{2}x_{13}^{2}-t_{1}x_{23}^{2}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(A.6)

Under the assumption
u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3}=D
, the expression factorizes in three integrals over the individual parameters
t_{j}
, such that we reverse the Schwinger trick (
A.2
) to find the uniqueness relation (
3.10
)

\begin{split}\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ \frac{1}{\left[x_{10}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{20}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{30}^{2}\right]^{u_{3}}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\pi^{\frac{D}{2}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{D}{2}-u_{1})\Gamma(\frac{D}{2}-u_{2})\Gamma(\frac{D}{2}-u_{3})}{\Gamma(u_{1})\Gamma(u_{2})\Gamma(u_{3})}\\
&\cdot\frac{1}{\left[x_{23}^{2}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{1}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{13}^{2}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{3}}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(A.7)

A.2
Proof of the fermionic uniqueness relation

The fermionic STR (
3.40
) is proven in a similar manner as the bosonic STR in (
A.1
).
However, we have to rewrite the fermionic propagators as bosonic ones with a derivative acting on them.
The fermionic star is the l. h. s. of (
3.40
) and reads

\mathsf{fstar}:=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ \frac{1}{\left[x_{10}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{x_{10,\alpha\dot{\alpha}}}{\left|x_{10}\right|}\frac{1}{\left[x_{20}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\frac{\bar{x}_{20}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}}{\left|x_{20}\right|}\frac{1}{\left[x_{30}^{2}\right]^{u_{3}}}\penalty\ ,

(A.8)

with
u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3}=D
.
We can turn the additional fermion propagators into derivatives by the useful equality

\frac{1}{\left[x_{10}^{2}\right]^{u}}\frac{x_{10,\alpha\dot{\alpha}}}{\left|x_{10}\right|}=\frac{1}{1-2u}\partial_{1,\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{10}^{2}\right]^{u-\frac{1}{2}}}\penalty\ ,

(A.9)

which leaves us with

\begin{split}\mathsf{fstar}=\frac{1}{(1-2u_{1})(1-2u_{2})}\partial_{1,\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\penalty\ \frac{1}{\left[x_{10}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}-\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{20}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}-\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{30}^{2}\right]^{u_{3}}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(A.10)

Note that the scalar integral crucially cannot be turned into a triangle by the scalar STR (
3.10
), since the exponents only add up to
D-1
.
Still, one can introduce Schwinger parameters by (
A.2
), which leaves us with a Gaussian integral and performing it, gives

\mathsf{fstar}=\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\,\mathrm{d}s_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s_{3}\penalty\ \frac{s_{1}^{(u_{1}-\frac{1}{2})-1}s_{2}^{(u_{2}-\frac{1}{2})-1}s_{3}^{u_{3}-1}}{\Gamma(u_{1}+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(u_{2}+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(u_{3})}\left(\frac{\pi}{S}\right)^{\frac{D}{2}}\partial_{1,\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\,\mathsf{exp}\penalty\ .

(A.11)

Here we use again the abbreviation
S=s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}
and we denote

\mathsf{exp}:=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{S}\left(s_{1}s_{2}\cdot x_{12}^{2}+s_{1}s_{3}\cdot x_{13}^{2}+s_{2}s_{3}\cdot x_{23}^{2}\right)}\penalty\ .

(A.12)

By usage of
x_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{x}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}=x^{2}\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}
and
\partial_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{x}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}=D\cdot\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}
, one can show that acting with the derivatives on
\mathsf{exp}
gives

\partial_{1,\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\,\mathsf{exp}=4\frac{s_{1}s_{2}}{S}\left[\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}\left(\frac{D}{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}s_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{i}}\right)+s_{3}\cdot x_{13,\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{x}_{23}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\right]\mathsf{exp}\penalty\ .

(A.13)

The r. h. s. can be plugged into (
A.11
) and the terms with the
s_{i}
-derivatives can be reformulated by integration by parts,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s_{1}^{u_{1}-\frac{1}{2}}}{S^{\frac{D}{2}+1}}\cdot s_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{1}}\mathsf{exp}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s_{1}^{u_{1}-\frac{1}{2}}}{S^{\frac{D}{2}+1}}\left[u_{1}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{s_{1}}{S}\left(\frac{D}{2}+1\right)\right]\mathsf{exp}\penalty\ ,

(A.14a)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s_{2}^{u_{2}-\frac{1}{2}}}{S^{\frac{D}{2}+1}}\cdot s_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{2}}\mathsf{exp}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s_{2}^{u_{2}-\frac{1}{2}}}{S^{\frac{D}{2}+1}}\left[u_{2}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{s_{2}}{S}\left(\frac{D}{2}+1\right)\right]\mathsf{exp}\penalty\ ,

(A.14b)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s_{3}^{u_{3}-1}}{S^{\frac{D}{2}+1}}\cdot s_{3}\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{3}}\mathsf{exp}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s_{3}^{u_{3}-1}}{S^{\frac{D}{2}+1}}\left[u_{3}-\frac{s_{3}}{S}\left(\frac{D}{2}+1\right)\right]\mathsf{exp}\penalty\ .

(A.14c)

We find that the expression in the round brackets in (
A.13
) does not contribute to (
A.11
), where we recall that
u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3}=D
.
Thus, we have

\mathsf{fstar}=\pi^{\frac{D}{2}}\,x_{13,\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{x}_{23}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\,\mathrm{d}s_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s_{3}\penalty\ \frac{s_{1}^{u_{1}-\frac{1}{2}}s_{2}^{u_{2}-\frac{1}{2}}s_{3}^{u_{3}}}{\Gamma(u_{1}+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(u_{2}+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(u_{3})}\,S^{-\frac{D}{2}-1}\,\mathsf{exp}\penalty\ ,

(A.15)

and next we can make the same substitution as in the scalar case (
A.5
).
Therefore, we obtain

\begin{split}\mathsf{fstar}&=\pi^{\frac{D}{2}}\,x_{13,\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{x}_{23}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\,\mathrm{d}t_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t_{3}\penalty\ \frac{t_{1}^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{1}-\frac{1}{2}}t_{2}^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{2}-\frac{1}{2}}t_{3}^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{3}-1}}{\Gamma(u_{1}+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(u_{2}+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(u_{3})}\,\mathrm{e}^{-t_{3}x_{12}^{2}-t_{2}x_{13}^{2}-t_{1}x_{23}^{2}}\\
