&=r_{\frac{1}{2}}(u_{3},u_{1},u_{2})\cdot\frac{1}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{3}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\left[x_{13}^{2}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{2}}}\frac{x_{13,\alpha\dot{\alpha}}}{\left|x_{13}\right|}\cdot\frac{1}{\left[x_{23}^{2}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{1}}}\frac{\bar{x}_{23}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}}{\left|x_{23}\right|}\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(A.16)

after reversing the Schwinger trick (
A.2
).
This is the r. h. s. of (
3.40
) and completes the proof of the fermionic spin-
\frac{1}{2}
STR.

A.3
Details of the super-integral calculations

A.3.1
Super chain relation

We will present the derivation of the super chain relations (
3.60
) in three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
superspace as well as four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace.
The computation to proof (
3.61
) works analogously.
We start with the integral

\left.\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}_{0}\frac{1}{\left[x_{1\bar{0}}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{2\bar{0}}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{0}=0\\
\bar{\theta}_{1,2}=0\end{subarray}}

(A.17)

and represent the fermionic dependency via shift operators
1
1

1

A very useful relation for shift operators is

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\,\partial_{\mu}}\left(f(x)\cdot g(x)\right)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\,\partial_{\mu}}f(x)\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\,\partial_{\mu}}g(x)\penalty\ .

, c. f. (
3.53
).
Afterwards, we can execute the integral over the bosonic subspace by (
3.8
) to find

\begin{split}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}_{0}\;\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\theta_{1}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{0}\partial_{1,\mu}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\theta_{2}\sigma^{\nu}\bar{\theta}_{0}\partial_{2,\nu}}\;\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\,\frac{1}{\left[x_{10}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{20}^{2}\right]^{u_{2}}}\\
=\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}_{0}\;\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\theta_{12}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}_{0}\partial_{1,\mu}}\frac{r(D-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}+u_{2}-\frac{D}{2}}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(A.18)

We used the fact that
\partial_{2,\mu}
can be replaced by
-\partial_{1,\mu}
, when acting on a function of
x_{12}
.
The Graßmann integral can now be executed, which amounts to picking the second order in the expansion of the exponential.
Consecutively using (
D.5
) for
D=4

\mathcal{N}=1
superspace and (
D.11
) for
D=3

\mathcal{N}=2
superspace, leaves us with

\theta_{12}^{2}\square_{1}\frac{r(D-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}+u_{2}-\frac{D}{2}}}=\left\{\begin{matrix}4\,r_{0}(2-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\frac{\theta_{12}^{2}}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}+u_{2}-\frac{1}{2}}}\\
-4\,r_{0}(3-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\frac{\theta_{12}^{2}}{\left[x_{12}^{2}\right]^{u_{1}+u_{2}-1}}\end{matrix}\right.\penalty\ ,

(A.19)

where we used the functional relation of the gamma function after performing the derivative by the equation
\square_{1}[x_{12}^{2}]^{-u}=-4u(\frac{D}{2}-u-1)[x_{12}^{2}]^{-u-1}
.
As in (
3.60
), the upper case is related to the three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
superspace and the lower case corresponds to the four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace.

A.3.2
An auxiliary relation for the construction of super x-unity

We present the derivation of (
3.66
) by direct calculation.
Again, the upper case refers to three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
superspace and the lower case to the four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace.
The diagram reads

\left.\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{0}\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}_{0}\;\delta^{(2)}(\theta_{0})\frac{1}{\left[x_{2\bar{0}}\right]^{u_{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{3\bar{0}}\right]^{D-1-u_{1}-u_{2}}}\penalty\ \int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{1}\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta_{1}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}_{1}\;\delta^{(2)}(\bar{\theta}_{1})\frac{1}{\left[x_{1\bar{0}}\right]^{u_{1}}}\right|_{\bar{\theta}_{2,3}=0}\penalty\ .

(A.20)

The fermionic part of the superspace integral over
z_{1}
can be performed, and we find

\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{1}\;\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta_{1}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}_{1}\;\delta^{(2)}(\bar{\theta}_{1})\frac{1}{\left[x_{1\bar{0}}\right]^{u_{1}}}=\left\{\begin{matrix}4\\
-4\end{matrix}\right\}\cdot u_{1}(\tfrac{D}{2}-u_{1}-1)\;\bar{\theta}^{2}_{0}\;\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{1}\;\frac{1}{\left[x_{10}\right]^{u_{1}+1}}\penalty\ .

(A.21)

Here we used again the relation
\square_{1}[x_{12}^{2}]^{-u}=-4u(\frac{D}{2}-u-1)[x_{12}^{2}]^{-u-1}
.
Plugging (
A.21
) into (
A.20
), we can perform the
z_{0}
superspace integral.
The fermionic integrations evaluate the fermionic delta functions
\theta_{0}^{2}
and
\bar{\theta}_{0}^{2}
, and the bosonic integral is performed by the bosonic star-triangle relation in
D
dimensions (
3.10
).
We obtain for (
A.20
)

\left\{\begin{matrix}4\\
-4\end{matrix}\right\}\cdot u_{1}(\tfrac{D}{2}-u_{1}-1)r_{0}(u_{2},D-1-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1}+1)\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{1}\;\frac{1}{\left[x_{13}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{2}}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{23}\right]^{\frac{D}{2}-u_{1}-1}}\frac{1}{\left[x_{12}\right]^{u_{1}+u_{2}+1-\frac{D}{2}}}\penalty\ .

(A.22)

Next, we use the bosonic chain relation (
3.8
) to evaluate the integration over
x_{1}
and we find the dependence on the external points vanishing and (
A.20
) turning into a factor

\left\{\begin{matrix}4\\
-4\end{matrix}\right\}\cdot u_{1}(\tfrac{D}{2}-u_{1}-1)r_{0}(u_{2},D-1-u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1}+1)r_{0}(D-u_{1}-1,u_{1}+u_{2}+1-\tfrac{D}{2},\tfrac{D}{2}-u_{2})\penalty\ .

(A.23)

Finally, the result of (
3.66
) can be obtained by using the explicit form of the
r_{0}
-factor (
3.9
) and the functional relation of the gamma function.

Chapter B
Perturbative calculations in bi-scalar fishnet theory

B.1
Renormalization of the double-trace couplings

We give a detailed derivation of the beta function of the double-trace couplings of bi-scalar fishnet theory as stated in section
5.1.2
.
Our calculation is up to three loops
1
1

1

We thank G. Korchemsky for correspondence on this topic.
, however the fixed-point was obtained up to seven loops in
[
79
]
using Mellin-Barnes techniques.
In this section we use momentum-space Feynman diagrams and regularized the divergences by dimensional regularization.
Therefore, our spacetime is assumed to have
D=4-2\varepsilon
dimensions and once all the divergences are renormalized, we can take the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
Furthermore, we study the bi-scalar fishnet theory (
5.25
) on a spacetime with Euclidean signature.
In this section, we make use of momentum-space Feynman rules.

B.1.1
Regularized amplitudes

Formally, we introduce an counter term
\delta^{(L)}
for the
\alpha_{1}^{2}
coupling by

\alpha_{1}^{2}\rightarrow\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}:=\mu^{\varepsilon}\alpha_{1}^{2}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \mu^{\varepsilon}\alpha_{1}^{2}\cdot\delta^{(L)}:=\mu^{\varepsilon}\alpha_{1}^{2}Z^{(L)},

(B.1)

and
Z^{(L)}=1+\delta^{(L)}
.
In addition, we introduced a scale
\mu
, which vanished in the limit
D\rightarrow 4
.
The value of
\delta^{(L)}
, and therefore the value of
Z^{(L)}
, have to be determined loop-order by loop-order and the superscript
L
indicates the loop level under examination.
The condition for the tuning of
\delta^{(L)}
is the finiteness of the four-point amplitude

A(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\left\langle\mathrm{tr}\left(\phi_{1}(p_{1})\phi_{1}(p_{2})\right)\mathrm{tr}\left(\phi_{1}^{\dagger}(p_{3})\phi_{1}^{\dagger}(p_{4})\right)\right\rangle

(B.2)

up to
L
-loops, when one includes diagrams that contain the counter term vertex.
The diagrams containing it are of lower loop order than
L
, but since diagrams without counter term vertices are divergent as
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{L}}
in dimensional regularization,
\delta^{(L)}
admits an expansion

\delta^{(L)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \dfrac{(z_{1,1}+z_{1,2}+z_{1,3}+...)}{\varepsilon}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \dfrac{(z_{2,2}+z_{2,3}+z_{2,4}+...)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\penalty\ +\penalty\ ...\penalty\ .

(B.3)

The coefficients
z_{n,k}
are determined by requiring the cancellation of the
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{n}}
-divergences of the
k
-loop diagrams.
Furthermore, the coefficients depend on the renormalized couplings, since they correspond to diagrams containing vertices proportional to the renormalized couplings, i. e.
z_{n,k}=z_{n,k}(\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2},\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2})
.
At this point, we recall that the coupling
\xi^{2}
is not renormalized, since diagrams like the one shown in (
5.27
) are not possible.
However, we have to include the scale
\mu
to keep the coupling dimensionless and we write
\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}=\mu^{\varepsilon}\xi^{2}
.

In order to keep track of the overall order of the couplings, it is handy to introduce a bookkeeping parameter
w
and rescale the bare couplings as

\begin{array}[]{cc}\xi^{2}\rightarrow w\cdot\xi^{2}\penalty\ ,&\alpha_{1}^{2}\rightarrow w\cdot\alpha_{1}^{2}.\end{array}

(B.4)

Then, for the fishnet theory, the
L
-loop diagrams scale as
w^{L+1}
, which implies for for the coefficients

z_{n,k}\rightarrow z_{n,k}w^{k}.

(B.5)

We recall that
k
refers to the number of loops at which the factor
z_{n,k}
has to compensate the divergences.
However, special to fishnet theory with its quartic interactions,
k
is related to the coupling-order, which is the number of vertices in a graph.
The reason can be found in the topology of the Feynman graphs (the skeleton ones, not the fatgraph).
We consider coordinate-space Feynman diagrams for this reasoning.
For a graph with
L
loops it Eulers formula
L=E-V+1
for connected graphs holds
2
2

2

This form can be derived from the general one,
\chi=b_{0}-b_{1}+b_{2}
, (
5.17
). Skeleton graphs are drawn on the plane, therefore
\chi=2
. The number of vertices is the zeroth Betti number
b_{0}=V
, the number of propagators the first,
b_{1}=E
, and the number of faces
b_{2}
is the number of loops plus the external face surrounding the graph. Therefore, we have
b_{2}=L+1
.
, where the number of vertices is the sum of four external ones (which spawn external edges) and internal ones
V=4+V_{\mathrm{int}}
.
The number of propagators is half the number of open half-edges, which are the four external ones and, specific for the fishnet theory with quartic coupling, four times the number of internal vertices.
Thus, the number of propagators is
E=\frac{1}{2}(4+4\cdot V_{\mathrm{int}})
.
Putting everything together, one finds
L=V_{\mathrm{int}}-1
, i.e. all diagrams with the same number of vertices belong to the same loop order.
Hence, the exponent of the bookkeeping parameter
w
keeps track of the number of vertices and loops.
The rescaled renormalization factor for L-loop renormalization can be written formally as the double sum

Z^{(L)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ 1+\delta^{(L)}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{L}\sum_{n=1}^{k}\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{n}}z_{nk}w^{k}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{k}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \sum_{n=1}^{k}\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{n}}z_{nk}\penalty\ .

(B.6)

Therefore, the rescaling (
B.4
) labels the different coupling/loop orders in the expansion of the renormalized coupling (
B.1
),

\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}=\mu^{\varepsilon}\alpha_{1}^{2}w+\mu^{\varepsilon}\alpha_{1}^{2}\delta_{1}w^{2}+\mu^{\varepsilon}\alpha_{1}^{2}\delta_{2}w^{3}+...\penalty\ .

(B.7)

Via the coefficients
\delta_{k}
, the diagram, which consist of only one
\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}
vertex compensates the divergences of multiple higher-loop diagrams that are all of order
k+1
in
w
.
The “bare” higher-loop diagrams need to have
k+1
vertices to match the order in
w
, since they only involve the first term on the r. h. s. of (
B.7
), and by
L=V_{\mathrm{int}}-1
, those have
L=k
loops.
Of course, all coefficients
\delta_{k}
participate at every higher Loop order
L\geq k
as well, but at loop order
L=k
they appear fist and exclusively at this order in the single vertex diagram.
Thus, they are determined the easiest at this loop level.

To determine the
\delta_{k}
’s, we follow the paradigm that after the transition of the bare coupling to the renormalized one, one requires to have a finite four point correlation function (
B.2
).
Prior to renormalizing the coupling (i.e. performing (
B.1
)), the
L
-loop four point functions receives contributions from diagrams with loops
\leq L

A_{L}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \sum_{\ell=0}^{L}A^{(\ell)}\penalty\ ,

(B.8)

where
A^{(\ell)}
consists of all “bare”
\ell
-loop diagrams.
Since, the number of loops is linked to the number of vertices and therefore to the power of
w
(after introducing the bookkeeping parameter but not the renormalized coupling) by
L=V_{\mathrm{int}-1}
, the divergent
\ell
-loop contributions are

A^{(\ell)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ w^{\ell+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\ell}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{n}}a^{(\ell)}_{n}(\alpha_{1}^{2},\xi^{2})\penalty\ .

(B.9)

Replacing
\alpha_{1}^{2}
by the renormalized coupling
\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}
yields the finite amplitude

A^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{R}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ w^{\ell+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\ell}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{R},nk}(\alpha_{1}^{2},\xi^{2};\delta_{k})w^{k}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ A_{\mathrm{R},L}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \sum_{\ell=0}^{L}A^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{R}}\penalty\ ,

(B.10)

where the coefficients
a^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{R},nk}
compensate the
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{n}}
divergences.
Thus, renormalization up to
L
-loops means that all terms of
A_{\mathrm{R},L}
of order smaller of equal to
w^{L+1}
have to be finite.
This statement can be made precise by introducing the operator
\kappa
, which just keeps the divergent part of any
\varepsilon
-expansion
3
3

3

This operator could also be defined differently to apply another scheme, e g. for the
\bar{\mathrm{MS}}
-scheme the
\kappa
-operator should be replaced by
\kappa^{\bar{\mathrm{MS}}}\left[\bullet\right]=\kappa\left[\bullet\right]+\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}
, e g.
\kappa\left[\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{7}{\varepsilon}+4+\varepsilon^{5}\right]=\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{7}{\varepsilon}
.
The central constraint, imposed by finiteness, that allows to determine the orders of the counter term
\delta_{k}
order by order is

\kappa\left[A_{\mathrm{R},L}\right]\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\penalty\ 0+\mathcal{O}\left(w^{L+2}\right)\,.

(B.11)

Essentially, this has to hold for every order in
w
separately, thus this equation furnishes
L
equations for all the
L
unknowns
\delta_{1},...,\delta_{L}
.
In the following, we renormalize
\alpha_{1}^{2}
from (
B.2
) up to three loops, which yields
\delta_{1}
,
\delta_{2}
and
\delta_{3}
.

Tree Level

The renormalization procedure explained above will now be applied. The
0
-loop contribution, i. e. just the double-trace vertex, is

A^{(0)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ 4\cdot(4\pi)^{2}\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\longrightarrow A^{(0)}_{\mathrm{R}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ 4\cdot(4\pi)^{2}\,\mu^{\varepsilon}\left(\alpha_{1}^{2}w+\alpha_{1}^{2}\delta_{1}w^{2}+\alpha_{1}^{2}\delta_{2}w^{3}+\mathcal{O}(w^{4})\right)\,.

(B.12)

Formally one can now “renormalize” up to
0
-loop order by imposing finiteness by (
B.11
)

\kappa\left[A_{\mathrm{R},0}\right]=\kappa\left[A^{(0)}_{\mathrm{R}}\right]=\kappa\left[4\cdot(4\pi)^{2}\,\mu^{\varepsilon}\alpha_{1}^{2}w\right]+\mathcal{O}(w^{2})=0+\mathcal{O}(w^{2})\,.

(B.13)

However, since there are not any divergences the condition (
B.11
) is trivially satisfied and has not to be imposed.
Note that the
\delta_{k}
’s are not appearing here because they are of a higher order in
w
.
Despite of containing divergent parts, they are not seen by the
\kappa
-operator.

One-loop level

The first non-trivial order is at
1
-loop.
We encounter the integral
[
79
]

\begin{split}\pi(s)\penalty\ :=\penalty\ &\int\dfrac{\text{d}^{4-2\varepsilon}\ell}{\mathrm{i}(2\pi)^{4-2\varepsilon}}\dfrac{1}{\ell^{2}(k-\ell)^{2}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \dfrac{(-s/\mu^{2})^{-\varepsilon}}{(4\pi)^{2-\varepsilon}}\dfrac{\Gamma(\varepsilon)\;\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)^{2}}{\Gamma(2-2\varepsilon)}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\dfrac{1}{16\pi^{2}\varepsilon}+\dfrac{2-\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}+\text{log}\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{-s}\right)}{16\pi^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\,,\end{split}

(B.14)

with
k^{2}=s
and we use the abbreviations
s_{ij}=(p_{i}+p_{j})^{2}
and
\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}
for the Euler-Mascheroni constant.
The one-loop contribution is

\begin{split}A^{(1)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\left[\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[scale]{figures/biscalarfishnet/diagrams/ren/alpha1/1loop/VV.pdf}\end{gathered}+(p_{1}\leftrightarrow p_{2})\right]+\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[scale]{figures/biscalarfishnet/diagrams/ren/alpha1/1loop/H.pdf}\end{gathered}+\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[scale]{figures/biscalarfishnet/diagrams/ren/alpha1/1loop/x_H.pdf}\end{gathered}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &8\cdot\left[(4\pi)^{2}\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\right]^{2}\cdot\pi(s_{12})\penalty\ +\penalty\ \left[(4\pi)^{2}\,\xi^{2}\right]^{2}\cdot\pi(s_{13})\penalty\ +\penalty\ \left[(4\pi)^{2}\,\xi^{2}\right]^{2}\cdot\pi(s_{23})\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\dfrac{32\pi^{2}\,(\xi^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{4})}{\varepsilon}\penalty\ +\penalty\ 32\pi^{2}\cdot\left(\xi^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{4}\right)\left[2-\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}+\text{log}\left(4\pi\right)\right]\\
&\penalty\ -\penalty\ 16\pi^{2}\cdot\left[8\,\alpha_{1}^{4}\,\text{log}\left(\frac{-s_{12}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\xi^{4}\,\text{log}\left(\frac{-s_{13}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\xi^{4}\,\text{log}\left(\frac{-s_{23}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right]\penalty\ +\penalty\ \mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\,.\end{split}

(B.15)

We equipped the diagrams with the appropriate symmetry factors.
Inserting the renormalized coupling (
B.7
) (although only the lowest order in
w
is of importance here) for
\alpha_{1}^{2}
and
\xi^{2}\rightarrow\xi^{2}_{\mathrm{R}}
yields

\begin{split}A^{(1)}_{\mathrm{R}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\mu^{2\varepsilon}\dfrac{32\pi^{2}\,(\xi^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{4})}{\varepsilon}w^{2}\penalty\ +\penalty\ 32\pi^{2}\cdot\mu^{2\varepsilon}\left(\xi^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{4}\right)\left[2-\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}+\text{log}\left(4\pi\right)\right]w^{2}\\
&\penalty\ -\penalty\ 16\pi^{2}\cdot\mu^{2\varepsilon}\left[8\,\alpha_{1}^{4}\,\text{log}\left(\frac{-s_{12}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\xi^{4}\,\text{log}\left(\frac{-s_{13}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\xi^{4}\,\text{log}\left(\frac{-s_{23}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right]w^{2}\\
&\penalty\ +\penalty\ \mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\penalty\ +\penalty\ \mathcal{O}(w^{3})\,.\end{split}

(B.16)

The
1
-loop amplitude then is obtained by adding the relevant orders from the tree level (
B.12
) to the one-loop contribution.
We find

\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{R},1}

\displaystyle=4\cdot(4\pi)^{2}\,\mu^{\varepsilon}\left(\alpha_{1}^{2}w+\alpha_{1}^{2}\delta_{1}w^{2}\right)+\mu^{2\varepsilon}\dfrac{32\pi^{2}\,(\xi^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{4})}{\varepsilon}w^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{0})+\mathcal{O}(w^{3})+O\left(\varepsilon\right)\,.

(B.17)

Imposing finiteness by (
B.11
) gives the defining equation for
\delta_{1}
,

\kappa\left[A_{\mathrm{R},1}\right]=4\cdot(4\pi)^{2}\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\kappa\left[\delta_{1}\right]w^{2}+\dfrac{32\pi^{2}\,(\xi^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{4})}{\varepsilon}w^{2}+\mathcal{O}(w^{3})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0+\mathcal{O}(w^{3})\,.

(B.18)

Since the
\delta_{k}
’s are purely divergent, we have

\delta_{1}=\kappa\left[\delta_{1}\right]=-\frac{\xi^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{4}}{2\alpha_{1}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\penalty\ .

(B.19)

Note that here it makes not difference if the renormalized or bare couplings appear because we can neglect higher orders in
w
and
\varepsilon
, since
\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}=\xi^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
and
\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}=\alpha_{1}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(w)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
.
This allows to reexpress (
B.19
) as

\delta_{1}=-\frac{\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{2\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\penalty\ .

(B.20)

With that and (
B.6
), we find the first contribution to the renormalization factor

z_{11}=-\frac{\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{2\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}}\penalty\ .

(B.21)

Two-loop level

At two loops, we need another Feynman integral
[
79
]
,

\begin{split}V(s)\penalty\ :=\penalty\ &\int\dfrac{\text{d}^{4-2\varepsilon}\ell}{\mathrm{i}(2\pi)^{4-2\varepsilon}}\dfrac{\pi(\ell^{2})}{(\ell+p_{1})^{2}(\ell-p_{2})^{2}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \dfrac{(-s/\mu^{2})^{-2\varepsilon}}{(4\pi)^{4-2\varepsilon}}\dfrac{\Gamma(\varepsilon)\;\Gamma(2\varepsilon)\;\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)^{2}\;\Gamma(1-2\varepsilon)^{2}}{\Gamma(2-2\varepsilon)\;\Gamma(2-3\varepsilon)}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\dfrac{1}{512\pi^{4}\varepsilon^{2}}+\dfrac{5-2\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}+2\,\text{log}\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{-s}\right)}{512\pi^{4}\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{0})\,.\end{split}

(B.22)

The bare
2
-loop contribution is made up by the diagrams

\begin{split}A^{(2)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\left[\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[scale]{figures/biscalarfishnet/diagrams/ren/alpha1/2loop/VVV.pdf}\end{gathered}+\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[scale]{figures/biscalarfishnet/diagrams/ren/alpha1/2loop/HV.pdf}\end{gathered}+\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[scale]{figures/biscalarfishnet/diagrams/ren/alpha1/2loop/VH.pdf}\end{gathered}\right]\\
&\penalty\ +\penalty\ (p_{1}\leftrightarrow p_{2})\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &16\cdot\left[(4\pi)^{2}\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\right]^{3}\cdot\pi(s_{12})^{2}\penalty\ +\penalty\ 2\cdot 4\;\left[(4\pi)^{2}\,\xi^{2}\right]^{2}(4\pi)^{2}\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\cdot V(s_{12})\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\dfrac{64\pi^{2}\cdot\alpha_{1}^{2}\left(\xi^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{4}\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\\
&\penalty\ +\penalty\ \dfrac{64\pi^{2}\cdot\alpha_{1}^{2}\left(\xi^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{4}\right)}{\varepsilon}\left[5-2\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-2\text{log}\left(\frac{-s_{12}}{4\pi\mu^{2}}\right)\right]\penalty\ -\penalty\ \dfrac{64\pi^{2}\cdot\alpha_{1}^{2}4\,\alpha_{1}^{6}}{\varepsilon}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{0})\,.\end{split}

(B.23)

After introducing the renormalized couplings, one finds

\begin{split}A^{(2)}_{\mathrm{R}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\frac{64\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}w^{3}\left(4\alpha_{1}^{4}+\xi^{4}\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\\
&-\frac{64\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}w^{3}\left[8\alpha_{1}^{4}\left(\log\left(\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-2\right)+\xi^{4}\left(2\log\left(\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)+2\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-5\right)\right]}{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{0}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(w^{4}\right).\end{split}

(B.24)

Notice that
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
are in terms
\mathcal{O}(w^{4})
in the equation above but they enter
A_{\mathrm{R},2}
by
A^{(1)}_{\mathrm{R}}
and
A^{(0)}_{\mathrm{R}}
at the order
\mathcal{O}(w^{3})
, respectively.
The sum yields the divergent part of the
2
-loop amplitude

\begin{split}\kappa\left[A_{\mathrm{R},2}\right]\penalty\ =\penalty\ &w^{3}\left\{\frac{64\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}\left(4\alpha_{1}^{4}+\xi^{4}\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right.\\
&\left.-\frac{64\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}\left[-4\alpha_{1}^{2}\delta_{1}+8\alpha_{1}^{4}\left(\log\left(\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-2\right)+\xi^{4}\left(2\log\left(\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)+2\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-5\right)\right]}{\varepsilon}\right.\\
&\left.-256\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{4}\delta_{1}\left(\log\left(\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-2\right)+64\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}\delta_{2}\right\}\\
&+w^{2}\left\{64\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}\delta_{1}+\frac{32\pi^{2}\left(4\alpha_{1}^{4}+\xi^{4}\right)}{\epsilon}\right\}.\end{split}

(B.25)

With this expression one can solve the system of equations imposed by the the vanishing of the orders
w^{3}
and
w^{4}
separately by (
B.11
).
With
\delta_{1}=\kappa\left[\delta_{1}\right]
and
\delta_{2}=\kappa\left[\delta_{2}\right]
, the solutions are

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=-\frac{\xi^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{4}}{2\alpha_{1}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{2}=-\frac{\xi^{4}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{\xi^{4}+4\alpha_{1}^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\penalty\ .

(B.26)

Finally, one can replace the bare couplings by the renormalized ones.
As before, the corrections are of higher order in
w
than the one corresponding to the
\delta_{k}
’s or
z_{nk}
’s and we find

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=-\frac{\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{2\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ z_{11}=-\frac{\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+4\,\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{2\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}}\penalty\ ,

(B.27a)

\displaystyle\delta_{2}=-\frac{\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+4\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ z_{12}=-\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4},\penalty\ \penalty\ z_{22}=\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+4\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}\penalty\ .

(B.27b)

Three-loop level

At three loops, the expression for the amplitude-contribution becomes rather lengthy.
We present the Feynman graphs used and the result for the renormalization factor
Z^{(3)}
.
We find the renormalization coefficients of the lowest order to be

\begin{split}Z=&1-\frac{4\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}+\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{2\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\varepsilon}-\frac{\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{8}-4\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{6\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\varepsilon}+\frac{4\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}+\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{20\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{8}}{6\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}\\
&-\frac{16\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+48\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{8}+\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{8}}{6\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\varepsilon^{3}}\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(B.28)

where the last term is a three-loop contribution.
There are four different classes of Feynman diagrams with three loops, which contribute the the correlation function (
B.2
).
We use the abbreviation
S:=\log\frac{s}{4\pi\mu^{2}}
.

•

The chain of three bubble integrals is the single one (
B.14
), raised to the third power,

\begin{split}G_{1}\penalty\ :=\penalty\ &\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[scale]{figures/biscalarfishnet/diagrams/ren/alpha1/3loop/VVVV.pdf}\end{gathered}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &16\cdot\left[(4\pi)^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}\right]^{4}\cdot\pi(s_{12})^{3}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \alpha_{1}^{8}\cdot 8^{2\varepsilon+3}\pi^{3\varepsilon+2}\frac{\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)^{6}\Gamma(\varepsilon)^{3}}{\Gamma(2-2\varepsilon)^{3}}\left(\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{-3\varepsilon}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\frac{512\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{8}}{\varepsilon^{3}}-\frac{1536\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{8}\left[S+\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-2\right]}{\varepsilon^{2}}\\
&-\frac{128\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{8}\left[-18S^{2}-36(\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-2)S-18\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}+\pi^{2}+72\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-96\right]}{\varepsilon}\\
&+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{0}\right).\end{split}

(B.29)

•

The combination of a bubble integral (
B.14
) and the two-loop integral (
B.22
) is

\begin{split}G_{2}\penalty\ :=\penalty\ &\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[scale]{figures/biscalarfishnet/diagrams/ren/alpha1/3loop/VVH.pdf}\end{gathered}\penalty\ =\penalty\ D_{\mathrm{HVV}}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &4\cdot\left[(4\pi)^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}\right]^{2}\left[(4\pi)^{2}\xi^{2}\right]^{2}\cdot\pi(s_{12})V(s_{12})\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\alpha_{1}^{4}\;\xi^{4}\cdot 2^{6\varepsilon+7}\pi^{3\varepsilon+2}\frac{\Gamma(1-2\varepsilon)^{2}\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)^{4}\Gamma(\varepsilon)^{2}\Gamma(2\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(2-3\varepsilon)\Gamma(2-2\varepsilon)^{2}}\left(\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{-3\varepsilon}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\frac{64\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{4}\xi^{4}}{\varepsilon^{3}}-\frac{64\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{4}\xi^{4}\left(3S+3\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-7\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\\
&+\frac{16\pi^{2}\xi^{4}\left[54S^{2}+36(3\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-7)S+54\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}+\pi^{2}-252\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}+396\right]}{3\varepsilon}\\
&+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{0}\right).\end{split}

(B.30)

•

The diagram with a
\alpha_{1}^{2}
vertex left and right is a special case of a so-called kite diagram.
It is determined in
[
203
]
via a hypergeometric function.

\begin{split}G_{3}\penalty\ :=\penalty\ &\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[scale]{figures/biscalarfishnet/diagrams/ren/alpha1/3loop/VHV.pdf}\end{gathered}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &8\cdot\left(4\pi\alpha_{1}\right)^{4}\left(4\pi\xi\right)^{4}\left(-\mu^{2}\right)^{2\varepsilon}\\
&\cdot\int\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{4-2\varepsilon}k_{1}}{\mathrm{i}\left(2\pi\right)^{4-2\varepsilon}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{4-2\varepsilon}k_{2}}{\mathrm{i}\left(2\pi\right)^{4-2\varepsilon}}\dfrac{1}{k_{1}^{2}\,\left(k_{1}-p\right)^{2}}\,\pi\left(\left(k_{1}-k_{2}\right)^{2}\right)\dfrac{1}{k_{2}^{2}\,\left(k_{2}-p\right)^{2}}\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &8\cdot\alpha_{1}^{4}\xi^{4}\cdot 2^{6\varepsilon+5}\pi^{3\varepsilon+2}\cdot\varepsilon\;\frac{\Gamma(1-2\varepsilon)\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)^{4}\Gamma(\varepsilon)\Gamma(3\varepsilon-1)}{(3\varepsilon-2)\Gamma(3-4\varepsilon)\Gamma(2-2\varepsilon)\Gamma(\varepsilon+1)}\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\right)^{3\varepsilon}\\
&\cdot\left[{}_{3}F_{2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1,\penalty\ 2-3\varepsilon,\penalty\ 2-2\varepsilon\\
\penalty\ 3-4\varepsilon,\penalty\ 3-3\varepsilon\penalty\ \end{smallmatrix}|\,1\right)+(3\varepsilon-2)\frac{\Gamma(\varepsilon)\Gamma(2\varepsilon-1)\Gamma(3-4\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)}\penalty\ \cos(2\pi\varepsilon)\right]\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\frac{128\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{4}\xi^{4}}{3\varepsilon^{3}}-\frac{128\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{4}\xi^{4}\left[3S+3\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-7\right]}{3\varepsilon^{2}}\\
&-\frac{32\pi^{2}\alpha_{1}^{4}\xi^{4}\left[-18S^{2}-12(3\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-7)S-18\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}+\pi^{2}+84\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-124\right]}{3\varepsilon}\\
&+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{0}\right).\end{split}

(B.31)

•

The last diagram is a variation of a box-integral, where two propagators have non-unit exponents.
It is presented in
[
204
]
.

\begin{split}G_{4}\penalty\ :=\penalty\ &\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[scale]{figures/biscalarfishnet/diagrams/ren/alpha1/3loop/HH.pdf}\end{gathered}\penalty\ +\penalty\ (p_{1}\leftrightarrow p_{2})\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\left(4\pi\xi\right)^{8}\left(\mu^{2}\right)^{-2\varepsilon}\int\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{4-2\varepsilon}k}{\mathrm{i}\left(2\pi\right)^{4-2\varepsilon}}\pi\left(\left(k-p_{1}\right)^{2}\right)\dfrac{1}{k^{2}\,\left(k-p\right)^{2}}\pi\left(\left(k-p_{3}\right)^{2}\right)\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &(4\pi)^{8}\xi^{8}\cdot\mathrm{i}^{2\varepsilon}(4\pi)^{3\varepsilon-6}\left(\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{-2\varepsilon-\delta}\left(\frac{t}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{-3\varepsilon-\delta}\left(\frac{\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)^{2}\Gamma(\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(2-2\varepsilon)}\right)^{2}\\
&\left[\left(\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{2\varepsilon+\delta}\text{c}_{1}(1,\varepsilon+\delta,1,\varepsilon|4-2\varepsilon)\penalty\ \,_{3}F_{2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1,\penalty\ 1,\penalty\ 3\varepsilon+\delta\\
2\varepsilon+1,\penalty\ 2\varepsilon+1+\delta\end{smallmatrix}|-\frac{s}{t}\right)\right.\\
&+\left(\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{\delta}\left(\frac{t}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{2\varepsilon}\cdot\text{c}_{2}(1,\varepsilon,1,\varepsilon+\delta|4-2\varepsilon)\penalty\ \,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1-2\varepsilon,\penalty\ \varepsilon+\delta\\
1+\delta\end{smallmatrix}|-\frac{s}{t}\right)\\
&\left.+\left(\frac{t}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{2\varepsilon+\delta}\cdot\text{c}_{2}(1,\varepsilon+\delta,1,\varepsilon+\delta|4-2\varepsilon)\penalty\ \,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1-2\varepsilon-\delta,\penalty\ \varepsilon\\
1-\delta\end{smallmatrix}|-\frac{s}{t}\right)\right]\\
&+(t\rightarrow u)\\
\penalty\ =\penalty\ &\frac{32\pi^{2}\xi^{8}}{3\varepsilon^{3}}-\frac{32\pi^{2}\xi^{8}\left(3S+3\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-8\right)}{3\varepsilon^{2}}\\
&+\frac{8\pi^{2}\xi^{8}\left[18S^{2}+12(3\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}-8)S+18\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}+3\pi^{2}-96\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}+176\right]}{3\varepsilon}\\
&+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{0}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\delta\right).\end{split}

(B.32)

With
\nu_{ijkl}=\nu_{i}+\nu_{j}+\nu_{k}+\nu_{l}
,
\nu_{ijk}=\nu_{i}+\nu_{j}+\nu_{k}
and
\nu_{ij}=\nu_{i}+\nu_{j}
, we use the abbreviations

\displaystyle\text{c}_{1}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\nu_{4}|d)\penalty\ =\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{i}^{d}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-\nu_{123}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-\nu_{134}\right)\Gamma\left(-\frac{d}{2}+\nu_{1234}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\nu_{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\nu_{4}\right)\Gamma\left(d-\nu_{1234}\right)}

(B.33a)

\displaystyle\text{c}_{2}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\nu_{4}|d)\penalty\ =\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{i}^{d}\Gamma\left(\nu_{2}-\nu_{4}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-\nu_{12}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-\nu_{23}\right)\Gamma\left(-\frac{d}{2}+\nu_{123}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\nu_{1}\right)\Gamma\left(\nu_{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\nu_{3}\right)\Gamma\left(d-\nu_{1234}\right)},

(B.33b)

where
c_{2}
is divergent for
\nu_{2}=\nu_{4}
, however this is compensated in
G_{4}
by the introduction of
\delta
by modifying
\nu_{2}\rightarrow\nu_{2}+\delta
, then expanding
\delta
around zero shows that the divergence gets canceled by the hypergeometric function and the final result is finite (modulo the divergences in
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
).

B.1.2
Beta function of the double-trace coupling

The beta function describes how the coupling
\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}
runs with the scale
\mu
.
However, we want the bi-scalar fishnet theory to be a CFT.
And indeed, we have to make sure that the coupling
\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}
is scale-independent.
This implies that we have to tune the double-trace coupling to a zero of the beta function.
Here we determine the beta function and its zeros.
By definition, we have

\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\penalty\ :=\penalty\ \mu\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2},

(B.34)

where, in dimensional regularization,
\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}=\mu^{\varepsilon}\alpha_{1}^{2}Z^{(L)}
has a non-trivial
\mu
-dependence also through
Z^{(L)}
.
Performing the derivative, we obtain the following expressions for the beta function

\displaystyle\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\penalty\ =\penalty\

\displaystyle\varepsilon\cdot\mu^{\varepsilon}Z^{(L)}\alpha_{1}^{2}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \alpha_{1}^{2}\mu^{\varepsilon}\cdot\mu\dfrac{\mathrm{d}Z^{(L)}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}

(B.35a)

\displaystyle\penalty\ =\penalty\

\displaystyle\varepsilon\cdot\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\cdot\frac{\mu}{Z^{(L)}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}Z^{(L)}}{\mathrm{d}\mu},

(B.35b)

\displaystyle\penalty\ =\penalty\

\displaystyle\varepsilon\cdot\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{D=4}\penalty\ ,

(B.35c)

expressed in the bare- and renormalized coupling, respectively.
In the last line, the second term was denoted as
\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{D=4}
, since it is indeed the four dimensional beta function, i. e.
\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{D=4}
.
Thus, for its explicit calculation, knowledge about
Z^{(L)}
is decisive.

The beta function of
\xi^{2}
formally plays a role as well, despite of being zero when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
It is

\beta_{\xi^{2}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \mu\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}=\varepsilon\cdot\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}\penalty\ ,

(B.36)

and
Z_{\xi^{2}}=1
because there are no diagrams, which could renormalize this interaction, see (
5.27
).
Consequently, we have
\beta_{\xi^{2}}^{D=4}=0
.
However,
\beta_{\xi^{2}}
is required for the calculation for the beta function of
\alpha_{1}^{2}
, since
Z^{(L)}
depends on
\xi^{2}_{\mathrm{R}}
.
Using the chain rule for derivatives in (
B.35b
), we obtain

\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \varepsilon\cdot\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{Z^{(L)}}\left[\dfrac{\partial Z^{(L)}}{\partial\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}}\beta_{\xi^{2}}+\dfrac{\partial Z^{(L)}}{\partial\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}}\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\right].

(B.37)

This equation has to be solved for
\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}
.
To do so, we first use the fact that beta functions are finite for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
and we make the ansatz
\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}=\varepsilon\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{(1)}+\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{(0)}
.
Next, we multiply both sides of (
B.37
) by
Z^{(L)}
and use the expansion (
B.6
) at
L\rightarrow\infty
.
Finally, comparing terms proportional to the same powers of
\varepsilon
gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{Order}\penalty\ \varepsilon:\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{(1)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}

(B.38a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Order}\penalty\ \varepsilon^{0}:\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{(0)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\left[\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}\dfrac{\partial z_{1k}}{\partial\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}}+\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\dfrac{\partial z_{1k}}{\partial\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}}\right]w^{k}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{D=4}\penalty\ .

(B.38b)

Since the beta function is finite, the other orders
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}
yield further equations involving the coefficients
z_{nk}
, which can be used as consistency checks for the calculation of the diagrams.
Note that the beta function depends through (
B.38b
) only on the coefficients
z_{1k}
, that are those corresponding to the
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
-poles of the
k
-loop diagrams.
In the end, the full beta function is

\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \varepsilon\cdot\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\left[\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}\dfrac{\partial z_{1k}}{\partial\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}}+\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\dfrac{\partial z_{1k}}{\partial\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}}\right]w^{k}\penalty\ .

(B.39)

Only the second term, which is
\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{D=4}
, survives in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
The introduction of the bookkeeping parameter can always be undone by setting
w\rightarrow 1
.

For the three-loop result of the renormalization factor (
B.28
), we find the double-trace beta function of bi-scalar fishnet theory,

\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{3-\mathrm{loop}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \varepsilon\cdot\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\penalty\ -\penalty\ \dfrac{1}{2}\left(\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+4\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}\right)\penalty\ -\penalty\ 2\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{2}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \dfrac{1}{2}\left(-4\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}}^{4}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{8}\right)\penalty\ .

(B.40)

The four-dimensional beta function has to be zero for conformal symmetry on the quantum level.
This is fulfilled, if the fixed-point of the coupling
\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}*}
satisfies

\beta_{\alpha_{1}^{2}}^{3-\mathrm{loop},D=4}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ -\penalty\ \dfrac{1}{2}\left(\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+4\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}*}^{4}\right)\penalty\ -\penalty\ 2\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}*}^{2}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \dfrac{1}{2}\left(-4\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}*}^{4}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{8}\right)=0\penalty\ .

(B.41)

This equation has two solutions for
\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}*}^{2}
, namely

\alpha_{1,\mathrm{R}*}^{2}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \dfrac{-\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}\pm\sqrt{-\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}+\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{8}+\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{12}}}{2(1+\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4})}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \pm\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}-\dfrac{\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}}{2}\mp\dfrac{3\mathrm{i}}{4}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{8})\penalty\ ,

(B.42)

which are the first three orders of the expansion of the fixed points, when expanding for small
\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}
.
Note that at one of the fixed points (
B.42
), the coupling renormalization
Z^{(3)}
is not
1
but

Z^{(3)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ 1+\left[\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}-\dfrac{4\mathrm{i}}{3}\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{10})\right]\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})

(B.43)

for small
\xi_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}
.
Finally, we remark that from now on, section
5.1.2
and below, we will drop the subscript and call the renormalized couplings again
\alpha_{1}^{2}
and
\xi^{2}
.

B.2
The anomalous dimension of
\mathrm{tr}(\phi_{1}^{2})

At the fixed-points of the double-trace couplings, in particular
\alpha_{1}^{2}=\alpha_{1,*}^{2}
, see (
B.42
), the bi-scalar fishnet theory (
5.25
) is a CFT.
Conformal symmetry implies that the properly renormalized two-point functions of an operator
\mathcal{O}
take the following form:

\begin{split}\left<\mathcal{O}(x)\bar{\mathcal{O}}(y)\right>_{\mathrm{R}}&=\mu^{\gamma(\xi^{2})}\sqrt{Z_{\mathcal{O}}}^{2}\left<\mathcal{O}(x)\bar{\mathcal{O}}(y)\right>=\dfrac{v(\xi^{2})\mu^{\gamma(\xi^{2})}}{\left|x-y\right|^{2\Delta_{0}+\gamma(\xi^{2})}}\\
&=\dfrac{v_{0}}{\left|x-y\right|^{2\Delta_{0}}}\left[1-\frac{\xi^{2}\left(v_{0}\gamma_{1}L-v_{1}\right)}{v_{0}}+\frac{\xi^{4}\left(v_{0}\gamma_{1}^{2}L^{2}-2\gamma_{1}v_{1}L-v_{0}\gamma_{2}L+v_{2}\right)}{2v_{0}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\xi^{6}\right)\right].\end{split}

(B.44)

The classical dimension
\Delta_{0}
, the normalization of the two-point function
v(\xi^{2})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{v_{k}}{k!}\xi^{2k}
and the anomalous dimension
\gamma(\xi^{2})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma_{k}}{k!}\xi^{2k}
are all related to the operator
\mathcal{O}
under investigation.
The latter two are functions of the coupling
\xi^{2}
and thus their expansion coefficients
v_{k}
and
\gamma_{k}
can be determined by perturbative calculations up to a certain loop order.
Here, the abbreviation
L:=\log{\left[\mu^{2}(x-y)^{2}\right]}
is used and the energy scale
\mu
adjusts to the mass dimensions in the expansion in
\xi^{2}
.
The wave-function renormalization factor
Z_{\mathcal{O}}
is specific for the operator and has to be determined at the case at hand
[
205
]
.

Our operator of interest is
\mathcal{O}=\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}\phi_{1}\right]
, which has a classical dimension
\Delta_{0}=2
and the goal of this section is to compute its anomalous dimension and two-point normalization,
\gamma=\gamma(\xi^{2})
and
v=v(\xi^{2})
, respectively, via perturbation theory.
In order to determine their expansion coefficients, one has to match the formal expansion (
B.44
) to the expansion in Feynman diagrams at the double-trace couplings’ fixed point.
This will be done in the following up to 3-loop diagrams and with the only appearing double-trace coupling
\alpha_{1}^{2}
untuned.
Just at the end, when both expressions are getting compared, the perturbation expansion will be evaluated at its fixed-point
\alpha_{1,*}^{2}
.

The expressions for the diagrams can be obtained by using directly the coordinate-space Feynman rules, or by Fourier transforming the respective momentum-space Feynman diagrams via the useful relation (see (
3.4
))

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}p}{(2\pi)^{D}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\;p\cdot x}\left(p^{2}\right)^{\alpha}\penalty\ =\penalty\ 4^{\alpha}\pi^{-D/2}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{D}{2}+\alpha)}{\Gamma(-\alpha)}\frac{1}{\left(x^{2}\right)^{\frac{D}{2}+\alpha}}\penalty\ .

(B.45)

We will follow the latter strategy such that we can use the Feynman diagrams appearing in the amplitude (
B.2
) in section
B.1.1
for
p_{2}=p_{4}=0
and
p_{1}=p_{3}=p
(this amputates two external propagators) and we Fourier transform them.
For now, we set
y=0
, knowing that we can reinstate it at a non-zero value by shifting
x
in the Fourier transformed diagrams.
We find the following expressions for the diagrams in dimensional regularization (
D=4-2\varepsilon
):

•

Tree level: the lowest-order contribution is the Fourier transform of the first diagram in (
B.15
) and we use the cartoon
\mathrm{O}
as an abbreviation for the two-point coordinate-space diagram.
The tree level contribution is

A_{\mathrm{tree}}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}}}{\pi}\right)^{2\varepsilon}\mathrm{O}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4-2\varepsilon}}\frac{\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)^{2}}{\left(x^{2}\right)^{2-2\varepsilon}}\penalty\ .

(B.46)

Since it is finite for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
and is contained in higher-order diagrams, it will be factored out in all of following diagrams to simplify the calculation.
Note that a one-loop diagram in momentum-space is the finite tree level contribution in coordinate-space, when Fourier transforming it.
The tree level diagram (
B.46
) produces the first factor before the expansion in (
B.44
).
Here and in the following, factors like
\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}}/\pi\right)^{\left(l+2\right)\varepsilon}
ensure the absence of terms proportional to the Euler-Mascheroni constant
\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}
, which are getting captured by a redefinition of
\mu
in this way.

•

One-loop level: the only diagram contributing is
\mathrm{O}\makebox[4.0pt]{O}
, which is the cartoon for the amputated version of the first diagram in (
B.23
):

\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}}}{\pi}\right)^{3\varepsilon}\mathrm{O}\makebox[4.0pt]{O}=2\cdot(4\pi)^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}\cdot\frac{A_{\mathrm{tree}}}{8\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+L+1+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)\right]\,.

(B.47)

•

There are two diagrams contributing at two-loops:

–

Adding another bubble to the one-loop diagram gives the two loop diagram
\mathrm{O}\makebox[4.0pt]{O}\makebox[8.0pt]{O}
, related to (
B.29
) after the Fourier transform, which evaluates to

\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}}}{\pi}\right)^{4\varepsilon}\mathrm{O}\makebox[4.0pt]{O}\makebox[8.0pt]{O}=4\cdot(4\pi)^{4}\alpha_{1}^{4}\cdot\frac{A_{\mathrm{tree}}}{256\pi^{4}}\left[\frac{3}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{6L+6}{\varepsilon}+6L^{2}+12L+3\zeta_{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)\right]\,.

(B.48)

–

The first diagram containing a single-trace interaction (two vertices to be precise) is
<
O
>
, related to (
B.31
), with the regularized expression

\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}}}{\pi}\right)^{4\varepsilon}\raisebox{1.0pt}{<}\makebox[3.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O}}\makebox[7.0pt]{\raisebox{1.0pt}{>}}=1\cdot(4\pi)^{4}\xi^{4}\cdot\frac{A_{\mathrm{tree}}}{256\pi^{4}}\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{2L+3}{\varepsilon}+2L^{2}+6L+\zeta_{2}+3+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)\right]\,.

(B.49)

•

The three-loop level consist of two diagrams as well:

–

The (bubble)
4
diagram is illustrated by the cartoon
\mathrm{O}\makebox[4.0pt]{O}\makebox[8.0pt]{O}\makebox[4.0pt]{O}
and has the
\varepsilon
-expansion

\begin{split}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}}}{\pi}\right)^{5\varepsilon}\mathrm{O}\makebox[4.0pt]{O}\makebox[8.0pt]{O}\makebox[4.0pt]{O}=8\cdot(4\pi)^{6}\alpha_{1}^{6}\cdot\frac{A_{\mathrm{tree}}}{1024\pi^{6}}\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{3}}+\frac{3(L+1)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\frac{9}{2}L^{2}+9L+\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right.\\
\left.+\left(\frac{9}{2}L^{3}+\frac{27}{2}L^{2}+\frac{3}{4}L+53\zeta(3)+\frac{3}{4}-40\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)\right]\,.\end{split}

(B.50)

–

The composition of the diagram
<
O
>
with a bubble gives the second three-loop diagram with an expansion

\begin{split}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}}}{\pi}\right)^{5\varepsilon}\raisebox{1.0pt}{<}\makebox[3.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O}}\makebox[7.0pt]{\raisebox{1.0pt}{>}}\makebox[4.0pt]{O}=4\cdot(4\pi)^{6}\alpha_{1}^{2}\xi^{4}\cdot\frac{A_{\mathrm{tree}}}{1024\pi^{6}}\left[\frac{1}{3\varepsilon^{3}}+\frac{3L+4}{3\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\frac{9}{2}L^{2}+12L+\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}+4}{3\varepsilon}\right.\\
\left.+\frac{18L^{3}+72L^{2}+3\left(16+\pi^{2}\right)L+4\left(67\zeta(3)+\pi^{2}-44\right)}{12}+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)\right]\,.\end{split}

(B.51)

To obtain the perturbative three-loop expression for the two-point function (
B.44
) (terms of quartic order in the couplings and higher are out of reach at this order), one has to sum up the diagrams (
B.46
-
B.51
).
This results in a divergent expression due to the
1/\varepsilon^{3}
,
1/\varepsilon^{2}
and
1/\varepsilon
poles in the individual diagrams.
Those divergences are subject to two
Z
-factors for renormalization, the coupling renormalization of (
B.28
) and the wave-function renormalization of the operator
\mathrm{tr}\left[\phi_{1}\phi_{1}\right]
.
The latter one has to be additionally introduced, the coupling renormalization alone is not capable of capturing all divergences in the correlation function.
We determine it now by first making the ansatz

Z_{\mathcal{O}}=1+\frac{\tau_{13}+\tau_{12}+\tau_{11}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{\tau_{23}+\tau_{22}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\tau_{33}}{\varepsilon^{3}}

(B.52)

with coefficients
\tau_{nk}
of order
k
in the couplings, which have to be determined by requiring a finite, renormalized two-point function.
We illustrate this at the perturbative expression at two-loops (the three loop expression is very long),

\begin{split}\left<\mathcal{O}(x)\bar{\mathcal{O}}(y)\right>_{\mathrm{R}}=&\penalty\ A_{\mathrm{tree}}\cdot\\
\left\{1\right.+w&\left(\frac{4\alpha_{1}^{2}+\tau_{11}}{\varepsilon}+4(L+1)\alpha_{1}^{2}\right)\\
+w^{2}&\left[\frac{-\xi^{4}+4\alpha_{1}^{4}+4\alpha_{1}^{2}\tau_{11}+\tau_{22}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\xi^{4}+16(L+1)\alpha_{1}^{4}+4(L+1)\alpha_{1}^{2}\tau_{11}+\tau_{12}}{\varepsilon}\right.\\
&\left.+\left(\left(L^{2}+4L+3\right)\xi^{4}+\frac{4}{3}\left(15L^{2}+30L+\pi^{2}\right)\alpha_{1}^{4}+\frac{1}{3}\left(6L^{2}+12L+\pi^{2}\right)\alpha_{1}^{2}\tau_{11}\right)\right.\\
&\left.\left.+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(w^{3}\right)\right\}\,.\end{split}

(B.53)

Here, the counting parameter for the coupling order
w
is reinstalled.
The coupling is renormalized here, i. e. it enters the calculation multiplied by the first two coupling-orders of
Z
from (
B.28
).
The coefficients of the wave-function renormalization
Z_{\mathcal{O}}
, the
\tau
’s, can be determined by requiring the poles to vanish in (
B.53
).
When considering the analog three-loop expression, one can even determine the
\tau_{n3}
’s.
We find

\begin{array}[]{lll}\tau_{11}=-4\alpha_{1}^{2}\penalty\ ,&\tau_{12}=-\xi^{4}\penalty\ ,&\tau_{13}=-\frac{4}{3}\alpha_{1}^{2}\xi^{4}\penalty\ ,\\
&\tau_{22}=12\alpha_{1}^{4}+\xi^{4}\penalty\ ,&\tau_{23}=\frac{20}{3}\alpha_{1}^{2}\xi^{4}\penalty\ ,\\
&&\tau_{33}=-\frac{16}{3}\alpha_{1}^{2}\left(6\alpha_{1}^{4}+\xi^{4}\right)\penalty\ .\end{array}

(B.54)

This yields the three-loop wave-function renormalization of the operator
Z_{\mathcal{O}}
by (
B.52
).

As a remark, the necessity of the double-trace couplings becomes evident when requiring finiteness of the renormalized two-point function.
Without double-trace couplings, one would obtain (
B.53
) with
\alpha_{1}^{2}\rightarrow 0
.
This is

\begin{split}\left<\mathcal{O}(x)\bar{\mathcal{O}}(y)\right>_{\mathrm{R},\alpha_{1}^{2}\rightarrow 0}=A_{\mathrm{tree}}&\left\{1+w\left(\frac{\tau_{11}}{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)\right)\right.+w^{2}\left[\frac{\xi^{4}+\tau_{22}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{(2L+3)\xi^{4}+\tau_{12}}{\varepsilon}\right.\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\left.\frac{1}{6}\left(12L^{2}+36L+\pi^{2}+18\right)\xi^{4}+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)\right]\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\left.\mathcal{O}\left(w^{3}\right)\right\}\,,\end{split}

(B.55)

and requiring the poles to vanish would imply
\tau_{12}=-(2L+3)\xi^{4}
.
But this would mean that the corresponding counter-term is non-local due to the dependence on
L=\log{\left[\mu^{2}(x-y)^{2}\right]}
.

Let us get back to the conformal case where we tune the double-trace coupling to the fix-point (
B.42
).
Then the perturbative two-loop function (
B.53
) simplifies to

\left<\mathcal{O}(x)\bar{\mathcal{O}}(y)\right>_{\mathrm{R,fixed-point}}=A_{\mathrm{tree}}\left[1+2i(L+1)\xi^{2}+\left(-2L^{2}-4L+1\right)\xi^{4}+\mathcal{O}\left(\xi^{6}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)\right]\,,

(B.56)

after setting
w\rightarrow 1
(there is only the
\xi^{2}
-coupling left, so the counting parameter
w
is superfluous). Comparing (
B.56
) and (
B.44
) yields the coefficients
v_{0}
,
v_{1}
,
v_{3}
and
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
.
However, the calculation can be extend to three loops (it is not shown for brevity).
It yields even more coefficients and the anomalous dimension and the normalization of the two-point function at three loops are

\displaystyle\gamma(\xi^{2})

\displaystyle=-2\mathrm{i}\xi^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot 0\xi^{4}+\frac{1}{6}\cdot 6\mathrm{i}\xi^{6}+O(\xi^{8})=-2\mathrm{i}\xi^{2}+\mathrm{i}\xi^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{8})

(B.57)

\displaystyle\begin{split}v(\xi^{2})&=1+2\mathrm{i}\xi^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2\xi^{4}+\frac{1}{6}\cdot 2\mathrm{i}\left(-75+40\pi^{2}-308\zeta_{3}\right)\xi^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{8})\\
&=1+2\mathrm{i}\xi^{2}+\xi^{4}+\mathrm{i}\left(-25+80\zeta_{2}-\frac{308}{3}\zeta_{3}\right)\xi^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{8}).\end{split}

(B.58)

The anomalous dimension coincides with the lowest orders of the expansion of
-\sqrt{2-2\sqrt{1+4\xi^{4}}}
, which is the exact result obtained by the eigenvalue of the graph-building operator in (
5.50
).
The normalization starts involving values of the zeta function
\zeta_{i}
at the three-loop order.

Chapter C
Solving inversion relations

In this appendix we present a procedure for determining a solution of the inversion relations
[
35
]
, which is applied to (
5.93
) and furnishes the solution (
5.94
).
Furthermore, we give details on the special functions necessary for the computation of the free energy of the eight-vertex model, which is presented in section
2.1
.

C.1
General case

We call the function we are looking for
S(\alpha)
, since the method of solving inversion relations was pioneered for S-matrices in Sine-Gordon QFTs
[
144
]
.
The procedure was applied in
[
30
]
to many other integrable edge-interaction models.

Let us consider the relations in a fractional form
1
1

1

The fractional form is equivalent to the multiplicative one in (
5.93
).
It can be shown by first multiplying (
C.1b
) at
\alpha
with itself at
\alpha\rightarrow-\alpha
. Then one uses the unitarity relation (
C.1a
) for the denominator, which reduces it to one.
Finally, we re-parameterize
\beta:=\eta-\alpha
and find (
5.93
) after choosing
f_{0}(\beta)=\frac{\pi^{\beta}}{\Gamma(\beta+\ell)}
with
\eta=\frac{D}{2}
.
with the function
S(\alpha)
, which we would like to determine, with a crossing parameter
\eta
and a seed function
f_{0}(\alpha)
,

\displaystyle S(\alpha)S(-\alpha)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\mathrm{(Unitarity)}

(C.1a)

\displaystyle\frac{S(\eta-\alpha)}{S(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{0}(\eta-\alpha)}{f_{0}(\alpha)}

\displaystyle\mathrm{(Crossing)}\penalty\ .

(C.1b)

We will construct the solution of (
C.1
) in an iterative fashion
[
166
,
165
,
30
]
by first making an ansatz for one of the two equations in (
C.1
) and restricting it via the other, but again only up to some factor. This factor will be partially fixed by plugging the ansatz back in the first equation. Repeating this procedure infinitely many times yields the solution.
Concretely, it looks like this:

1.

Start with an ansatz for the crossing equation (
C.1b
).
There are two obvious choices,
S(\alpha)=f_{0}(\alpha)
or
S(\alpha)=\frac{1}{f_{0}(\eta-\alpha)}
.
we choose the latter, which we call the
crossing-choice
.
It consists of constructing the ansatz for the crossing equation always in such a way that the sought functions (here
S(\alpha)
, later
f_{2n}(\alpha)
) make up for the remaining part of the crossing equation with a minus sign in front of
\alpha
in the argument.
After this choice, one multiplies by a function
f_{1}
to make up for the remaining freedom in (
C.1b
).
It has to satisfy a crossing equation on its own and the first step in the iteration looks like

S(\alpha)=\frac{1}{f_{0}(\eta-\alpha)}f_{1}(\alpha)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{f_{1}(\eta-\alpha)}{f_{1}(\alpha)}=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{(Crossing}\penalty\ f_{1}\mathrm{)}\penalty\ .

(C.2)

Generally a step of the iteration consists of taking the ansatz from the last step, require it to satisfy a crossing/unitarity relation by making a choice which factor to compensate, multiplying by a new function, and observing that it has to satisfy a crossing/unitarity relation.
This relation will then be used in the second-to-next step.

2.

Now we use the unitarity relation (
C.1a
) by plugging in
S(\alpha)
as obtained in (
C.2
).
We obtain

\frac{f_{1}(\alpha)f_{1}(-\alpha)}{f_{0}(\eta-\alpha)f_{0}(\eta+\alpha)}=1\penalty\ .

(C.3)

For
f_{1}
to be a solution, we make an ansatz by the so-called
unitarity-choice
.
It refers to the rule that ansätze for solutions of unitarity equations,
f_{2n-1}
, always make up for that part of the equation, which carries a
+\alpha
in the argument.
This yields

f_{1}(\alpha)=f_{0}(\eta+\alpha)f_{2}(\alpha)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ f_{2}(\alpha)f_{2}(-\alpha)=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{(Unitarity}\penalty\ f_{2}\mathrm{)}\penalty\ .

(C.4)

Again, one has the freedom to multiply a function
f_{2}(\alpha)
, which however has to satisfy a unitarity relation.

3.

Next we take the crossing equation for
f_{1}
in (
C.2
) and plug in the new expression for
f_{1}
from (
C.4
).
This yields

\frac{f_{0}(2\eta-\alpha)f_{2}(\eta-\alpha)}{f_{0}(\eta+\alpha)f_{2}(\alpha)}=1\penalty\ .

(C.5)

Making the crossing-choice means constructing
f_{2}
such that it captures the part with the
-\alpha
in the argument, i. e.
f_{0}(2\eta-\alpha)
.
Yet, one still has the freedom to multiply
f_{0}(2\eta-\alpha)
with another function
f_{3}
, satisfying a crossing equation on its own.
Thus we set

f_{2}(\alpha)=f_{0}(2\eta-\alpha)f_{3}(\alpha)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{f_{3}(\eta-\alpha)}{f_{3}(\alpha)}=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{(Crossing}\penalty\ f_{3}\mathrm{)}\penalty\ .

(C.6)

4.

This step is again a unitarity-step, similar to the second one.
Now take the unitarity relation for
f_{2}
in (
C.4
) and plug in the ansatz for
f_{2}
from (
C.6
).
One finds

f_{0}(2\eta-\alpha)f_{0}(2\eta+\alpha)f_{3}(\alpha)f_{3}(-\alpha)=1\penalty\ .

(C.7)

Making the unitarity-choice refers to compensating
f_{0}(2\eta+\alpha)
by
f_{3}(\alpha)
.
Still, there is the freedom for another factor
f_{4}
, which again satisfies a unitarity relation, and we obtain

f_{3}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{f_{0}(2\eta+\alpha)}f_{4}(\alpha)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ f_{4}(\alpha)f_{4}(-\alpha)=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{(Unitarity}\penalty\ f_{4}\mathrm{)}\penalty\ .

(C.8)

5.

The way the steps continue hopefully became apparent by now, but we show yet another step, namely crossing step.
Take the crossing equation for
f_{3}
in (
C.6
) and plug it in the ansatz from the previous step, which gives

\frac{f_{0}(2\eta+\alpha)f_{4}(\eta-\alpha)}{f_{0}(3\eta-\alpha)f_{4}(\alpha)}=1\penalty\ .

(C.9)

The crossing-choice dictates that
f_{4}(\alpha)
compensates
f_{0}(3\eta-\alpha)
and another factor
f_{5}
can be multiplied if it satisfies a crossing equation itself, therefore it yields

f_{4}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{f_{0}(3\eta-\alpha)}f_{5}(\alpha)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{f_{5}(\eta-\alpha)}{f_{5}(\alpha)}=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{(Crossing}\penalty\ f_{5}\mathrm{)}\penalty\ .

(C.10)

After doing some more steps, one quickly realizes that the pattern continues and the
n
-th step is very similar to the
(n-4)
-th step, only with two more
\eta
’s in the argument of
f_{0}
.
Explicitly, those five steps show that the solution, up to now, is

S(\alpha)=\frac{1}{f_{0}(\eta-\alpha)}f_{0}(\eta+\alpha)f_{0}(2\eta-\alpha)\frac{1}{f_{0}(2\eta+\alpha)}\frac{1}{f_{0}(3\eta-\alpha)}\cdot f_{5}(\alpha)\penalty\ .

(C.11)

After infinitely many steps, we can observe that the procedure yields an infinite product, namely

S(\alpha)=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{f_{0}\left((2k-1)\cdot\eta+\alpha\right)f_{0}(2k\cdot\eta-\alpha)}{f_{0}\left((2k-1)\cdot\eta-\alpha\right)f_{0}(2k\cdot\eta+\alpha)}\penalty\ .

(C.12)

In a further step, the factors may be regrouped to give another representation,

S(\alpha)=\frac{1}{f_{0}(\eta-\alpha)}\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{f_{0}\left(2k\cdot\eta-\eta+\alpha\right)f_{0}(2k\cdot\eta-\alpha)}{f_{0}\left(2k\cdot\eta+\eta-\alpha\right)f_{0}(2k\cdot\eta+\alpha)}\penalty\ .

(C.13)

Finally, using the telescope product
\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{f_{0}((2k+1)\eta)}{f_{0}((2k-1)\eta)}=\frac{1}{f_{0}(\eta)}
, we obtain

S(\alpha)=\frac{f_{0}(\eta)}{f_{0}(\eta-\alpha)}\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{f_{0}\left(2\eta k-\eta+\alpha\right)f_{0}(2\eta k-\alpha)f_{0}(2\eta k+\eta)}{f_{0}\left(2\eta k+\eta-\alpha\right)f_{0}(2\eta k+\alpha)f_{0}(2\eta k-\eta)}\penalty\ .

(C.14)

We comment on the role of the choices (crossing- and unitarity-choice).
In this derivation, we picked the
+\alpha
arguments in the unitarity relations and
-\alpha
arguments in the crossing ones.
Exchanging this assignments would have given a similar expression (
C.12
) but with the arguments of
f_{0}
multiplied with a minus sign.
The latter choices would yield poles in the physical strip
\left[0,\eta\right)
, which corresponds to a solution not referring to the maximal eigenvalue.
Picking for both, unitarity and crossing, the
+\alpha
arguments or both times the
-\alpha
would not expand the product and only yield the relations (
C.1
), and hence just a trivial statement.
The result (
C.14
) is in accordance with
[
30
]
.

In conclusion, we will apply the general solution (
C.14
) to our set of inversion relations (
5.93
).
By the transition to the fractional form (
C.1
), as described in footnote
1
, we identify the seed function as

f_{0}(u)\penalty\ =\penalty\ \frac{\pi^{u}}{\Gamma(u+\ell)}

(C.15)

by comparing (
5.93
) with the general relations (
C.1
). Then by (
C.14
) we find the solution

\kappa_{\ell}(u)=\pi^{u}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{D}{2}-u+\ell\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{D}{2}+\ell\right)}\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma\left(Dk+\frac{D}{2}-u+\ell\right)\Gamma\left(Dk+u+\ell\right)\Gamma\left(Dk-\frac{D}{2}+\ell\right)}{\Gamma\left(Dk-\frac{D}{2}+u+\ell\right)\Gamma\left(Dk-u+\ell\right)\Gamma\left(Dk+\frac{D}{2}+\ell\right)}

(C.16)

where we used the QFT crossing parameter
\eta=\frac{D}{2}
.
This is the solution presented in (
5.94
).

C.2
Theta functions, elliptic gamma functions and the eight-vertex model

We use the notation of
[
206
]
for the theta functions.
They read

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(z|q)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}q^{(n+\frac{1}{2})^{2}}\mathrm{sin}[(2n+1)z]=2\,G\,q^{\frac{1}{4}}\,\mathrm{sin}(z)\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1-q^{2n}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}z}\right)\left(1-q^{2n}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z}\right)\penalty\ ,

(C.17a)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(z|q)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}q^{(n+\frac{1}{2})^{2}}\mathrm{cos}[(2n+1)z]=2\,G\,q^{\frac{1}{4}}\,\mathrm{cos}(z)\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1+q^{2n}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}z}\right)\left(1+q^{2n}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z}\right)\penalty\ ,

(C.17b)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(z|q)

\displaystyle=1+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}q^{n^{2}}\mathrm{cos}(2nz)=G\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1+q^{2n-1}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}z}\right)\left(1+q^{2n-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z}\right)\penalty\ ,

(C.17c)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(z|q)

\displaystyle=1+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}q^{n^{2}}\mathrm{cos}(2nz)=G\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1-q^{2n-1}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}z}\right)\left(1-q^{2n-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z}\right)

(C.17d)

with
G:=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{2n})
and the elliptic nome
q
.
They have a representation as an infinite product.
When one faces multiple infinite products, the elliptic gamma functions are elegant functions to describe their properties.
Using the convention by Rains
[
132
]
, we have the elliptic gamma function of
r
-th order

\Gamma^{(r)}(z|q_{1},...,q_{r})=\prod_{n_{1},...,n_{r}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1-q_{1}^{n_{1}+1}\cdots q_{r}^{n_{r}+1}z^{-1}}{\left(1-q_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots q_{r}^{n_{r}}z\right)^{(-1)^{r}}}=\mathrm{exp}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{r}z^{k}-q_{1}^{k}\cdots q_{r}^{k}z^{-k}}{k\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-p_{i}^{k})}\right]

(C.18)

and the equality between the first and second representation can be shown by taking the logarithm of the former, and expanding the logarithm of the nominator and denominator.
Then the
r
sums can be performed with the help of the geometric series.
We are particularly interested in the first- and second-order elliptic gamma functions and they satisfy the helpful relations

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}(q\cdot z|q)=\Gamma^{(1)}(z^{-1}|q)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}(z|q)=\Gamma^{(1)}(z|q^{2})\Gamma^{(1)}(q\cdot z|q^{2})\penalty\ ,

(C.19a)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(2)}(p\,q\cdot z|q,p)=\frac{1}{\Gamma^{(2)}(z^{-1}|q,p)}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(2)}(p\cdot z|q,p)=\Gamma^{(1)}(z|q)\Gamma^{(2)}(z|q,p)\penalty\ .

(C.19b)

Next, we derive the eight-vertex model’s free energy from the generic solution of inversion relations (
C.14
).
The inversion relations for the eight-vertex model (
2.21
) may be brought into the fractional form (
C.1
), if we redefine
\kappa(u)=\mathrm{w}(u)\tilde{\kappa}(u)
.
We find the seed function to be
f_{0}(u)=\mathrm{w}(u)^{-1}
and, with the help of the general solution (
C.14
), the free energy of the eight-vertex model is found as

\kappa(u)=\mathrm{w}(u)\frac{\mathrm{w}(\eta-u)}{\mathrm{w}(\eta)}\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{w}(2\eta k+\eta-u)\mathrm{w}(2\eta k+u)\mathrm{w}(2\eta k-\eta)}{\mathrm{w}(2\eta k-\eta+u)\mathrm{w}(2\eta k-u)\mathrm{w}(2\eta k+\eta)}\penalty\ .

(C.20)

The function
\mathrm{w}(u)
is given in (
2.14
) as
\mathrm{w}(u)=-\mathrm{i}\rho\cdot\Theta(0)\Theta(\mathrm{i}(\eta+u))H(\mathrm{i}(\eta+u))
.
It is a lengthy calculation to plug this into (
C.20
) and express it in terms of
\Gamma^{(1)}
and
\Gamma^{(2)}
.
It is convenient to consider
\mathrm{log}\,\kappa(u)
and to use the product representation of the functions
H(u)
and
\Theta(u)
by the theta functions (
C.17
).
One of the two infinite products from the definition of the theta functions and the solution (
C.20
), which turn into sums after taking the logarithm, can be eliminated by the geometric series.
The remaining one is used to identify the elliptic gamma functions in the second representation of (
C.18
).
Eventually, using (
C.19
) and choosing
\rho=\Gamma^{(1)}(q|q^{2})^{2}
gives the form presented in (
2.22
).

Chapter D
Superspace notations

D.1
Four-dimensional
\mathbf{\mathcal{N}=1}
superspace

D.1.1
Spinor algebra

The notation follows
[
151
]
and for the sake of completeness we list important relations here as well.
The Pauli matrices are defined as

\begin{aligned} \sigma^{0}&=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ ,\\
\sigma^{1}&=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \sigma^{2}&=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&0\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ ,\\
\sigma^{3}&=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ .\end{aligned}

(D.1)

Small Greek indices of anti-commuting spinors are raised and lowered depending on their chirality,

\begin{aligned} \psi^{\alpha}=\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\psi_{\beta}\penalty\ ,\\
\psi_{\alpha}=\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}\psi^{\beta}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \bar{\psi}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\varepsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\bar{\psi}_{\dot{\beta}}\penalty\ ,\\
\bar{\psi}_{\dot{\alpha}}=\varepsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\bar{\psi}^{\dot{\beta}}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}

(D.2)

with the epsilon tensor squaring to identity as
\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\varepsilon_{\beta\gamma}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\gamma}
.
Its non-zero components are
\varepsilon_{21}=\varepsilon^{12}=1
and
\varepsilon_{12}=\varepsilon^{21}=-1
.

Spinor bilinears are

\begin{aligned} \psi\chi&=\left(\psi\chi\right)=\psi^{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}=-\psi_{\alpha}\chi^{\alpha}\penalty\ ,\\
\bar{\psi}\bar{\chi}&=\left(\bar{\psi}\bar{\chi}\right)=\bar{\psi}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\chi}^{\dot{\alpha}}=-\bar{\psi}^{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\chi}_{\dot{\alpha}}=\left(\psi\chi\right)^{\dagger}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \psi\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\chi}=\psi^{\alpha}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\chi}^{\dot{\alpha}}\penalty\ ,\\
\left(\psi\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\chi}\right)^{\dagger}=\chi\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\psi}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}

(D.3)

and we use the bracket notation
\left(\psi\chi\right)=\left(\chi\psi\right)
if the index contraction is unclear.
Denoting
\bar{\sigma}^{\mu,\dot{\alpha}\alpha}=\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\sigma^{\mu}_{\beta\dot{\beta}}\varepsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}
, one finds the relations

\begin{aligned} \sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}^{\dot{\beta}\beta}=-2\,\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}\delta_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\dot{\beta}}\penalty\ ,\\
\mathrm{tr}\left[\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu}\right]=-2\,\eta^{\mu\nu}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \left[\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu}+\sigma^{\nu}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\right]_{\alpha}^{\penalty\ \beta}=-2\,\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}\penalty\ ,\\
\left[\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\sigma^{\nu}+\bar{\sigma}^{\nu}\sigma^{\mu}\right]^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\penalty\ \dot{\beta}}=-2\,\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\dot{\beta}}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}

(D.4)

with the Lorentzian metric
\eta^{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,1,1)
.

For coinciding spinors and in particular for spinorial Graßmann numbers we further denote
\theta^{2}:=\left(\theta\theta\right)
and
\bar{\theta}^{2}:=\left(\bar{\theta}\bar{\theta}\right)
and we have the helpful relations

\begin{aligned} \theta^{\alpha}\theta^{\beta}&=-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\theta^{2}\penalty\ ,\\
\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\beta}}&=\phantom{-}\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\bar{\theta}^{2}\penalty\ .\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\;\theta\sigma^{\nu}\bar{\theta}=-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}

(D.5)

The covariant super derivatives and supersymmetry generators are given by

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}+\mathrm{i}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}\penalty\ ,\hskip 28.45274pt\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}=-\bar{\partial}_{\dot{\alpha}}-\mathrm{i}\theta^{\alpha}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}\penalty\ ,

(D.6a)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}\penalty\ ,\hskip 28.45274pt\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}=-\bar{\partial}_{\dot{\alpha}}+\mathrm{i}\theta^{\alpha}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}\penalty\ ,

(D.6b)

with
\partial_{\alpha}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{\alpha}}
,
\bar{\partial}_{\dot{\alpha}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}}
and
\partial_{\mu}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}
.

D.1.2
Berezin integral

Integration over the fermionic part of superspace picks out the quadratic component in the Graßmann spinors, such that

\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\;\theta^{2}=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \int\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\bar{\theta}^{2}=1\penalty\ .

(D.7)

Furthermore, squares of the Graßmann spinors, appearing in a Berezinian alongside other functions of the integration Graßmann variable, act as delta distributions on fermionic superspace, which we denote by
\delta^{\left(2\right)}\left(\theta_{12}\right)=\theta_{12}^{2}
and
\delta^{\left(2\right)}\left(\bar{\theta}_{12}\right)=\bar{\theta}_{12}^{2}
with
\theta_{12}=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}
and
\bar{\theta}_{12}=\bar{\theta}_{1}-\bar{\theta}_{2}
.
Concerning the mass dimension, Graßmann bilinears have the same values assigned as bosonic coordinates, i. e.
\left[\theta^{\alpha}\right]=\left[\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\right]=\frac{\left[x^{\mu}\right]}{2}=-\frac{1}{2}
.
This implies for the measure of the Berezin integral the mass dimensions
\left[\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\right]=\left[\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\right]=1
.

Note that integration and derivation for Graßmann numbers is equivalent, or in formulas

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\;f(\theta)=\left[-\frac{1}{4}D^{2}f(\theta)\right]_{\theta=0}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;f(\bar{\theta})=\left[-\frac{1}{4}\bar{D}^{2}f(\bar{\theta})\right]_{\bar{\theta}=0}\penalty\ ,

(D.8a)

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\theta}\;f(\theta,\bar{\theta})

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{16}D^{2}\bar{D}^{2}f(\theta,\bar{\theta})\right]_{\theta,\bar{\theta}=0}\penalty\ .

(D.8b)

D.2
Three-dimensional
\mathbf{\mathcal{N}=2}
superspace

We follow the notation of
[
170
]
for the spinor conventions.
The antisymmetric
\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}
has the components
\varepsilon^{12}=-\varepsilon_{12}=1
, such that
\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\varepsilon_{\beta\gamma}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\gamma}
.
The three-dimensional gamma matrices are
\gamma^{\mu\;\beta}_{\penalty\ \alpha}=(\mathrm{i}\sigma^{2},\sigma^{1},\sigma^{3})
and they satisfy the Clifford algebra
\gamma^{\mu\;\beta}_{\penalty\ \alpha}\gamma^{\nu\;\gamma}_{\penalty\ \beta}=\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta^{\gamma}_{\alpha}+\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\gamma^{\penalty\ \penalty\ \gamma}_{\rho\,\alpha}
with the Lorentzian spacetime metric
\eta^{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,1)
.
Lowering an index gives
\gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\mu}=\varepsilon_{\beta\delta}\gamma^{\mu\;\delta}_{\penalty\ \alpha}=(-\mathbb{1},-\sigma^{3},\sigma^{1})
, which is symmetric in the spinor indices
\gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\mu}=\gamma_{\beta\alpha}^{\mu}
.
Furthermore, the trace
\gamma^{\mu\;\alpha}_{\penalty\ \alpha}=0
vanishes.

Spinor indices of anti-commuting fermions and Graßmann numbers are raised and lowered with the antisymmetric epsilon tensor according to

\begin{aligned} \psi^{\alpha}=\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\psi_{\beta}\penalty\ ,\\
\psi_{\alpha}=\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}\psi^{\beta}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \bar{\psi}^{\alpha}=\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\bar{\psi}_{\beta}\penalty\ ,\\
\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}=\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}\bar{\psi}^{\beta}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}

(D.9)

and the spinor bilinears are

\begin{aligned} \psi\chi&=\psi^{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}=-\psi_{\alpha}\chi^{\alpha}\penalty\ ,\\
\bar{\psi}\bar{\chi}&=\bar{\psi}^{\alpha}\bar{\chi}_{\alpha}=-\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}\bar{\chi}^{\alpha}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \psi\gamma^{\mu}\bar{\chi}=\psi^{\alpha}\gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\bar{\chi}^{\beta}\penalty\ .\end{aligned}

(D.10)

We stress the difference of the conventions here to the four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
case (
D.3
).
Consequently, the squares of spinorial Graßmann numbers, for example, are denoted by
\theta^{2}:=\theta^{\alpha}\theta_{\alpha}
and
\bar{\theta}^{2}:=\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}
and we have the frequently used relations

\begin{aligned} \theta^{\alpha}\theta^{\beta}&=-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\theta^{2}\penalty\ ,\\
\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}^{\beta}&=-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\bar{\theta}^{2}\penalty\ .\end{aligned}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{aligned} \theta\gamma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\;\theta\gamma^{\nu}\bar{\theta}=\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}

(D.11)

which differ again in some signs to the
D=4

\mathcal{N}=1
conventions (
D.5
).
Other relations, like the more general Fierz identities, can be found in the appendix of
[
170
]
.
The covariant super derivatives and supersymmetry generators read

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}+\mathrm{i}\,\gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\bar{\theta}^{\beta}\partial_{\mu}\penalty\ ,\hskip 28.45274pt\bar{D}_{\alpha}=-\bar{\partial}_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\,\theta^{\beta}\gamma^{\mu}_{\beta\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\penalty\ ,

(D.12a)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\,\gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\bar{\theta}^{\beta}\partial_{\mu}\penalty\ ,\hskip 28.45274pt\bar{Q}_{\alpha}=-\bar{\partial}_{\alpha}+\mathrm{i}\,\theta^{\beta}\gamma^{\mu}_{\beta\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\penalty\ .

(D.12b)

Since the three-dimensional gamma matrices are real, the supercharges
Q_{\alpha}
and
\bar{Q}_{\alpha}
are counted individually and the group element corresponding to the supercharges generate the
\mathcal{N}=2
fermionic subspace.
It is described by the coordinates
\theta^{\alpha}
and
\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}
.
In contrast, the
\mathcal{N}=1
four-dimensional supercharges (
D.6b
) combine to a single complex supercharge and the coordinates are
\theta^{\alpha}
and
\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}
as well.
This explains why we only have to adjust the bosonic coordinate within
z=(x,\theta,\bar{\theta})
and drop the dot in the spinor indices, when performing calculations in three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
superspace instead of four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
superspace
[
207
,
208
]
.

The three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
chiral superfields have the expansions

\displaystyle\Phi(z)

\displaystyle=\phi(x_{+})+\sqrt{2}\;\theta\psi(x_{+})+\theta^{2}F(x_{+})\penalty\ ,

(D.13a)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\dagger}(z)

\displaystyle=\phi^{\dagger}(x_{-})-\sqrt{2}\;\bar{\theta}\bar{\psi}(x_{-})-\bar{\theta}^{2}F^{\dagger}(x_{-})\penalty\ ,

(D.13b)

with
x^{\mu}_{\pm}=x^{\mu}\pm\mathrm{i}\theta\gamma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}
.
Berezin integration of the Graßmann spinors works analogously to the four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
case, which is presented in section
D.1.2
.
